<summary>This is the title page of the 1552 edition of Leone Ebreo's "Dialogues of Love," a seminal work of Renaissance Neoplatonism. It includes a handwritten provenance note indicating it once belonged to the library of a scholar named Lothar Schneit.</summary>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page serves as the printer’s or editor's preface to a standalone edition of the third dialogue from Leone Ebreo’s "Dialoghi d'Amore." It establishes the editorial intent to make this specific section on the "universality of love" more accessible to a wider audience.</meta>

# TO THE DISCERNING
## Readers.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'P' containing an illustration of a rural landscape with several buildings, trees, and a figure walking on a path in the foreground.</image-desc>

*IT WILL PERHAPS SEEM a new thing, and altogether out of place, to see the Dialogue on the universality of love by Leone Ebreo <note>Leone Ebreo, also known as Judah Leon Abravanel (c. 1460–1535), was a Jewish physician, poet, and philosopher whose work on Neoplatonic love was immensely influential in the Renaissance.</note>—a man who, as gathered from his writings, possessed the greatest genius, was most learned, and highly practiced in Philosophy—published on its own, and without the usual company of the other Dialogues. Peradventure, we shall not receive the praise that our good intentions deserve and the grace of wise Readers, but rather their blame and displeasure, as if we had spoiled and maimed such a beautiful body by severing from it the most beautiful and best member it contained. This thing could undoubtedly be attributed to us as a failing if, by removing this part, the rest of the work remained lacking and imperfect; or if this Dialogue, separated from the others, lost that brilliance which the entire work together receives from the profoundest wisdom of the Author, who introduced Wisdom itself to speak there under the name of Sophia <note>original: "Sophia" (from the Greek word for wisdom).</note>. But since neither do those other parts (with this one removed) lose their accustomed beauty, nor does this one, separated from them, appear any less learned or pleasing, it did not seem inappropriate to us—since it treats the universality of Love—to publish it again alone, emended and most diligently corrected for the common benefit of everyone. We were led to this especially by the reasons written below. First, because we were earnestly requested to do so by virtuous and rare persons, whom for many respects we neither can nor wish to fail. Secondly, so that—since this dialogue is capable on its own of leading the human intellect to that happiness <note>The author uses the term "felicità" (felicity/happiness) in the sense of intellectual or spiritual fulfillment.</note> which is possible to achieve on earth—it might be impressed upon virtuous minds with more ease than it would in the company of the others, leaving them full of its health-giving fruits. Lastly, being taught by that common love which the work itself teaches us, we wished that—since it costs much less on its own than the entire work—a greater number of spirits who love the good sciences might be able to participate in it. Receive it then...*

<summary>In this preface, the editor justifies publishing the third dialogue of Leone Ebreo's "Dialoghi d'Amore" as a standalone volume, arguing that its brilliance remains intact even when separated from the full work and that its lower cost increases accessibility for students of philosophy.</summary>
<keywords>Leone Ebreo, Dialogues of Love, Sophia, Wisdom, Philosophy, Renaissance publishing, Neoplatonism, accessibility of knowledge</keywords>

-> you that there is more contentment and more utility to be <-
-> gained from it than from a thousand other tall tales <note>original: "cantafauole" – a dismissive term for frivolous, nonsensical, or poorly written stories.</note> that emerge <-
-> every day; and without doubt the paper that <-
-> they soil is worth much more than any substance <note>original: "construtto" – meaning the useful structure or logical sense of the writing.</note> <-

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page marks the beginning of the first dialogue between the characters Sophia and Philone. It follows the editor's preface and introduces the central theme of the work: a philosophical investigation into the nature of love.</meta>

<sig>A iii</sig>
<meta>catchword: speak=</meta>

# SOPHIA AND PHILONE ON THE
# UNIVERSALITY OF LOVE.

## SOPHIA.

GOD SAVE YOU, Philone; *do you pass by like this without speaking?* PH. *The friend of my health <note>The Italian "salute" means both "health" and "salvation." Philone is playing on Sophia being the source of both his well-being and his greeting.</note> greets me! God save you too, Sophia; what do you want from me?* SO. *I would like you to remember the debt you owe me, and now seems a convenient time to pay it, if it pleases you.* PH. *I, in debt to you? For what? It certainly is not a debt of benefit or goodwill, for toward me you have been generous only with pain.* SO. *I grant you it is not a debt of gratitude, but a debt of promise—which, while not as noble, is nonetheless more binding.* PH. *I do not recall promising you anything except to love you and to endure your disdain until Charon <note>In Greek mythology, the ferryman who carries souls across the river Styx to the underworld.</note> carries me across the river of oblivion <note>The river Lethe, whose waters caused the dead to forget their earthly lives.</note>; and furthermore, if the soul finds itself with any feeling there, it shall never be stripped of affection and martyrdom. Of this promise I need no reminder, for I pay it daily.* SO. *You are forgetful, Philone, or you pretend to be. Yet the debtor should remember the debt no less than the creditor. Do you not remember that a few days ago, at the end of our discussion on love and desire, you promised to tell me fully about the origin and genealogy of love? How have you forgotten it so quickly?* PH. *Oh, oh, I remember! Do not wonder, Sophia, that since you have usurped my memory, I cannot remember such things.* SO. *Even if I have usurped it, I take it away from foreign things, but not from my own.* PH. *My soul remembers only those things of yours which fill it with love and pain; these other matters, though they are yours, are foreign to my suffering.* SO. *Let it be as you wish; I forgive your forgetfulness, but I do not release you from your promise. And since we have convenient time, let us sit under this shade, and tell me of the birth of love, and what was its first origin.* PH. *If you wish us to speak of the birth of love, in this present conversation <note>original: "confabulatione," a formal term for a dialogue or scholarly discussion.</note> I must first tell you of the universality <note>original: "comunita," referring to the idea that love is a force common to all things in the universe, from rocks to angels.</note> of its being, and of its wide-reaching universality; and another time then speak=*

<summary>Sophia stops Philone to demand he fulfill a promise from their previous conversation: to explain the origins and "genealogy" of love. Philone agrees to do so but insists they must first discuss how love is a universal force present throughout all existence.</summary>
<keywords>Sophia, Philone, debt of love, Charon, river of oblivion, genealogy of love, universality of love, Neoplatonic dialogue</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous one. Philone’s sentence, interrupted at "cannot remembe-," is completed here with "-ber its origin." The dialogue moves from the specific promise made by Philone to a broader philosophical inquiry into whether love is a universal force or something unique to certain beings.</meta>

<sig>A</sig>

*-ber its origin.* <note>The first word "remo" completes "remember" from the previous page.</note> *SO. Is not the origin of a thing prior to its universality? PH. It is indeed prior in existence, but it is not prior in our* <term>cognition</term> <note>From the Italian "cognitione," referring to the process of human understanding or perception.</note>. *SO. How so? PH. Because the commonality of love is more manifest to us than its origin, and from known things one arrives at the knowledge of the unknown. SO. You speak the truth; the universality of love is quite manifest, because almost no human is stripped of it, neither male nor female, neither old nor young; and even infants, in their first awareness, possess love for their mothers and their nurses. PH. Do you then think love is no more common than the human race? SO. Furthermore, love is found in all* <term>irrational animals</term> <note>A standard Renaissance term for animals, which were believed to lack "reason" (the rational soul) compared to humans.</note> *that procreate, between females and males, and between children and parents. PH. Not only is procreation the cause of the love found in humans and other animals, but there are many other causes; nevertheless, love is not only in these, but rather its commonality extends to many more things in the world. SO. Tell me first what other causes are found in living beings, and afterwards you shall tell me how love can also be found in things that are neither animate nor reproductive. PH. I will tell you first one and then the other. Animals, besides naturally loving things that are suitable in order to pursue them—just as they hate unsuitable things in order to flee them—also love one another reciprocally for five causes. First, for the desire and the delight of common* <term>generation</term> <note>Procreation or reproduction.</note>, *males with females. Second, for generative succession, such as fathers and mothers with their children. Third, for benefit, which not only generates love in the recipient toward the giver, but no less causes it in the giver toward the recipient, even if they were of different species; as is seen when a dog or a goat nurses a child, they have a very great love for one another, and likewise if some other animal of a foreign species is nursed. Fourth, for the likeness of the same species, or another very similar one; for you will see individuals of every species of non-predatory animals engage in society because of the love they have for one another. Even predatory animals, although they do not go in company so as to enjoy the whole hunt alone, nonetheless have respect and love for those of their own species, so as not to use their natural and cruel ferocity or venomousness against them. And furthermore, among different species of animals, some friendly similarity is found, such as the dolphin with man; just as others are found who naturally hate each other, such as the* <term>basilisk</term> <note>A mythical reptile said to be the king of serpents, capable of killing with a single glance.</note> *and man, who kill each other with sight alone.*

<vocab>Love, Cognition, Generation, Animals, Basilisk, Dolphin, Species</vocab>

<summary>Philone explains that while the origin of love is philosophically prior, its universal presence is easier for humans to observe first. He outlines five natural causes for reciprocal love among animals, including reproduction, kinship, gratitude for nursing, species similarity, and natural affinities.</summary>
<keywords>universality of love, human nature, animal behavior, cross-species nursing, predatory vs social animals, the basilisk, Philone, Sophia</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues Philone’s list of the causes of love. He completes the fifth cause for animals (companionship) before transitioning to a comparative analysis of how these same five causes operate in human beings, specifically noting the role of reason.</meta>

<sig>A iiij</sig>

Fifthly, because of **constant companionship**, which makes not only animals of the same species friends, but even those of different species and of a hostile nature; as one sees a dog with a lion, and a lamb with a wolf, become friends through companionship. <note>Italian: "società." In this context, it refers to the act of living together or habitual association rather than a formal society.</note>

SO. I have understood the causes of love among animals; tell me now those of the love among humans. 

PH. The causes of reciprocal love among humans are these same five as for animals, but the use of **reason** makes them more intense or more moderate, directly or indirectly, according to the diversity of human ends.

SO. Explain these differences to me in each of these five causes. 

PH. The first—stemming from desire and the pleasure found in <term>procreation</term> <note>Italian: "generatione," referring to the act of conceiving and producing offspring.</note>—is in humans a cause of more intense, firm, and exclusive love than in animals, and it is usually more concealed by reason. 

SO. Explain these differences more particularly. 

PH. It is more intense in humans because they love women with greater vehemence and seek them with greater solicitude—so much so that for them they neglect eating and sleeping and set aside all rest. And it is firmer in them because the love between man and woman is preserved longer, so that neither satiety, nor absence, nor any obstacle can dissolve it. It is more <term>exclusive</term> <note>Italian: "propio." Philone is arguing that human pair-bonding is more specific and legally/socially defined than animal mating.</note> because every man has more of a proprietary claim to a specific woman than the male animal has to the female; for although some form of appropriation is found in some animals, in humans it is more perfect and definite. Furthermore, such love is more concealed in humans than in animals because reason usually restrains its excess and judges it base when it is not regulated by it; and because of the force that this carnal appetite has in humans, and because of its disobedience to reason, humans cover the <term>organs of generation</term> <note>The reproductive organs.</note> as things shameful and rebellious against moderate decency.

SOPHIA. Tell me the difference between humans and animals regarding the second cause of love, namely **generative succession**. 

PHILONE. Through generative succession, animals reciprocally love their children with fathers and mothers only, and especially those mothers who are usually their nurturers, or like the father who feeds them, and not otherwise. But humans love fathers and mothers together, and also brothers and other relatives through their proximity in birth. It is indeed true that sometimes human greed and other excesses cause love to be lost not 

<summary>Philone explains that while humans and animals share the same five natural causes of love, reason renders human love more intense, enduring, and exclusive. He also discusses how humans, unlike animals, extend familial love to a broader circle of kin and use reason to moderate and conceal carnal desires.</summary>

<keywords>reason, procreation, human nature, animal behavior, companionship, familial love, modesty, carnal appetite</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Sophia and Philo. Having discussed the first cause of love (procreation/desire), they now compare humans and animals regarding the other four causes: family bonds, benefits received, shared species/homeland, and companionship.</meta>

<sig>p iiii</sig>

...only of brothers and relatives, but also fathers and mothers and their own wives, which does not happen among irrational animals.
SO. Tell me the difference regarding the third cause of love—that is, **benefit** <note>Italian: *beneficio*. In this philosophical context, it refers to an act of kindness, a gift, or a service rendered from one to another.</note>. PH. Benefit is the reason why one person loves another, just as it is among animals, but in this I wish to praise irrational creatures more; for they are found to love more out of gratitude for the benefit received than out of hope of receiving one in the future. However, the <term>avarice</term> <note>Greed or the excessive desire for wealth, which Philo argues disrupts the natural flow of gratitude.</note> of men who are not virtuous makes them move more readily for the hope of a single benefit than for gratitude for many already received. And this cause of benefit is so broad that it seems to comprise the majority of the others. SO. In that fourth cause—being of the **same species**—tell me if there is any difference between humans and irrational animals. PH. Naturally, humans love one another as other animals of the same species do, and especially those who are of the same homeland or land. But humans do not have such true and firm love as irrational animals; for even the fiercest and most cruel animals do not use cruelty toward those of their own species: the lion does not prey upon another lion, nor does the serpent bite another serpent with venom. But humans receive more evil and offenses from other humans than from all other animals, or from any other contrary things in the universe. Envy—human envy—and the human sword do more harm than all the rest of accidental and natural things. This is the cause of the corruption of natural love among men: avarice and the care they have for superfluous things, from which <term>enmity</term> <note>Hostility or hatred; the opposite of friendship.</note> is generated—not only between those distant in different homelands, but even between those of the same province, the same city, and the same house; between brothers and brothers, fathers and sons, husbands and wives. To these, add still other human superstitions that are the cause of cruel enmities. SO. You have yet to speak of the final cause of love—that is, **companionship**, and whether in that there is some difference between humans and irrational animals. PH. Companionship and conversation <note>Italian: *società & conversazione*. In the Renaissance, "conversation" meant more than just talking; it referred to the whole manner of living and interacting with others.</note> have greater force in human love and friendship than in that of animals, because it is more internal, as speech makes it much more penetrating into the minds of men; and even when one is absent, the impression remains longer in the memory than it does in irrational creatures. SO. I have understood how all these five causes of love found in irrational animals are also found in...

<summary>Philo compares humans unfavorably to animals regarding gratitude and natural kinship, arguing that human greed and envy corrupt the natural bonds of species and family, while noting that human companionship is deepened by speech and memory.</summary>
<keywords>causes of love, benefit, gratitude, avarice, species, cruelty, envy, companionship, conversation, memory</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Philo and Sophia regarding the causes of love. Philo has just finished discussing how humans often treat their own kind with more cruelty than wild animals do. He now moves to the final two human-specific causes of love: physical/astrological temperament and moral virtue.</meta>

...men and their differences, but I would like to know if there is any other reason for love in men that is not found in irrational creatures. PH. There are two reasons for love in men of which animals are totally deprived. SO. Explain them. PH. One is the conformity of nature and **complexion** <term>complexion</term> <note>Italian: *complessione*. In Renaissance medicine and philosophy, this refers to an individual's specific temperament or "makeup," determined by the balance of the four bodily humors: blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile.</note> of one man with another, which—without any other reason—makes them become friends upon their first meeting. Finding no other reason for such friendship, it is said that their complexions "suit" one another. In effect, it is a certain similarity or harmonic correspondence that one complexion has with the other, just as one also finds hatred among men without apparent cause, which arises from a lack of similarity or a disproportion of their complexions. The **Astrologers** <note>During this period, astrology was considered a serious science related to medicine and philosophy, used to explain natural inclinations.</note> say that this friendly conformity proceeds from a similar position or proportionality of the planets and celestial signs at the birth of both individuals; likewise, the hostile deformity of complexions arises from a dissimilar and disproportionate celestial position at their birth. This cause of love and friendship is known in men, but not in animals. SO. What is the other? PH. The other is **moral and intellectual virtues**, which are the reasons why excellent men are greatly loved by good men. The merits of these virtues cause **honest love** <term>honest love</term> <note>Italian: *amore honesto*. Derived from the Latin *amor honestus*, this refers to a noble, virtuous love directed toward the "Good," as opposed to love based on mere utility or physical pleasure.</note>, which is the most worthy of all. For human beings, without any other cause, love one another effectively for virtue and wisdom alone with a love more perfect and more firm than that based on utility or pleasure. All the other five reasons for love are included within this one; this alone is "honest love" and is generated by right reason, and therefore it is not found in irrational animals. SOPH. I have understood how many reasons for love there are in men and in irrational animals, but I see that all of these are proper to living beings. Yet you say that love is not only common to living beings, but also to other **insensible bodies** <note>Inanimate objects, such as stones, elements, or celestial bodies, which do not possess sensory organs.</note>, which seems strange to me. PH. Why strange? SO. Because nothing can be loved if it is not first known, and insensible bodies do not have a **cognitive faculty** <term>cognitive faculty</term> <note>Italian: *uirtù conoscatiua*. The power or ability to perceive, recognize, or understand information about the world.</note> within themselves. Furthermore, love arises from the **will** or **appetite** <term>appetite</term> <note>Italian: *appetito*. In this philosophical sense, it refers to a natural inclination or "pull" toward what is good or necessary for an object's existence, not just physical hunger.</note> that is imprinted in the senses. Insensible bodies have neither will, nor appetite, nor sensation; how, then, can they have love? PH. Knowledge and appetite—and consequently love—exist in three modes: **sensitive**, **rational**, and **voluntary**. SO. Explain all three to me.

<summary>Philo identifies two unique human causes of love: astrological/humoral "complexion" and moral virtue (honest love). He then begins to explain to a skeptical Sophia how love can also exist in inanimate objects through different modes of knowledge and appetite.</summary>
<keywords>complexion, astrology, moral virtue, honest love, inanimate objects, appetite, cognitive faculty, Philo, Sophia</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Philo and Sophia. Having previously discussed the specific causes of human love—such as physical temperament and moral virtue—Philo now transitions into a systematic classification of love into three levels: natural, sensitive, and rational, explaining how these layers overlap in humans.</meta>

PHILO. Natural knowledge, appetite, or love is that which is found in non-sentient bodies, such as the elements and species of stones, and also plants, herbs, and trees; for all these have knowledge of their end and a natural inclination toward it. This inclination moves them toward that end, just as heavy bodies descend downward and light ones ascend upward, as if to a known and desired place of their own. This inclination is called, and truly is, natural appetite and love. Sensitive knowledge, or rather sensitive love, is that which is found in irrational animals, in order to pursue what is suitable and flee what is unsuitable—such as seeking food, drink, **temperance** <note>In this context, Philo likely refers to the animal's natural instinct for moderation or physical balance required for survival.</note>, mating, rest, and similar things. It is necessary first to know these things and then to crave or love them, and subsequently to pursue them; for if the animal did not know them, it would not desire them, nor would it love them if it did not crave them, nor would it pursue them to have them; and not having them, it could not live. But this knowledge is not rational, nor is this appetite or love voluntary, for the **will** <note>In Renaissance philosophy, "will" (volontà) is strictly defined as a faculty that requires rational deliberation, thus excluding animals.</note> does not exist without reason; rather, these are the works of the **sensitive faculty** <term>sensitive faculty</term> <note>The power of the soul related to perception through the senses and the physical desires that follow.</note>. For this reason, we call it sensitive knowledge and love, or more properly speaking, appetite. Rational and voluntary knowledge and love is found only in human beings, as it proceeds from and is governed by reason, which—among all bodies **subject to birth and decay** <note>original: "generabili & corruttibili." In Aristotelian physics, this refers to the "sublunary" world (the Earth) where things are born, change, and eventually die, unlike the eternal celestial spheres.</note>—is granted only to humans.

SOPHIA. You say that voluntary love exists only in humans and not in other animals or lower bodies; and you also say that sensitive love or appetite exists in irrational animals and not in insensible bodies; and you say that natural love and appetite is that which is found only in the lower, insensible bodies. I wish now to understand if this natural love is also found in animals alongside the sensitive love they properly possess, and if this natural and sensitive love is also found in humans together with the rational and voluntary love that is unique to them.

PHILO. You have asked well, and it is indeed so: for with the more excellent love, the less excellent is also found, but with that which is lesser, the greater is not always present. Thus, in human beings, along with rational and voluntary love, one also finds sensitive love—to pursue those sensible things that are suitable for life while fleeing the unsuitable—and one also finds in them the natural inclination of bodies...

<summary>Philo categorizes love into three hierarchical types—natural (inanimate objects/plants), sensitive (animals), and rational (humans)—explaining that higher beings possess the capacities of the levels below them.</summary>
<keywords>natural love, sensitive love, rational love, appetite, will, reason, hierarchy of being, Aristotelian physics</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues Philo's systematic explanation of the three levels of love. Having just defined sensitive love in animals, Philo now addresses Sophia’s skepticism regarding whether inanimate objects and lower animals can truly be said to "love" or "know" their destinations.</meta>

insensible bodies, so that if a man falls from a height he will naturally tend downward, as a heavy body. In animals, too, this natural inclination is found, for as heavy bodies they naturally seek the bottom—that is, the center of the earth—as their own known and desired place.

SOPHIA. What reasons do you have to call these natural inclinations "sensitive love"? For love would seem properly to be an effect of the **will** <note>In Renaissance philosophy, the "will" is a specific faculty of the rational soul; Sophia is arguing that without reason, there can be no true love.</note>, and the will (among all lower beings) is found only in humans; call the others appetite or inclination, but not love.

PHILONE. Things are known by their opposites; for as **Aristotle** <note>original: "Aristotile"</note> says, the science of opposites is one and the same. The opposite of this is and is called **Hate**; therefore, this should reasonably be called love. Just as in humans voluntary hate is the opposite of love, so in animals the hate of things unsuitable for life is the opposite of the love of suitable things. Thus the animal flees one and follows the other; for hate is the cause of making it flee, just as love is the cause of making it follow. In irrational bodies, there is a natural love of the heavy for the bottom, and for that reason it follows it, just as it flees the top because it holds it in hate; and the light body is the opposite, for it loves the top and hates the bottom. And just as hate is found in all, so love is found in all.

SOPHIA. And how can one who has neither reason, nor sense, nor imagination—as these lower and insensible bodies are—possess knowledge?

PHILONE. Although they do not have these cognitive powers within themselves, they are directed by Nature, which knows all lower things, or rather by the **Soul of the World** <term>Soul of the World</term> <note>The *Anima Mundi*, a Neoplatonic concept describing an intermediate force that animates the physical universe and carries out the divine plan.</note>, in a direct and infallible knowledge of its natural things for the preservation of their natures.

SOPHIA. And how can one love who does not feel?

PHILONE. Just as lower bodies are rightly directed by Nature to know their end and their proper places, so they are directed by her to love them and desire them, and to move themselves to find them when they are separated from them. Just as the **Arrow** <note>original: "Saetta"</note> seeks the mark directly, not through its own knowledge, but through the knowledge of the archer by whom it is directed, so these bodies seek their own end and place, not through their own knowledge, but through the direct knowledge of the **First Creator**, infused into the soul of the world and into the universal nature of lower things; so that just as the inclination of the arrow comes...

<summary>Philo argues that even inanimate objects possess "natural love," directed not by their own reason but by the "Soul of the World" and the Creator, using the famous analogy of an arrow guided by an archer.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, natural love, Hate, Soul of the World, Anima Mundi, teleology, First Creator, physics of elements</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue where Philo explains that even inanimate objects possess a form of "knowledge" and "love" that directs them to their natural places. He is currently distinguishing between artificial appetite (willed) and natural appetite (instinctual/elemental).</meta>

...from knowledge and love, or artificial appetite; so too that of these irrational bodies comes from knowledge and natural love.

SOPHIA. The manner of love and knowledge found in these **inanimate bodies** <note>original: "corpi morti" (literally "dead bodies"); in Renaissance physics, this refers to non-living matter like stones or elements which lack a sensitive soul.</note> resonates with me, but I would like to know if there is perhaps any other love or appetite in them besides that which they have for their proper places—such as the light tending upward and the heavy downward.

PHILO. The love that the elements and other inanimate bodies have for their proper places, and the hate they have for their opposites, is like the love and hate that animals have for things that are unsuitable for them. Thus they flee one and follow the other; furthermore, this love is of the same kind as that which terrestrial animals have for the earth, or sea-creatures for the water, or winged creatures for the air, and the **Salamander** <note>According to Renaissance natural history, the salamander was a creature born from and sustained by fire.</note> for the fire, which is said to be born in it and dwell there. Such is the love of the elements for their proper places. Beyond these types of love, I tell you that in the elements are found all the other five causes of reciprocal love that we said were found in animals.

SOPHIA. All of those?

PHILO. All of them.

SOPHIA. Tell me about them in detail.

PHILO. I shall begin with the last one, which is the love of the same **species** <note>original: "spetie"; here meaning kind, category, or essential nature.</note>, because it is the most manifest. You will see that parts of the earth found outside of the whole move with effective love to unite with the entire earth. And so stones that form in the air run quickly to the earth; and rivers and other waters generated in the hollows of the earth—from vapors that exhale there and convert into water—as soon as they are found in sufficient quantity, run to find the sea and the whole element of water, because of the love they have for their species. And the airy vapors or winds generated in the hollows of the earth strive to break out through **earthquakes** <note>original: "tremuoti"; early modern science often taught that earthquakes were caused by wind or air trapped underground struggling to escape to its natural place in the sky.</note>, desiring to find their element of air because of the love they have for their species. And likewise, the fire generated down here moves to rise to the place of its element in the upper part, for the love of its species.

SOPHIA. I understand the love elements have for their own species; tell me of the other causes.

PHILO. I will speak of the penultimate of the five causes of love, which is the fourth: **association** <note>original: "società"; referring to the "social" compatibility or companionship between different elements.</note>, because this too is manifest by being proportioned to their natural places.

SOPHIA. And what other association is found in the elements and other bodies?

PHILO. Each of the elements (that is: earth, air, water, and fire) enjoys rest near one of the others but not near all of them. The earth flees the approach of the heavens and of fire, and seeks the cen...

<summary>Philo explains that inanimate elements possess a natural love and "social" inclination, moving toward their own kind (species) and seeking rest near compatible elements. He uses examples like rivers seeking the sea and winds causing earthquakes to illustrate these elemental desires.</summary>
<keywords>natural love, elements, species, association, physics, earthquakes, fire, water, earth, air, Philo, Sophia</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>The text continues from a discussion regarding the positioning of the physical elements in the universe, specifically concluding a point about the Earth (the "tro" at the beginning likely completes the word "centro" or "l'altro").</meta>

...center, which is the farthest from Heaven; it likes to be near the Water and near the Air from below, but not from above. For if it finds itself above, it flees to the bottom and never rests until it is as far from Heaven as possible. 
SO. And why does it do this, since every good comes from Heaven? 
PH. It does so because it is the heaviest / and the coarsest <note>original: "grossa," referring to the density of the material</note> of all the elements; and being sluggish, it likes rest more than any of the others. Since Heaven is always in continuous motion without ever stopping, the Earth, in order to remain still, distances itself from it as much as it can. Only in the center (which is the lowest point) does it find rest, surrounded by Water on one side and by Air on the other. 

SO. I have understood about the Earth; tell me about the Water. 
PH. Water also has something of the heavy / and the sluggish, but not as much as the Earth, yet more than the others. For this reason, it too flees from Heaven / so as not to move with speed, as the Air / and the Fire do. It seeks the depths / and likes to stay close to the Earth; but above and below it has the Air, for which it feels love, whereas with Fire it has enmity and hatred. Because of this, it flees / and distances itself from it, and cannot bear to stay with it without the company of the others. 

SO. Tell me about the Air? 
PH. To the Air (by its lightness and subtlety) the celestial nature and proximity are pleasing; and with its <term>levity: its natural lack of weight</term> it seeks it as much as it can and ascends on high. It does not go immediately next to the heavens, because it is not of a substance as purified as Fire, which takes the first place. Therefore, Air loves to be near Fire, beneath it, and also loves the vicinity of the Water / and of the Earth; but it cannot be below them, only above. With ease, it follows the continuous circular motion of the heavens, and it is friendly to Fire and to Water. Because these two are contrary enemies to one another, Air has placed itself in the middle of them / as a friend to both, so that they cannot damage each other with continuous war. 

SO. I still need to know about the Fire. 
PH. Fire is the most subtle / and light / and purified of all the elements; it has love for none of them / except for the Air, whose proximity it enjoys; thus it stays above it. It loves the heavens and never rests, wherever it finds itself, until it is near them. This is the <term>social love: the natural attraction or "affinity" that keeps the physical elements in their proper order</term> found in the four Elements. 

SO. I like this, but why have you not assigned in this the cause of why Fire is so hot, and Water is so cold, and the qualities of the others? 
PHILO. Because this does not belong to the knowledge of love, but I will tell you, for it is useful to the others. Know that the Heavens, with their continuous motion, and with the rays of the Sun and the other Planets and Fixed Stars of the eighth Heaven, heat this globe of the <term>dead body: the material, sublunary world which is considered "dead" or inert compared to the living, moving heavens</term>,

<summary>This page describes the "social love" or natural affinities between the four elements (Earth, Water, Air, and Fire), explaining their hierarchical arrangement and how they interact based on their weight and purity.</summary>
<keywords>Four Elements, Earth, Water, Air, Fire, Aristotelian physics, Social Love, celestial motion, cosmology, Sofia, Philo</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>The text continues from the previous page's discussion of the elements. The word "A..." at the end of the last page is completed here as "Aria" (Air) or part of the description of the spheres below the Moon.</meta>

...which fills the entire concavity of the Sphere of the Moon; and that first part of this globe <note>Referring to the "sublunary" world—everything below the moon</note> which is closest to Heaven, being heated, becomes more purified and greatly thinned, becoming light. Its heat is so intense that it consumes all the moisture that remains, so that it dries it out, and this is **Fire**. Then, as this celestial heat spreads further into that part of this globe that follows the fire, it makes it also hot, but not so much that it consumes the moisture; and this is **Air**, which is hot and moist. Through this heat, it is also purified and thinned, and remains light—only a little less so than fire, for being less hot. This Celestial heat spreads further in this globe, beyond the Air, but it is not so much that it creates a hot element; rather, by its distance from Heaven it remains cold, but not so much that moisture cannot exist there. It remains heavy due to the density caused by the cold, and seeks the bottom; and this is the element of **Water**, cold and moist. Beyond this, the coldness in the remaining center of this globe, beneath the Water, is so great that it constricts the moisture, and it remains a very heavy body, cold and dry: this is the **Earth**. 

So it is that Air and Fire—which by their proximity receive more of the heat and celestial benefit, which is the life of the inferior bodies—love heaven more; wherever they are found, they approach it and move with it in its continuous circular motion. The Earth and the Water, because they receive little of the celestial heat and life, do not love it so, nor do they approach it; rather, they flee from it so as to be able to rest quietly without moving with it continually and circularly.

**SO.** Since the Earth is the lowest and most vile <note>original: "uile," meaning lowly or least noble in the cosmic hierarchy</note> of all the other Elements as you say, and is furthest from the source of life which is heaven, how is it that so many diverse things are generated within it—more than in any other Element? Such as **Stones** of so many kinds, some large, clean, and beautiful, others clear and very precious; and **Metals**, not only the common ones like Iron, Lead, Copper, Tin, and Quicksilver <note>original: "Argento uiuo"</note> but others rich and lustrous like Silver and Gold. Then there is such a diversity of Herbs, Flowers, Trees, and Fruits as the Earth produces; and further, it has such a multitude and variety of animals, all of which are bound to the earth. For although some plants and a great abundance of different animals are found in the Sea, and likewise those that fly in the Air, they all nevertheless have an acknowledgement to the earth, and it is there that they principally remain. Above all, in the earth is generated the **human race** <note>original: "humana generatione"</note> of miraculous perfection among all the bodies that are under Heaven, which is not generated nor placed in any other sphere of the elements; how...

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Philo (PH.) and Sophia (SO.), expanding on the previous page's description of the four elements. Having just defined Earth as the heaviest and coldest element, the conversation now turns to its unique role in the cosmos despite its "lowly" status.</meta>

Tell me then, you say that the earth is the most vile and the most **mortified** <note>In this context, "mortified" means "deadened" or lacking in the vital heat and movement associated with the heavens.</note> of all four elements:

PH. Although the Earth, because it is the most distant from heaven, and in itself the most dense, cold, low, and most alien to life, it nonetheless—by standing united in the center—receives all together within itself all the **influences** <term>influences: the invisible "flow" of power from the stars believed to shape earthly events</term> and rays of all the stars, planets, and celestial bodies. And here they are so **complexioned** <term>complexioned: the balancing and mixing of different qualities (hot, cold, moist, dry) into a single substance</term> that (drawing into itself the virtue of all the other elements) they come to be combined in such ways and manners that all the things you mentioned are generated. Such a thing would not be possible in the place of any other element, for they are not a common receptacle where all celestial and elemental virtues unite. In the other elements, these virtues only pass through but do not stop; they only settle in the earth because of its density and because it is in the center, where all the rays strike and, by **reverberating** <note>reverberation: the "bouncing back" of celestial light and energy, which increases its intensity, much like sunlight reflecting off a mirror.</note>, they become stronger. So it is that this earth is the proper and ordinary wife of the Celestial Body, and the other elements are his concubines, since heaven generates in her all, or the greater part, of its offspring, and she is adorned with such many and diverse things.

SO. I am satisfied regarding my doubt; let us return to our purpose. Tell me about the other causes of love in men and animals, and if they are found in the elements and in other inanimate bodies—such as that third cause of benefit, the second of generative succession, and the first of the desire and delight in generation.

PH. That cause of "benefit" in these elemental bodies is the same as that of "generative succession," because the thing generated loves its generator as its benefactor, and the generator loves the thing generated as the recipient of its benefit. This cause of generative succession is indeed found in those things generated from the elements; for you will see things generated in the region of the air from the vapors that ascend from the earth and the sea. When the vapors are moist, water, snow, and hail are generated from them; once these are generated, they immediately descend with a loving impetus to find the sea and the earth, their mother. And if the vapors are dry, winds and fiery things are made from them; the winds seek the air with their breathings, and the fiery part goes higher seeking the fire, each one moved by the love of its own origin. You will also see metals and stones generated by the earth; when they are found outside of it, they seek it with speed and never rest until they are within it, just as children seek their mothers, with whom they find rest. So too the earth generates them with love, holds them, and preserves them; and plants, herbs, and trees have so much love...

<summary>Philo explains that while Earth is the "lowest" element, it acts as the "wife" of the Heavens by catching and concentrating celestial rays to create life. He argues that even inanimate things like rain and stones show "love" by seeking to return to their place of origin.</summary>
<keywords>elements, earth as wife, celestial influence, generation, natural place, planetary rays, complexions, Leo Hebraeus, love in inanimate objects</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Philo and Sophia, moving from the Earth’s role as the "wife of Heaven" to her maternal character. Philo explains how the Earth nurtures her offspring, leading into a deeper discussion on the "Prime Matter" that underlies all elemental change.</meta>

to Earth their mother, and their generator, so that they never wish to depart from her without **corruption** <note>corruption: in this philosophical context, "corruption" refers to the decay or dissolution of a physical body back into its basic elements.</note>, but rather embrace her with the affection of their roots, as children do to their mothers; and this Earth, like a merciful mother with no small charity and love, not only generates them, but always takes care to nourish them from her own vitals and humors, drawing them from her interior to the surface to maintain them with these, just as a mother does, who draws milk from her vitals to her breasts to nurse her children; furthermore, when the Earth lacks the moisture to give to them, with prayers and supplications she asks it of the Heaven and the Air, and buys and barters it with her ascending vapors, from which rainwater is **generated** <note>original: "gña" (genera). The text uses a common scribal abbreviation.</note> to nourish her plants and her animals. What mother could be more full of mercy and charity toward her children?

SO. Certainly such care is wonderful in a body without a soul, such as the Earth, and much more wonderful is the care of Him who was able to make it so **curious** <term>curious: meaning skillfully or intricately fashioned, reflecting the ingenuity of the Creator.</term>. It only remains for me now to understand how the first cause of love in animals—which is the desire and delight of generation—is found in the Elements, and in the Matter of all things, and in bodies without a sensitive soul.

PH. Generative love is found in the Elements and in the Matter of all inferior things beyond these four Elements. We indeed see that from these, all generated things are produced.

SO. It is indeed so, but the Elements themselves are also generable; therefore you must tell me from what things they are generated.

PH. From what? From one another. We see that from Water, Air is made; and from Air, Water is made; from Fire, Air [is made]; and from Air, Fire; and so also the Earth.

SO. This too that you say is true; but what of those things that are generated from the Elements? These Elements are the matter and foundation that remain in the thing generated by all four of them united **virtually** <term>virtually: meaning that the "virtue" or essential power of the elements remains present in a compound, even if their outward form has changed.</term>; whereas when one element is generated from another, it cannot be so, for when Fire is converted into Water, Fire does not remain in the Water; rather, Fire is corrupted and Water is generated. And because it is so, it is necessary to assign some matter common to all the Elements, in which these **transmutations** <term>transmutations: the process by which one substance changes into another by losing one form and taking on another.</term> of theirs can take place; which, being once informed in the form of Air through sufficient alteration, and leaving that form of Air, takes the form of Water, and so of the others.

PH. This is what the Philosophers call **Prime Matter**, and the most ancient call it **Chaos** <note>original: "Caos"</note>, which in Greek means **confusion** <note>original: "confusione"</note>, because all things are potentially and genuinely within...

<summary>Philo uses the metaphor of Earth as a nursing mother to explain how even inanimate elements exhibit "generative love." The dialogue then transitions into a technical discussion of Prime Matter (Chaos) as the underlying substance that allows the four elements to transform into one another.</summary>
<keywords>Earth as mother, nursing metaphor, generative love, four elements, transmutation, Prime Matter, Chaos, Aristotelian physics, Philo, Sophia</keywords>

that together / and in confusion / and from that all things are made, each

<meta>Continuing the dialogue between Philo and Sophia regarding how prime matter receives forms through the influence of the heavens and the harmony of the elements.</meta>

by the virtue of heaven, in the whole, other forms more excellent than any of the elements, in diverse degrees, while the elements still remain materially mixed within them. **SO.** What are these forms that the elements (through their <note>original: "amicitia"; "friendship" refers to the harmonious proportion or balance between the four contrary elements (earth, water, air, fire) that allows them to combine into a single substance.</note> friendship) receive? **PH.** And how many are the degrees?
In the first and most subtle degree of friendship, they receive the forms of mixtures that are not animate <note>i.e., things without life or a soul.</note>, such as the forms of stones—some dark and others clearer, and others lustrous and most bright—in which the earth provides hardness; water provides clarity; air provides transparency; and fire provides luster or, rather, luminosity, and the rays that are found in precious stones. Also resulting from this first friendly mixture of the elements are the forms of Metals: some coarse, like Iron and Lead; others cleaner, like Copper, Tin, and Quicksilver; others clear and beautiful, like Silver and Gold. In all of these, water dominates so much that fire is able to liquefy them. In all these things, the form of the mixture—whether Stone or Metal—is more perfect the greater and more equal the friendship of the elements is within it. And when the friendship of these four contrary elements is of a higher degree, and its love is more united, with greater quality and with less excess of any one of them, they not only have the forms of mixture, but they also receive more excellent forms, such as the animate ones. First among these are those of the <term>vegetative soul: the lowest level of the soul in classical philosophy, responsible for basic biological functions like growth and reproduction</term>, which cause in plants germination, nutrition, growth in every direction, and the generation of their like through seeds or branches; and thus are generated all species of plants. Of these, the least perfect are the Herbs; Trees are the most perfect. And among them, each species is more perfect than another and of more excellent operation the more these four contrary elements are found in it with greater love, and with more unity and equal friendship; and this is the second degree of their friendship. And when the love of the elements is greater, more united, and more equal, it not only receives the forms of the mixture and the forms of the vegetative soul (of nutrition, growth, and generation) but they also receive, furthermore, the forms of the <term>sensitive soul: the level of the soul that grants living beings the ability to perceive their environment and move themselves</term>, along with sense, local movement, imagination, and appetite. And from this degree of friendship are generated all species of animals: terrestrial, aquatic, and volatile <note>original: "uolatili"; referring to birds or any creatures that fly.</note>. Some of these are imperfect, having neither movement nor sense, except for that of touch; but the perfect animals have all the senses and movement, and one species is more excellent than another in its operation, the greater and of more...

<summary>Philo explains the hierarchy of being based on the "friendship" or balance of the four elements, moving from inanimate stones and metals to the vegetative soul in plants and the sensitive soul in animals.</summary>
<keywords>elemental friendship, four elements, mineralogy, metals, vegetative soul, sensitive soul, Aristotelian biology, hierarchy of being</keywords>

--- Page 25 ---
<meta>Continuing the dialogue between Philo and Sophia regarding the degrees of love found within the material world, specifically how the union of the four elements allows for the presence of the soul.</meta>

...re-union and equality, and this is the third degree of love in the **elements**. The fourth and last degree of love and friendship that is found in the **elements** is when they come into the most equal love and the most united friendship that is possible; then, they not only receive within themselves the mixed, vegetative, and sensitive forms, along with the motive faculties, but they are even capable of participating in a form more distant and alien to the sight of these generable and corruptible bodies; indeed, they participate in the form proper to **celestial** and eternal bodies, which is the <term>intellective soul<note>In Renaissance philosophy, the 'intellective soul' is the highest part of the human soul, capable of reason and connecting the mortal human to the divine or eternal realm.</note></term>, which alone among all inferior beings is found in the human species. **SO.** And how was it possible that man, being made of these same contrary and corruptible elements, could have obtained an eternal and intellectual form, annexed to the celestial bodies? **PH.** Because the love of his elements is so equal, uniform, and perfect that it unites all the contrariety of the elements, and the body remains distant from every contradiction and opposition, just like the celestial body, which is stripped of every contrary; and because of that, it comes to participate in that intellectual and eternal form with which the celestial bodies are usually <term>informed<note>To 'inform' here means to give shape or essence to matter; the soul is the 'form' that gives life and character to the physical 'matter' of the body.</note></term>. **SO.** I have never heard of such friendship in the elements, though I know well that (according to the perfection of the <term>complexion<note>Also called 'temperament,' this refers to the specific balance of the four humors or elements within an individual body.</note></term> of those elements) the form of the compound becomes more or less perfect. **PH.** The complexion of the elements and their friendship—how they remain contraries united together without strife or contradiction—does that not seem like love and friendship to you? Some call this friendship **Harmony**, **Music**, and **Concordance**; you know that friendship makes concordance, just as enmity causes discord. And for this reason, the philosopher <term>Empedocles<note>original: "Empedocles"; a Greek Pre-Socratic philosopher who famously taught that Love (Philia) and Strife (Neikos) were the two forces that moved the four elements.</note></term> says that the causes of the generation of all inferior things are six: namely, the four Elements, Friendship, and Enmity. The friendship of the four contrary elements causes all the generations of the bodies composed of them, and their enmity causes their corruption; because according to these four degrees of the generation of love which I have told you about in the four elements—which are the cause of the generation of all composite bodies—in the four degrees of composition, you must understand just as many degrees of hatred, which are the cause of their dissolution and corruption. So, just as every evil and ruin comes from the enmity of these four contrary elements, so every good and generation comes from their love and friendship. **SO.** I like the discourse you have made regarding the manners and causes of love that are found in this inferior world—that is, in all generable and corruptible things—as much in men as in brute animals, as in plants, and in mixed things that have no soul at all, and also in the four elements and in the first matter common to all,

<meta>Continuing from the previous discussion where Philo explained how the perfect balance of elements in humans allows them to participate in the intellectual and eternal form of the celestial bodies.</meta>

...and I see well that just as one species of animals loves another and keeps company with it, while another hates and flees, so too among plants there are some species found to be friends with others, growing together, and when they are in company they <term>generate<note>The original text uses "gñrano," an abbreviation for "generano," meaning to produce or reproduce.</note></term> better; and others are enemies which, being near each other, spoil one another. And we see the **Metals**, one accompanying the other in its mineral vein, and another opposing it. And likewise in **Precious Stones**. And we see the **Magnet** <note>original: "Calamita"</note> being so loved by **Iron** that, despite the size and weight of the iron, it moves and goes to find it. And in conclusion, I see that there is no body under heaven that does not have love / desire / and natural appetite / whether it be sensual / or truly voluntary, according to what you have said; but in the **celestial bodies** / and in the spiritual intellects, it would seem strange to me if love were found / since within them there are none of the passions of these <term>generable bodies<note>Bodies subject to birth, change, and decay, unlike the "eternal" stars and planets.</note></term>. 

**PH.** In the celestial bodies / and in intellectual things, there is no less love found than in the inferior ones; rather, it is more eminent and of greater excellence. 

**SO.** I would like to know in what way, because the principal / and most common cause that I see is the love of procreation, and there being no procreation among eternal things, how can love exist in them? 

**PH.** There is no procreation in them because they are <term>ungenerable<note>Meaning they were not "born" or created in a physical sense and do not change over time.</note></term> and incorruptible, but the procreation of inferior things comes from the heavens, as from a true father, just as **prime matter** is the first mother in procreation, and then the **four elements**, especially the earth, which is the most manifest mother; surely you know that the fathers of procreation are no less full of love than the mothers; indeed, they have a more excellent and perfect love. 

**SO.** Tell me more extensively about this paternal love of the heavens. 

**PH.** In general I tell you that as the heaven—the father of all things that can be born—moves in its continuous circular motion above the entire globe of **prime matter** <note>The fundamental, formless substance that makes up the universe before it is given a specific shape.</note>, and by moving and mixing all its parts, she germinates all the genera / species / and individuals of the inferior world of procreation, just as when the male moves upon the female, and by moving her, she produces children. 

**SO.** Tell me about this propagation more particularly and clearly. 

**PH.** **Prime matter** (like a female) has a receiving body and humidity that nourishes it, a spirit that penetrates it, and the fire and natural heat that tempers and vivifies it. 

**SOPHIA.** Explain each one to me. 

**PH.** The **Earth** is the body of prime matter, receiving all the <term>influence<note>In Renaissance thought, "influence" referred to the literal flowing of celestial power or energy from the stars down into earthly matter.</note></term> of her male, which is the heaven. **Water** is the humidity / that nourishes her. **Air** is the spirit / that penetrates her. **Fire** is the natural heat / that tempers and vivifies her.

<summary>Sophia and Philo discuss how love and attraction exist in all things, from magnets to plants, eventually concluding that the Heavens act as a father to Earth's "prime matter" to create all life.</summary>

<meta>Continuing from the previous discussion where Philo explained that love exists even in eternal celestial bodies, the dialogue now shifts to how these heavens interact with the earth through a metaphor of biological reproduction.</meta>

**SO.** In what way does heaven influence its generation upon the earth?

**PH.** The entire body of heaven is the male / who covers and surrounds her with continuous motion, and she (though she is still) moves just a little bit because of the movement of her male; but her moisture, which is the water, and her spirit, which is the air, and her natural heat, which is the fire, move actively through the masculine celestial motion. In the same way, all these things move within the female at the time of coitus / through the motion of the male, even though she does not move corporally, but rather stays quiet to receive the seed of <term>generation<note>The process of creation or reproduction of physical things.</note></term> from her male.

**SO.** What seed does heaven provide to the earth, and how can it provide it?

**PH.** The seed that the earth receives from heaven is the **dew** / and the **rainwater**, which—along with the solar / and lunar rays / and those of the other planets / and fixed stars—generates in the earth / and in the sea all the species / and individuals of composite bodies within the four degrees of composition that I have told you about.

**SO.** What specifically in heaven are the producers of this seed?

**PH.** The entire heaven produces it with its continuous motion, just as the whole human body, collectively, produces sperm; and in the manner that the human body is composed of homogeneous / non-organized members—that is / bones / nerves / veins, <term>panniculi<note>Thin membranes or layers of tissue.</note></term> / and cartilages, besides the flesh / which is a filler between one and the other—so the great body of the eighth heaven is composed of fixed stars of diverse natures / which are divided into five magnitudes, and another species of nebulous stars, besides the substance of the <term>diaphanous<note>Transparent or translucent; here referring to the clear, crystalline substance of the celestial spheres.</note></term> body of heaven which continues and fills the space between one and another.

**SO.** And the seven planets / what purpose do they serve / in the generation of this seed of the world?

**PH.** The seven planets are the seven principal organic members in the generation of this seed, just as in man there are those that generate the Sperm.

**SO.** Tell me about them in detail.

**PH.** The generation of sperm in man / depends first on the **heart** and the spirits / with natural heat, which is the formal element in the sperm. Second is the **brain**, representing the moisture, which is the matter of the sperm. Third is the **liver** / which tempers the sperm with a gentle <term>decoction<note>A process of "cooking" or refining fluids within the body, a common concept in pre-modern medicine.</note></term>, and creates / and increases it / from the most purified blood. Fourth is the **spleen**, which (after it has purified it by attracting the <term>melancholy dregs<note>Referring to "black bile," one of the four humors thought to govern health and temperament; here seen as a waste product to be filtered.</note></term>) thickens it and makes it viscous and windy. Fifth is the **spine**, which with its own decoction, makes it stinging / hot / and inciting, especially through the portion of <term>choler<note>Yellow bile, associated with heat and aggression.</note></term> which they always receive from the gallbladder. Sixth are the **testicles**, in which the sperm receives purification / of <term>complexion<note>The specific balance of humors (hot, cold, wet, dry) that determines a person's nature and physical state.</note></term> / and a feminine generative nature. Seventh / and last is the **penis** <note>original: "uerga"</note>, which provides the seed to the recipient female.

**SO.** I understan—

<meta>Philo continues the previous discussion, moving from the general structure of the heavens to the specific roles of the seven planets, comparing them to the internal organs of a human being to explain how they "father" life on Earth.</meta>

...how these seven organic members work together in the generation of male sperm. But what does this have to do with the seven planets?

**PH.** In the same way, the seven planets in the heavens work together for the generation of the "world's seed."

**SO.** In what manner?

**PH.** The **Sun** is the heat of heaven, from which comes the natural, spiritual warmth that causes vapors to rise from the earth and the sea; it generates the water and the dew, which is the seed. Its rays and its "aspects" <note>In astrology, an 'aspect' is the specific angular relationship between planets.</note> guide it, especially through the change of the four seasons of the year, which it creates through its annual motion.

The **Moon** is the brain of heaven, causing the moisture that is in the seed; just as the winds change and waters descend according to its phases, it creates the dampness of the night and the dew, which is seminal nourishment.

**Jupiter** is the liver of heaven, which with its gentle heat aids in the generation of water, in the tempering of the air, and the mildness of the seasons.

**Saturn** is the spleen of heaven, which through its coldness and dryness causes vapors to thicken and waters to freeze; it moves the winds that carry the water and temper the dissipation of the heat.

**Mars** is the gallbladder and the veins of heaven. With its excessive heat, it assists in the ascent of vapors and liquefies water, making it flow; by thinning it, Mars makes it penetrating and gives it morning seminal heat, so that the coldness of Saturn and the Moon does not make the seed unfit for generation due to a lack of active heat.

**Venus** is the testicles of heaven, and she has great power in producing water that is good and perfect for sowing. Her coldness and moisture are benign and very <term>digested<note>In Renaissance science, 'digestion' refers to the refinement or maturation of a substance through natural heat.</note></term>, and fit to cause earthly generation, because of the proportion and proximity that the kidneys have to the testicles in the generation of sperm. The poets have imagined Mars to be in love with Venus because one provides the impulse and the other the moisture prepared for the seed.

**Mercury** is the <term>virga<note>The penis; from the Latin for 'rod' or 'staff'.</note></term> of heaven; sometimes he is direct and sometimes retrograde. At times he actually causes rain, and at other times he prevents it. He moves primarily by his proximity to the Sun and the aspect of the Moon, just as the organ of desire moves by the prompting of the heart, the imagination, and the memory of the brain.

So you see, Sophia, how heaven is the most perfect husband to the earth; with all his organic and homogeneous members, he moves and strives to pour the seed into her, and to generate within her so many beautiful generations and such diversity. Do you not see that such supreme diligence and subtle procedure would not continue, if not for...

<meta>Philo continues the explanation of the celestial influence on the physical world, moving from the specific qualities of each planet to the overarching relationship between Heaven and Earth as a married couple, and then mapping the seven planets onto the human body.</meta>

<sig>B iiij</sig>

...a most fervent and steadfast love, which heaven has for the earth as its own lady with whom it is in love—or rather, to whom it is married. It has love for all generated things and takes miraculous care in their nourishment and preservation, as if they were its own children. The earth, in turn, is a mother who loves heaven as a most beloved husband or lover and benefactor; and the things generated love heaven as a pious father and excellent caretaker. With this reciprocal love, the corporeal universe is united, and the world is adorned and sustained. What other demonstration do you wish to understand regarding the commonality of love?

**SO.** I have understood how wonderful the matrimonial and reciprocal love is between the earth and the heaven, and thus how much the earth shares in the properties of a wife, and the heaven in those of a husband, with its seven planets corresponding to the members that work together in the generation of human sperm. I have already understood that each of these seven planets has significance (according to the astrologers) over a part of the human members—but not those specifically for generation. Rather, they are said to have significance over the external members of the head, which serve our sensible and internal cognition <note>In Renaissance philosophy, "internal cognition" refers to the "internal senses" like imagination, memory, and common sense.</note>.

**PH.** It is quite true that the seven planets have significance over the seven openings in the head that serve feeling and cognition. That is: the **Sun** over the right eye and the **Moon** over the left, because both are the "eyes of heaven." **Saturn** is over the right ear, and **Jupiter** over the left—or, according to others, the reverse. **Mars** is over the right nostril, and **Venus** over the left—or, according to others, the reverse. **Mercury** is over the tongue or the mouth, because he rules over speech and doctrine. But this does not prevent them (as the astrologers say) from also having significance over these other seven members of the body that work together in generation, as I told you.

**SO.** For what reason are these two modes of partial significance attributed to human members?

**PH.** Because these seven members of cognition in man correspond to those seven of generation.

**SO.** In what way?

**PH.** The heart and the brain are in the body like the eyes are in the head; the liver and the spleen are like the two ears; the kidneys and the testicles are like the two nostrils; the <term>penis<note>Original: "verga," literally "rod" or "staff," a common Renaissance euphemism.</note></term> is proportioned to the tongue in its position, its shape, its extension, and its contraction. It is placed in the center of the whole work, for just as the movement of the penis produces corporal generation, so the tongue produces spiritual generation through disciplined speech, creating spiritual "children" just as the penis creates corporal ones. And just as the kiss is common to both—the one imitating the other—and just as all...

<meta>Continuing Philo’s explanation from the previous page, where he began mapping the seven planets to the sensory openings of the human head, he now draws a parallel between the organs of "cognition" in the head and the organs of "generation" (reproduction) in the body, eventually explaining why the human being is called a "Microcosm."</meta>

the others serve the tongue in cognition, and she is the goal of apprehension and of the outcome of that cognition, and in her consists likewise the goal and its result. In the same way, all the others serve the rod <term>verga: literally "rod" or "staff," a standard Renaissance term for the penis</term> in generation, and in it consists their goal and their result. And just as the tongue is placed between the two hands, which are instruments of execution for that which is known and spoken, so the rod is placed between the feet, which are instruments of motion for approaching the recipient female.

**SO.** I have understood this corresponding proportion of the cognitive members of the head to the generative members of the body; but tell me, why are there not similarly two kinds of corresponding planets in heaven—one for cognition and one for generation—to make the similarity more perfect?

**PH.** Heaven, because of its simplicity and spirituality, generates inferior things with the same members and instruments used for cognition. Thus, the heart and the brain, which are the producers of the generative seed of heaven, are the eyes with which it sees—namely, the Sun and the Moon. The liver and the spleen, which temper the seed, are the ears with which it hears—namely, Saturn and Jupiter. The kidneys and the testicles, which perfect the seed, are the nostrils with which it smells—namely, Mars and Venus. The rod that delivers the seed is the Mercurial tongue, which is the judge of cognition. But in man and other perfect animals <note>In this context, "perfect" refers to "complete" or "complex" organisms, specifically those that reproduce sexually.</note>, though they are in the image and likeness of heaven, it was nevertheless necessary for their cognitive members to be distinct from their generative ones; the former were placed in the upper part of the head, and the latter in the lower part of the body, corresponding one to the other.

**SO.** I am satisfied with this, but I remain in doubt: you have likened heaven to the male, and matter, the earth, and the other elements to the female. Yet I have always understood that man is a likeness not only of heaven, but of the entire universe, both corporeal and incorporeal together.

**PH.** That is the truth: man is the image of the entire universe, and for this reason the Greeks call him <term>Microcosmos: from the Greek "mikros" (small) and "kosmos" (world/universe)</term>, which means "small world." Nevertheless, man—and likewise every other perfect animal—contains within himself both male and female, because his sperm is preserved in both of them and not in one alone. Therefore, not only in the Latin language does "man" <note>original: "homo"</note> signify both the male and the female, but also in the Hebrew language—the most ancient mother and origin of all languages. **Adam**, which means "man," signifies both male and female, and in its proper meaning contains both together. And the philosophers affirm that heaven alone is a perfect animal; and Pythagoras posited that within it there were "right" and "left," just as in every other perfect

<meta>Continuing the discussion from the previous page, Philo is explaining the macrocosmic nature of the universe. Having compared the organs of the human head and body to the planets, he now moves to the broader structure of the heavens, specifically how ancient philosophers like Pythagoras and Aristotle assigned "body parts" and directions (right, left, up, down) to the celestial sphere.</meta>

...animal, saying that the half of the heavens from the **Equatorial line** toward the **North** <note>original: "Tramontana," the North Star or the North wind, commonly used to denote the North</note> saw larger, clearer, and more numerous fixed stars than those seen from the Equator toward the other Pole. It also seemed to him that this caused a greater and more excellent **generation** <note>In this context, "generation" refers to the creation and life-cycles of living things</note> in the lower parts of the earth in that region than in the other. He called the other half of the heavens (the one from the Equatorial line to the other **Antarctic Pole**, which we cannot see) the **left** of the heavens. 

But the philosopher **Aristotle** (confirming that the heavens are a perfect animal) says that it not only has these two parts of an animal—namely, right and left—but also possesses the other parts of an animal: front and back (which are the face and shoulders) and up and down (which are the head and feet). In an animal, all these six parts are found to be divided and distinct. The right and left presuppose the other four, without which they could not exist; for the right and left are parts of the **width** of the animal’s body, while up and down (the head and feet) are parts of the **length**, which naturally precedes width. The front and back (the face and shoulders) are parts of the **depth** of the animal’s body, which is the foundation of both length and width. 

Thus, if there are a right and a left in the heavens (according to **Pythagoras**), there must also be found the other four parts of the other two dimensions: the head and feet for length, and the face and shoulders for depth. Aristotle says that our Pole is not the "right" of the heavens, nor is the other the "left," as Pythagoras claimed. This is because the difference and superiority of one over the other would not exist in the heavens themselves, but only in how they appear to us, or in relation to us. Perhaps in the other part unknown to us, more fixed stars are found in the sky and more inhabitants on the earth; in our own times, the experience of the **Portuguese** and **Spanish** navigations has demonstrated part of this to us. 

Therefore, he says that the **East** is the "right" of the heavens and the **West** is the "left." He posits that the entire body of the heavens is one animal, the head of which is the **Antarctic Pole** (hidden from us), and the feet are the **Arctic Pole** of the North. In this way, the right remains in the East and the left in the West. The **face** is that part which moves from East to West, and the **shoulders** (or the back) is that part which moves from West to East underneath. Thus, the whole universe is a man—or rather, an animal—containing both male and female; and the heavens being one of the two most perfectly...
