<meta>Continuing from the previous page, Philo has been explaining the macrocosmic structure of the universe as an "animal." He now expands on the gendered relationship between the heavens and the earth and addresses Sophia's confusion regarding directional orientations like "Right" and "Left" on a spherical globe.</meta>

with all its parts, certainly you can believe that it is the male—or the man—and that the earth, and the **prime matter** <term>Prime matter (materia prima): In Renaissance philosophy, this is the formless "stuff" of the universe that receives shape and life from the heavens.</term> with the elements, is the female. These two are always joined in matrimonial love—or rather, in the reciprocal affection of two true lovers—just as I have told you.

**SO.** I am pleased with what you told me regarding **Aristotle** <note>Aristotle's *De Caelo* (On the Heavens) is the primary source for the idea that the universe has directional parts.</note> and the "animal" nature of the heavens, as well as its six naturally different parts. In animals, as in plants, one indeed finds a difference between the head and the feet; in plants, the head is the root and the feet are the leaves, meaning that in this respect a plant is an animal turned upside down. While in an animal there is a distinction from top to bottom, one does not find in plants the differences of the other parts, for they have no face, nor shoulders, nor right, nor left. But as for this which Aristotle says—that the East is the **right** of the heavens and the West is the **left**—a doubt occurs to me. Neither the East nor the West is the same for all the inhabitants of the earth. In fact, our East is the West to others who live beneath us, who are called the **Antipodes** <term>Antipodes: People living on the opposite side of the Earth, whose feet would be "opposite" ours.</term>, and our West is the East to them. Since there are inhabitants all around the roundness of the earth from the rising to the setting of the sun, and the East of some is the West of others, which of these "Easts" shall be the one? Why choose one more than another? If every East is the "right" side, then the same place would be both right and left. Resolve this for me, as it seems quite doubtful.

**PH.** Your doubt, oh Sophia, is not very easy to resolve. Some say that the East which constitutes the "right" of the heavens is the East of those who live in the middle of the length of the world's habitable lands, from East to West. They believe this because they think only half the length of the world is inhabited—or rather, uncovered land—and that the other half is covered by water.

**SO.** Is this true, in your opinion?

**PH.** It is certainly not true, for we know that the greater part of the roundness of the earth from East to West is uncovered, and that every place has its own East. One East should not be more "right" than another, especially since what is East to one is West to another. In that way, the same East would be both right and left, as you said. Because of this, others say that the zodiac sign of **Aries** is the "right."

**SO.** You find good arguments for them; however, the Sun is not equally benevolent or a benefactor to all inhabitants of the earth when it is in Aries. Those in the other half of the earth, who live beyond the **equator** <note>original: "equinotiale"</note> and see the other, Antarctic Pole—who are called the **Antichthones** <term>Antitoni (Antichthones): A classical term for people living in the Southern Hemisphere, whose seasons are the reverse of those in the North.</term>—receive the benefit of Spring when the Sun is in **Libra**. This is because the Sun then begins to approach them, and they experience the loss of Autumn when the Sun is in...

<summary>Sophia challenges Aristotle's assertion that the "East" is the right side of the cosmic animal, noting that directions are relative on a spherical Earth. Philo explores different geographical and astrological theories to define a fixed "Right" for the universe.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, cosmology, Antipodes, Antichthones, East and West, Aries, Libra, Equator, microcosm, macrocosm</keywords>

--- Page 33 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page, Sophia is questioning how the "Right" and "Left" of the heavens can be fixed if different people on Earth experience East and West differently depending on their location.</meta>

**Aries** <note>The first sign of the Zodiac, used here as a reference point for the movement of the sun.</note>, which at that time moves away from them in the opposite way than it does from us; therefore, our right would be their left, and yet the right of the animal is the right for everyone, and likewise the left, and without doubt it is so. For I have already heard that those who inhabit the region beyond the **Torrid Zone** <term>Torrid Zone: The tropics. Ancient geographers believed this area was too hot for human habitation; Sophia is discussing people in the Southern Hemisphere.</term> have Spring when we do, and Autumn likewise. Therefore, I pray you, Philo, do not leave my doubt without a true solution, if you know one.

**PH.** Those who have commented on **Aristotle** have found no other ways to save his argument than these two; and because they recognized the weakness of these solutions, they took hold of the least inconvenient one they could find. You, Sophia, should be content with that which they (who knew more than you) were content with.

**SO.** I delight in my own satisfaction, and not in that of others. I see that you are even less satisfied with these solutions than I am. So that I may be at peace, you must either admit to me that your Aristotle has erred, or truly find—to give me more satisfaction—an answer to this.

**PH.** Since my mind is turned toward you, none of my thoughts can be denied to you. I understand Aristotle differently; he subtly declares the functions of these six parts, both in the heavens and in every perfect animal. He says that the "high"—or the true **head**, which is the beginning of the animal's length—is that part from which the power of motion depends, which comes from the head, or rather the **brain, nerves, and motive spirits** <note>In Renaissance physiology, "motive spirits" were thought to be subtle vapors that carried commands from the brain to the muscles to create movement.</note>. And the **right** is the part where that same motion begins, as is manifest in man; and the **face**, or rather the **front**, is that from which the motion of the right sets out. The other three parts are the opposites of these in such operations.

**SO.** I understand this; let us come to the doubt.

**PH.** Aristotle says that the **right** is that part from which the Sun and the other stars and planets rise—that is, the **East**. He says this is not appropriated to a part marked out materially, but to all parts potentially, insofar as they are the East. And they set out toward the West, and not the contrary, according to the daily motion of the heavens. But the erratic motion of the planets, which is from West to East, is a **leftward motion** and from the left side; it is like the imperfect and weak movement of the left hand in man. Just as the motion from East to West in any part of the sky is a right-handed motion and from the right side. Because the head of the heavens is the **Antarctic pole** and the feet are the **Arctic pole** <note>Aristotle argued that since motion begins in the "right," and the stars move from East to West, the "top" of the universe must be the South Pole to make that geometry work.</note>, as he says, it is necessary that as the whole sky moves always and in every part from East to West, that motion is from the right side, and the opposite is from the left. And the face remains that part which is between the East and the West.

with all its parts, you can certainly believe that it is the male—or the man—and that the earth and **prime matter** <term>Prime Matter: In ancient philosophy, the "first material" that has no qualities of its own but can become anything when combined with form.</term> along with the elements is the female. These two are always joined together in matrimonial love, or rather in the reciprocal affection of two true lovers, just as I have told you.

**SO.** I like what you have told me of **Aristotle** <note>original: "Aristotile"</note> regarding the animality of the heavens and its six naturally distinct parts. In animals, as in plants, one finds a difference between the head and the feet; in a plant, the "head" is the root and the "feet" are the leaves, so that in this respect it is an animal turned upside down. In the heavens, however, we do not find the distinctions of other parts, for they have no face, no shoulders, no right, and no left. Yet Aristotle says that the East is the right side of the heavens and the West is the left. A doubt occurs to me: East and West are not the same for all inhabitants of the earth. In fact, our East is the West to others who live beneath us—those called the **Antipodes** <term>Antipodes: People living on the opposite side of the globe, "feet-to-feet" with us.</term>—and our West is their East. Since there are inhabitants all around the roundness of the earth from the rising to the setting sun, and one man’s East is another’s West, which of these "Easts" would be the right side? Why would one be chosen over another? If every East is the "right," then the same place would be both right and left at once. Resolve this for me, as it seems quite doubtful.

**PH.** Your doubt, O Sophia, is not very easy to resolve. Some say that the "East" which is the right side of the heavens is the East of those who live in the middle of the inhabited world's length, from the rising to the setting sun. They believe this because they think only half of the world's length is inhabited or "discovered" land, and that the other half is covered by water.

**SO.** Is this true, according to you?

**PH.** Not at all, for we know that the greater part of the earth’s circumference from East to West is discovered, and every part has its own East. One should not be "more right" than another, especially since what is East to one is West to another. In this way, the same East would be both right and left, as you said. Because of this, some others say that the sign of **Aries** <note>The ram; the first sign of the zodiac.</note> is the right side.

**SO.** You will find them out in that as well! For the Sun is not equally benevolent or a benefactor to all inhabitants of the earth when it is in Aries. Those on the other half of the earth—who live beyond the **equinoctial line** <term>Equinoctial line: The Equator.</term> and see the other, Antarctic Pole, whom we call the **Antitoni** <note>Likely a reference to the "Antichthones," the legendary inhabitants of the Southern Hemisphere.</note>—receive the benefit of Spring when the Sun is in **Libra** <note>The scales; the zodiac sign opposite Aries.</note>. At that time, it begins to draw near to them, and they experience the decline of Autumn when the Sun is in...

Aries, which then moves away from them in the opposite direction to us; therefore, our right would be their left, and yet the right side of the animal is the right side for everyone, and likewise the left. And without a doubt, it is so, for I have heard that those who live beyond the **Torrid Zone** <term>Torrid Zone: The region of the Earth between the Tropics, once thought to be too hot for human habitation.</term> have Spring when we do, and Autumn likewise. Therefore, I pray you, O **Philo** <note>original: "Philone"</note>, do not leave my doubt without a true solution, if you know it.

**PH.** Those who have commented on **Aristotle** <note>original: "Aristotile"</note> have found no other way to justify his position than these two options; and because they recognized the weakness of such a solution, they settled for the least inconvenient one they could find. You, O **Sophia** <note>Sophia: The name means "Wisdom" in Greek; she is the student/lover in this philosophical dialogue.</note>, be content with what they—who knew more than you—were content with.

**SO.** I take pleasure in my own understanding and not in that of others, and I see that you are less satisfied with these solutions than I am. So that I may be at peace, you must either grant me that your Aristotle has erred, or truly find a response to this that gives me more satisfaction.

**PH.** Since my mind is devoted to you, none of my thoughts can be denied to you. I understand Aristotle differently; he subtly explains the functions of these six parts, both in the heavens and in every perfect animal. He says that the "up" or the head—which is the beginning of the animal’s length—is that part upon which the **power of motion** <note>original: "uirtu del' moto"</note> depends, which comes from the head, or the brain, nerves, and motor spirits. And the "right" is the part where that same motion begins, as is evident in man; and the face, or the front, is that toward which the motion of the right side is directed. The other three parts are the opposites of these in their operations.

**SO.** I understand this; let us come to the doubt.

**PH.** Aristotle says that the "right" is that part where the Sun and the other stars and planets rise—that is, the **East** <note>original: "Oriẽte"</note>. And he says this is not assigned to a materially marked part, but to all parts potentially, insofar as they are "East." And they set out toward the **West** <note>original: "Occidẽte"</note>, and not the contrary. According to the "wandering" motion of the planets, which is from West to East, that is a "leftward" motion, and from the left side; it is like the imperfect and weak movement of the left hand in man. Whereas the movement from East to West in any part of the heavens is a "rightward" motion and belongs to the right side. Because the "head" of the heavens is the **Antarctic Pole** <note>original: "polo Antartico"</note> and the "feet" are the **Arctic Pole** <note>original: "polo Artico"</note>, as he says, it must be that as the whole sky moves always and in every part from East to West, that motion belongs to the right side, and the opposite to the left. Thus, the "face" remains that part which is between East and West. Above...

<meta>Philo is continuing his explanation to Sophia regarding how the spatial directions (up/down, front/back, right/left) apply to the celestial spheres differently than they do to living animals.</meta>

...toward which the heaven travels; in its rightward motion, the "shoulders" are that part that remains behind the East, below which the East is divided just as the right hand is from the shoulders.

**SO.** I believe I understand you; and according to this, in the heavens only "above" and "below"—or rather the "head" and the "feet"—are materially divided, as one is one of the **Poles** <note>The North and South celestial poles, the fixed points around which the sky appears to rotate.</note> and the other is the other. The other four directions are divided "formally" by the path of motion. Is it so, **Philo** <note>original: "Philone"</note>?

**PH.** It is so, and you have understood it well. However, in animals, all six parts are materially divided and distinct.

**SO.** Tell me, why is there such great diversity between them?

**PH.** Because an animal moves in a straight line from one place to another, its parts of length and width are divided and distinct. But in the heaven—which moves with a circular motion within itself and always turns upon itself—it is necessary that these parts within it be materially one and the same as the other, and all contained in the whole. They are divided only by the path of motion. For this reason, the head and the feet of the heaven—which are the two Poles—never change into one another and are materially divided, just as they are in animals.

**SO.** If the East and West are the same material thing, does it follow that the right and left are also one and the same?

**PH.** It is not so. Even though a specific section of the sky is materially "East" to some and "West" to others, nevertheless, according to the motion of the whole heaven, every part is "East" to everyone when it finds itself at its rising point. Through the path of motion, it is always the "right" and never the "left," because the heaven never moves—nor do any of its parts—contrary to that rightward motion. This is unlike the **wandering planets** <note>original: "pianeti erratici"; the planets were called "wanderers" because they appear to move independently against the background of the fixed stars.</note>, whose motion is always "leftward." They move in reverse to work against the rightward celestial motion, in order to favor the opposites here below and to cause their continuous **generation** <term>Generation and Corruption: The Aristotelian cycle of birth (generation) and decay (corruption) that characterizes the physical world.</term>.

**SO.** I have understood, and I remain satisfied regarding my doubt. Yet, I would still like you to explain in what way the "philosophizers" say that a single man is a likeness of the whole universe—reflecting the lower world of birth and decay, as well as the celestial world, and the spiritual, angelic, or divine world.

**PH.** It seems you are diverting me somewhat from our current subject, which is the universality of love; but since this matter depends on it in every way, I will tell you briefly. All three of these modes you explained—the physical, the celestial, and the intellectual—are contained within man, as in a **Microcosm** <term>Microcosm: From the Greek for "small world"; the ancient philosophical idea that the human body and soul reflect the structure of the entire universe.</term>. These are found in him not only as distinct powers and operations, but also divided by his members, parts, and physical location.

<summary>Philo explains that while the sky's directions are defined by its circular motion, the human being serves as a "Microcosm" that mirrors the physical, celestial, and divine realms of the universe.</summary>

<meta>Philo is transitioning from the discussion of the celestial spheres to an explanation of the human body as a microcosm, mirroring the structure of the universe.</meta>

of the human body. **SO.** Teach me all three of them specifically.

**PH.** The human body is divided into three parts, according to the world, one above the other. Of the lowest part, its highest limit is a membrane, or *panniculus* <note>A thin layer of tissue or membrane</note>, which divides the body in half at the waist, called the **Diaphragm**, and it extends down to the legs. The second, higher part is above that membrane and reaches to the head. The third and highest part is in the head itself. 

That first part contains the members of nutrition and of **generation** <note>original: "gñratiõe"</note>: the stomach, liver, gall bladder, spleen, viscera, intestines, kidneys, testicles, and the penis. These parts in the human body are proportional to the "inferior world of generation" <note>The sublunary world where things are born and die</note> in the universe. Just as in that world the four elements—**Fire**, **Air**, **Water**, and **Earth**—are generated from primary matter, so in this part of the body the four **humors** <note>The four bodily fluids that were believed to determine health and temperament</note> are generated from food, which is the "primary matter" of the body. These are: **Choler**, hot, dry, and subtle, of the quality of fire; **Blood**, hot and moist, gently tempered, of the quality of air; **Phlegm**, cold and moist, of the quality of water; and the **Melancholic** humor, cold and dry, of the quality of earth. 

And just as from the four elements animals are generated which (besides nutrition and growth) have sense and motion; and plants which have no sense, but only nutrition and growth; and other mixtures deprived of a soul, without sense, motion, nutrition, or growth—which are like the dregs of the elements, namely stones, fungi, salts, and metals—so from these four humors generated in this first part of man, there are generated members from the humors that have nutrition, growth, sense, and motion, such as the nerves, membranes, fleshy muscles, and others; and those that have no sense or motion of their own, such as the bones, cartilage, and veins. 

Furthermore, from food and the humors other things are generated that have no sense, motion, nutrition, or growth, but are dregs and superfluities of the food and humors, such as hard excrement, urine, sweat, and the discharges of the nose and ears. And just as in the inferior world certain animals are generated from putrefactions <note>The historical belief in "spontaneous generation" from decaying matter</note>, many of which are poisonous, so too from the putrefaction of the humors are generated many kinds of worms, some of which are poisonous. 

And just as in the inferior world (ultimately through celestial participation) **Man** is generated, who is a spiritual animal, so from the best of the humors of the human body, subtle and purified **spirits** are generated. These are made through participation and restoration of the **vital spirits** <note>original: "spī uitali"; subtle substances thought to carry life and heat through the arteries</note> which always reside in the heart. These belong to the second part of the human body, which corresponds to the celestial world, as we shall discuss.

For this reason, the motion of the planets is "leftward" and they move backwards to work against the celestial rightward motion, in order to favor the inferior opposites and to cause their continuous **generation** <note>The process of birth and creation in the physical world.</note>. **SO.** I have understood you and am satisfied regarding my doubt. Yet I would still like you to explain how the philosophers say that a single man is a **simulacrum** <note>An image, likeness, or representation.</note> of the entire universe—not only of the "inferior world" of generation and corruption, but also of the celestial world, and of the spiritual, angelic, and divine worlds. **PH.** You seem to be diverting me from our main purpose, which is the universality of love; but because this has some dependency on that matter, I will tell you briefly. These levels are found in him not only different in power and operation, but also divided by members, parts, and locations.

of the human body. **SO.** Teach me all three of them in detail.

These belong to the second part of the human body, which corresponds to the celestial realm, as we shall explain.

<meta>Sofia and Philo are continuing their discussion of the "microcosm," the idea that the human body is a miniature reflection of the universe. Having previously discussed the lower organs (the digestive and reproductive systems) as a reflection of the "elemental world" of Earth, Water, Air, and Fire, Philo now moves upward to the chest.</meta>

**SO.** I have well understood the correspondence of the lower part of man to the lower world of generation and corruption; tell me now of the celestial part. 

**PH.** The second part of the human body contains those spiritual members that are above the diaphragm <term>diaframa: the muscular partition between the chest and abdomen</term>, up to the passage of the throat—that is, the heart and the two lungs, the right and the left. In the right lung, there are three distinct lobes <note>original: "particelle di Polmone"</note>, and in the left, there are two. This part corresponds to the celestial world. The Heart is the Eighth Starry Sphere <note>the realm of the fixed stars in ancient astronomy</note>, together with all the heavens above it, which is the Primum Mobile <term>First Mover: the outermost sphere that moves all others</term>, which moves everything. It always moves equally, uniformly, and circularly, and it sustains every corporeal thing in the universe with its continuous motion; every other continuous motion found in the Planets and Elements proceeds from it. 

In the same way, the heart in man always moves in a circular and uniform motion and never rests. With its motion, it sustains the whole human body in life and is the cause of the continuous motion of the lungs and of all the pulsing arteries in the body. In the heavens, many stars are found—bright, large, medium, and small—and many celestial figures <note>constellations</note> are connected to this heaven of the Primum Mobile. 

There are also the erratic planets <note>original: "pianeti erratici," literally "wandering stars," referring to the planets whose paths across the sky seem irregular compared to the stars</note>, which are called thus because they "wander" in their motion: sometimes they move forward, sometimes they seem to turn backward, sometimes they move quickly, and sometimes slowly, but all of them follow the Primum Mobile. So it is with the lungs, which follow the heart and serve it in its continuous motion. These lungs (being spongy) expand and contract, sometimes quickly and sometimes slowly, just like the erratic planets and their governing principles. 

In the government of the universe, there are two luminaries: the Sun and the Moon. Above, the Sun is accompanied by the three "superior" planets, namely Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn; and above, with the Moon, are two others, namely Venus and Mercury. In the same way, the right lung, being the more primary, is the image of the Sun, and therefore holds with it three distinct lobes that proceed from that same lung <note>representing the three superior planets</note>. The left lung (which signifies the Moon) holds two lobes <note>representing the two inferior planets</note>. All of these together make the number seven <note>representing the seven classical planets: Sun, Moon, Mars, Jupiter, Saturn, Venus, and Mercury</note>. 

And just as the celestial world, through its rays and continuous motion, contains this inferior world—sharing vital heat, spirituality, and motion with it—so this heart, together with the lungs, sustains the whole body through the arteries, which participate in its continuous motion. Thus, the similarity is perfect in every way. 

**SO.** I enjoy this correspondence of the heart and the spiritual members with the celestial world and its influences on the lower world. If you will now please me, tell me the...

<meta>Building on the previous discussion where the chest was likened to the celestial spheres, Philo now ascends to the head, the final part of the human microcosm, comparing it to the highest spiritual and divine realms of the universe. This concludes the anatomical-cosmological map before the speakers return to their main topic: the nature of love.</meta>

### The correspondence of the spiritual and intellectual world in the human body.

**PH.** The head of man (which is the superior part of his body) is an image of the spiritual world, which (according to the divine Plato, and not far from the view of Aristotle) has three levels: Soul, Intellect, and Divinity. The Soul is that from which celestial motion proceeds, and which is able to govern the nature of the lower world, just as nature governs the "first matter" <term>materia prima: the most basic, formless substance from which all physical things are made</term> within it. In man, this corresponds to the brain, with its two powers of sensation and voluntary motion. These are contained within the sensitive soul <note>original: "anima sensitiua"</note>, and they are proportionate to the Soul of the World, which provides for and moves all physical bodies.

Next, in man, there is the *possible intellect* <term>intelletto possibile: the potential of the human mind to receive and understand knowledge</term>, which is the ultimate human form; it corresponds to the Intellect of the Universe, in which all the angelic creatures reside. Finally, there is in man the *agent intellect* <term>intelletto agente: the active power of the mind that turns potential thought into actual understanding</term>. When the "possible" is joined with the "agent," it becomes actual and full of perfection and the grace of God, coupled with His sacred divinity. This is what corresponds in man to the Divine Principle, from which all things take their beginning, and toward which all things are directed and find rest, as their ultimate end.

This should suffice for you, O Sophia, in this familiar conversation of ours regarding the image of man in relation to the whole universe, and why he was rightly called the **Microcosm** <term>Microcosmos: from the Greek for "little world," the philosophical idea that the human being is a miniature reflection of the entire universe</term> by the ancients. There are many other specific similarities, but they would be long and outside our purpose. We shall make use of what we have said when we speak of the birth and origin of love; then you will understand that it is not in vain that the things of the world love one another—the high [loving] the low, and the low the high—since they are all parts of one body, corresponding to a single integrity and perfection.

**SO.** Our talk has carried us away and distanced us somewhat from our purpose; let us return now to our intent. If I have understood you well, O Philo, you have shown how much love the heavens have (after the manner of a generating man) for the earth and for the first matter of the elements, as for his own woman receiving his generation. There is no doubt, following this, that the heavens also have love for all things generated by the earth or by the first matter of the elements, as a father for his own children. This love manifests itself broadly in the care he takes in preserving them and in preparing their nourishment: producing rainwater for the nourishment of plants, plants for the nourishment of animals, and both for the nourishment and service of man, as his firstborn or his principal offspring. He changes the four [elements]...

<meta>Continuing from the previous page, Philo concludes his description of how the human body mirrors the divine and celestial worlds. ...seasons: Spring, Summer, Autumn, and Winter, for the birth and nourishment of all things, to temper the air for the needs of their life, and to balance their temperaments <term>complessioni: in Renaissance medicine, the balance of the four humors (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) that determined a person's health and character</term>. Furthermore, it is evident that created things love the heavens as a merciful and true father, seen in the joy that animals take in the light of the Sun and the coming of the day, and in the sadness and withdrawal they feel at the darkening of the sky with the arrival of night. 

I am certain you could tell me much more of this, but it is enough for me what you have said regarding the reciprocal love of the heavens and the earth—like husband and wife—and of the love each of them has toward created things—like the love of a father and mother toward their children—and likewise the love of those created things toward the earth and toward the heavens, as children toward their mother and father. But what I would like to know from you is whether the celestial bodies (beyond the love they have for the things of the lower world) love one another reciprocally. Since there is no reproduction <note>original: "generatione"</note> among them—which seems to me the most powerful cause of love among the things of the universe—it would seem that there should be no reciprocal love or mutual delight between them.

**PH.** Even though the celestial bodies lack neighboring and mutual reproduction, they do not lack perfect and reciprocal love. The principal reason that demonstrates their love is their friendship and the harmonious concord that is perpetually found in them; and you know that every concord proceeds from true friendship or true love. If you were to contemplate, O Sophia, the correspondence and the harmony of the motions of the celestial bodies—of those first ones that move from East <note>original: "Leuante"</note> to West <note>original: "Ponente"</note>, and those others that move in the opposite direction, from West to East—one with a very fast motion, another with less speed, some slow, and others very slow; sometimes they move in retrograde <term>retrogradi: the appearance of a planet moving backward in its orbit from the perspective of Earth</term>, and sometimes they stand as if still, in their station before moving forward or before moving backward; sometimes they deviate toward the North <note>original: "Settentrione"</note>, sometimes toward the South <note>original: "Mezzogiorno"</note>, sometimes they travel through the middle of the Zodiac, and one of them (which is the Sun) never departs from that straight path of the Zodiac, nor ever moves toward the North or the South, as all the other planets do. And if you knew the movement of the celestial Orbs (for which various motions are necessary), their measures, their forms, and positions, their poles, their epicycles <term>epicicli: small circles used in ancient astronomy to explain the complex orbital paths of planets</term>, and their centers—one ascending, another descending, one Oriental of the Sun, and the other Occidental.

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Philo and Sophia. Philo is transitioning from a scientific discussion on cosmic harmony to a defense of classical mythology. He explains that the "scandalous" stories of the gods told by poets contain layers of hidden meaning—literal, moral, and allegorical—using the myth of Perseus as a primary example.</meta>

...union; it is certain that the Poets have portrayed this lascivious love, as between male and female, some matrimonial and others adulterous, and they even present it as generating other Gods. These things are certainly very foreign to the nature of the celestial beings, but (as the common people say) the lies of Poets are many.

PHILO: The poets have not spoken vain things, nor lies in this matter, as you believe.

SOPHIA: How not? Would you ever believe such things of the celestial Gods?

PHILO: I believe it because I understand it; and you too, if you understand it, will believe it.

SOPHIA: Make me understand them, then, so that I may believe them.

PHILO: The ancient poets folded not just one, but many intentions into their poems, which they call "senses" <note>levels of meaning</note>. They place first of all the **literal sense**, like an outer rind <note>original: *scorza*</note>, the history of certain persons and their notable deeds, worthy of memory. Then, within that same fiction, they place—like a more intrinsic rind, closer to the marrow <note>original: *midolla*</note>—the **moral sense**, useful for the active life of men, approving virtuous acts and condemning vices. Beyond this, beneath those very words, they signify some true understanding of Natural, Celestial, Astrological, or even Theological things. And sometimes two—or even all three—of these scientific senses are included in the fable, like the marrows of the fruit within its rinds. These "marrowy" senses are called **allegory**.

SOPHIA: It seems to me no small artifice, nor of a meager intellect, to fold so many and such diverse and high meanings into a historical narrative, whether true or fictional. Therefore, I would like from you some brief example, so that it might be more believable to me.

PHILO: Believe, certainly, Sophia, that those ancients wished no less to exercise the mind in the artifice of signifying the matters of the sciences than in the true knowledge of them; and I will give you an example. Perseus, son of Jove <note>Jupiter</note> (by poetic fiction), killed the Gorgon <note>Medusa</note>, and as a victor, flew into the Ether, which is the highest part of heaven.

The **historical sense** is that this Perseus, son of Jove—either through the participation in the Jovian virtues <note>qualities associated with the planet Jupiter, such as justice or nobility</note> that were in him, or rather, by genealogy from one of those Kings of Crete, or Athens, or Arcadia, who were called "Jove"—killed the Gorgon, a tyrant on earth. And because he was virtuous, he was exalted by men even unto heaven.

It signifies also, **morally**, the prudent man, son of Jove, endowed with his virtues, who killed the low vice (which is the earthly thing signified by the Gorgon) and rose into the heaven of virtue. It also signifies **allegorically**, first, that the...

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Philo and Sophia regarding the allegorical meanings behind the loves of the gods. Philo is explaining that the "love" described by poets is not literal lust but a representation of the natural harmony and mutual dependence that exists between celestial bodies—stars and planets—which mirror the internal cooperation of the human body.</meta>

SOPHIA. In what way?

PHILO. The principal cause of the love found in celestial bodies is the conformity of nature, much like the harmony of the <term>complexions</term> <note>The "complexion" refers to the balance of the four humors in a person's temperament.</note> in men. Between the heavens, the planets, and the stars, there is such a conformity of nature and essence that their motions and actions correspond with such proportion that a single harmonic unity is made from diverse parts. Because of this, they appear more like the different members of an **organized body** <note>An "organism" or a body where every part has a specific function for the whole.</note> than separate, diverse bodies.

Just as from different voices—one high-pitched, the other deep—a single, entire song is generated that is sweet to the ear, and if one of those voices were missing, the entire song or harmony would be corrupted; so too, from these diverse bodies—differing in size and in heavy or light motion, yet possessing their own proportion and conformity—a harmonic proportion is composed. It is so singular and united that if even the smallest particle were missing, the whole would be dissolved.

Thus, this conformity of nature is the cause of love among celestial bodies, not only as if they were different persons, but as members of a single person. For just as the **Heart** loves the **Brain** <note>original: *celebro*</note> and the other members, providing them with life, natural heat, and spirits; and the Brain provides the others with nerves, sense, and motion; and the **Liver** provides blood and veins—all because of the love they have for one another and the love each has for the whole as its own part, which love exceeds all other love—so too do the parts of heaven love each other reciprocally. Through their natural conformity, and by concurring in a union of purpose and work, they serve one another and accommodate each other's needs in such a way that they form a perfectly organized celestial body.

Furthermore, there is in them the other cause proper to the love of men, which is for **virtue**. Since every celestial body possesses an excellent virtue <note>In this context, "virtue" refers to both moral excellence and inherent power or efficacy.</note> that is necessary for the being of the others, and for the whole of heaven and the universe, once that virtue is recognized by the others, they love them for it.

I also say they love each other for the benefit they provide—not a private or particular benefit toward one, but a universal benefit to the whole universe, for without it, everything would be destroyed. In this same way, virtuous men love one another: for the good they do in the universe, and not for a particular benefit like that found in useful things.

So, since the celestial bodies are the most perfect of <term>animals</term> <note>In Renaissance philosophy, the stars were considered "animals" in the literal sense of *animale*—beings possessed of a soul (*anima*) and life.</note>, one finds in them the two causes of love found in humans, who are the most perfect species of animals.

SOPHIA. Since there is, as you say, such efficacy of love among the celestial bodies, then that which the poets invent concerning the love of the celestial Gods must not be in vain—such as the loves of **Jupiter** and **Apollo**.

<summary>Philo explains that cosmic love is the result of natural harmony and mutual dependence among celestial bodies, comparing the universe to a single organism where parts like the heart and liver work together for the common good.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Philo and Sophia regarding the hidden meanings within poetic fables. Philo has just finished explaining that fables contain multiple layers of "senses" (literal, moral, and scientific). He is now applying these layers to a specific (though unnamed) myth—likely that of Perseus and the Gorgon—to demonstrate how one story can represent human, natural, and theological truths.</meta>

the human mind, daughter of Jove <note>original: "Gioue"—the Roman king of the gods, Jupiter.</note>, by killing and overcoming the earthliness of the Gorgonic nature, ascended to understand celestial, high, and eternal things; human perfection consists in these very speculations. This allegory is "natural," because man belongs to the order of natural things. It also signifies another **celestial allegory**. Nature, daughter of Jove, having caused mortality and corruption in the lower earthly bodies through its continuous motion—this celestial nature, the producer of corruptible things, by exempting itself from that mortality, flew on high and remained immortal. It signifies yet a third **Theological allegory**: that the Angelic nature, which is the daughter of Jove the supreme God, creator of all things, by killing and removing from itself corporeality and earthy matter (signified by the Gorgon), ascended to heaven. For the <term>intelligences</term> <note>In Renaissance philosophy, "intelligences" were non-physical beings or angels believed to guide the movement of the stars and planets.</note> deprived of body and matter are those that perpetually move the **celestial orbs**.

SOPHIA: It is a wonderful thing to be able to put so many senses, full of true knowledge <note>original: "scientia"—referring to systematic wisdom or science.</note>, each one more excellent than the last, into so few words of a historical act. But tell me, why do they not declare their doctrines more freely?

PHILO: They wished to say these things with such artifice and brevity for many reasons. First, because they deemed it hateful to nature and to divinity to manifest their excellent secrets to every man. In this, they were certainly right; for to declare true and profound knowledge too clearly is to invite those who are unfit for it, in whose minds it becomes spoiled and adulterated, just as good wine does in a foul vessel. From this adultery follows a universal corruption of doctrines among all men, and it becomes more corrupt at every hour, passing from one unfit wit to another. This infirmity comes from manifesting scientific things too much. In our own time, through the broad speech of the moderns, it has become so contagious that one can hardly find an intellectual <note>original: "intellettuale"—here meaning a pure or refined intellect.</note> that can be "drunk" and is not spoiled. But in ancient times, they included the secrets of intellectual cognition within **fabulous rinds** <note>original: "scorze fabuloße"—the outer "bark" or "skin" of a fictional story or fable.</note> with the greatest artifice, so that no one could enter within except a wit fit for divine and intellectual things, and a mind that would preserve true knowledge rather than corrupt it.

SOPHIA: I like this reason: that high and excellent things should be recommended to high and clear wits, so that they are not debased by those who are unfit. But tell me the other reasons for these **poetic fictions**.

PHILO: They did it for four other reasons. The first is for the sake of brevity, which in...

--- Page 49 ---
<meta>This page continues Philo’s explanation to Sophia regarding the four reasons why ancient poets and philosophers hid deep truths within myths and fables. Philo is mid-sentence, explaining the first reason: the use of brevity as a tool for memory.</meta>

<sig>C iij</sig>

...few words, they might fold in many meanings; which brevity is very useful for the preservation of things in the memory, especially when done with such artifice that, by remembering a strange event, they would remember all the doctrinal senses included within it under those words. The second [reason] is to mix the delightful historical and fabulous with the true intellectual, and the sweet with the difficult, so that, human fragility being first enticed by the pleasure and sweetness of the fable, the truth of knowledge <note>original: "scientia"—referring to systematic wisdom or organized knowledge.</note> might enter the mind with sagacity. As children are usually taught disciplinary and virtuous things by starting with the easiest ones—especially since they can all exist together, one in the bark and the other in the marrow <note>The "bark" (scorza) refers to the literal surface story, while the "marrow" (medolla) refers to the essential internal meaning.</note>, as is found in poetic fictions. The third is for the preservation of intellectual matters, so that they do not come to vary over the course of time in the diverse minds of men; because by placing such meanings under these stories, the terms of those meanings cannot be changed. And also, for greater preservation, they have expressed the story in weighty and strictly observed verses, so that they cannot easily be corrupted, because weighty meter cannot tolerate error <note>original: "uitio"—literally "vice" or "defect," here referring to a mistake in the poetic meter that would signal the text has been altered.</note>; thus, neither through the lack of talent of minds nor the corruption of scribes could they easily adulterate the knowledge. The fourth and last is so that, with one and the same food, they could give things of different flavors to different [and even] contrary people to eat; so that lower minds might only take from the poems the story with the ornament of the verse and its melody; others more elevated eat, and beyond this, the moral sense; then others even higher can eat the **Allegorical food**, not only of **natural Philosophy**, but also of **Astrology**, and of **Theology**. Another end is added to this: that these poems, being thus a common food for every sort of man, cause it to be perpetuated in the minds of the multitude; for there are few who taste very difficult things, and from the few, the memory can quickly be lost should an age occur that makes men deviate from doctrine—as we have seen in some nations and religions, like among the Greeks and the Arabs, who (having been most learned) have almost entirely lost their knowledge. And it was already so in Italy, in the time of the Goths <note>A reference to the "Dark Ages" following the fall of the Roman Empire, which Renaissance thinkers viewed as a period of intellectual decline.</note>, after which that little which exists at present was renewed. The remedy for this is the artifice of putting knowledge under fabulous and historical songs, which because of their delight and sweetness, pass and are always preserved in the mouths of the common people, of men, women, and children.

<summary>Philo explains four reasons for using allegorical fables: to aid memory through brevity, to entice learners with sweetness, to protect the text from corruption through poetic meter, and to provide "food" for all levels of intellect while ensuring survival during dark ages.</summary>
<keywords>allegory, memory, poetic meter, preservation of knowledge, Greeks, Arabs, Goths, levels of meaning, education</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Philo and Sophia. Philo is finishing an explanation of the myth of Perseus and the Gorgon, illustrating how one story contains multiple layers of meaning (natural, celestial, and theological) before moving on to discuss why ancient philosophers hid their wisdom in fables.</meta>

...human mind, daughter of Jove <note>Jupiter/Zeus</note>, by slaying and conquering the earthliness of the Gorgonic nature, ascended to understand celestial, high, and eternal things; in which contemplations human perfection consists. This allegory is natural, because man belongs to natural things. It also intends to signify another celestial allegory: Nature, daughter of Jove, having caused mortality and corruption in the lower earthly bodies through her continuous motion, this celestial nature—hunter of corruptible things—exempting herself from that mortality, flew on high and remained immortal. It further signifies a third, theological allegory: that the Angelic nature, which is the daughter of Jove—the supreme God, creator of all things—by slaying and removing from itself the corporality and earthly matter signified by the Gorgon, ascended to heaven. For those intelligences <note>term: "intelligences" refers to spiritual beings, like angels, believed to govern the movement of the stars and planets</note> deprived of body and matter are those that perpetually move the celestial orbs.

SO. It is a wonderful thing to be able to put into so few words of a historical act so many meanings full of true knowledge <note>original: "scientia"—organized, philosophical knowledge</note>, each more excellent than the last. But tell me, why do they not declare their doctrines more freely?

PH. They wished to say these things with such artifice and brevity for many reasons. First, because they considered it hateful to nature and to divinity to manifest their excellent secrets to every man. In this they were certainly right, because explaining true and profound knowledge too clearly is like inviting those unfit for it; in their minds, it becomes spoiled and adulterated, as good wine does in a foul vessel. From this "adultery" follows a universal corruption of doctrines among all men, and it becomes more corrupted every hour, passing from one unfit mind to another. This infirmity arises from manifesting scientific matters too openly. In our own time, this has happened because of the loose talk of the moderns, which is so contagious that one can hardly find an intellectual work that can be "drunk" and is not spoiled. But in ancient times, they enclosed the secrets of intellectual cognition within fabulous "barks" <note>scorze—the outer layer or literal story of a fable</note> with the greatest artifice, so that no one could enter within except a mind <note>ingegno—talent or innate ability</note> suited to divine and intellectual things, a mind that preserves true knowledge rather than corrupting it.

SO. I like this reason—that high and excellent things should be entrusted to high and clear minds, so that they are not debased by those who are unfit. But tell me the other reasons for these poetic fictions.

PH. They did it for four other reasons as well. The first is for the sake of brevity, which in—

<summary>Philo explains the three-fold allegory of the Perseus myth (natural, celestial, and theological) and argues that the ancients hid their wisdom in fables to prevent deep truths from being corrupted by the unrefined minds of the masses.</summary>
<keywords>allegory, Perseus, Gorgon, Jove, celestial orbs, intelligences, ancient vs modern philosophy, fables, intellectual elitism</keywords>

--- Page 51 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Philo and Sophia. Philo is explaining why ancient philosophers used the "artifice" of fables and poetry to hide deep truths, providing four specific reasons for this method of teaching.</meta>

...few words, they might enfold many maxims <note>original: "sententie"—meaning aphorisms or deep philosophical thoughts</note>. This brevity is very useful for preserving ideas in the memory, especially when crafted with such skill that by recalling a strange event, one also recalls all the doctrinal meanings hidden within it, beneath those words. 

The second reason was to mix the delightful style of the historian and the storyteller <note>original: "Storiografo & fabuloso"</note> with true intellectual thought, blending the pleasant with the difficult. In this way, human fragility is first enticed by the pleasure and ease of the fable, allowing the truth of the science <note>term: "science" (scientia) here refers to structured philosophical knowledge or a branch of learning</note> to enter sagaciously and immediately into the mind. This is how children are usually taught disciplined and virtuous things: by starting with the easiest parts, especially since both can exist together—one in the "bark" or skin and the other in the "marrow" <note>This is a classic medieval/Renaissance metaphor for the outer story and the inner meaning</note>, as is found in poetic fictions. 

The third reason is for the preservation of intellectual concepts, so that they do not change over time within the various minds of men. By placing such maxims within these stories, the terms cannot be altered. Furthermore, for greater preservation, they cast the story into powerful and highly disciplined verses, so they cannot easily be corrupted; for a weighted meter cannot endure a defect. Thus, neither the laziness of minds nor the corruption of scribes could easily adulterate the science. 

The fourth and final reason is so that with the same food, they could serve guests of different tastes. Lower minds might only take from the poems the story along with the ornament and melody of the verse; other more elevated minds eat of the moral sense; and others still higher can eat the allegorical food—not only of natural philosophy, but also of astrology and theology. 

Another purpose is joined to this: because these poems are common food for every sort of person, they are perpetuated in the minds of the masses. Very difficult things are tasted by few, and the memory of things held by few is quickly lost if an age occurs that turns men away from learning—as we have seen in some nations and religions, like the Greeks and the Arabs, who (having been most learned) have almost entirely lost their science. It was once so in Italy in the time of the Goths <note>Referring to the period after the fall of the Western Roman Empire</note>, though what little exists at present was later renewed. The remedy for this is the skill of placing the sciences beneath fabulous and historical songs. Because of their delight and sweetness, they are passed along and preserved forever in the mouths of the common people: men, women, and children alike.

->C iij<-

--- Page 52 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the "artifice" of ancient writing. Philo has just finished listing four reasons why philosophers used fables and poetry to hide and preserve their teachings. Sophia now asks about the specific stylistic choices of the two greatest Greek philosophers, Plato and Aristotle.</meta>

SO. I like all these reasons for poetic fictions. But tell me, since Plato and Aristotle are the princes of philosophers, why did one of them choose not to use verse (even if he did use fables) but only prose? And why did the other use neither verse nor fables, but instead a disciplined discourse?

PH. Small men never break the laws, but only the great ones. The divine Plato, wishing to expand knowledge <term>science (scientia): here meaning structured philosophical knowledge or a formal body of learning</term>, removed one lock from it, which is that of verse; however, he did not remove the other lock, that of the fable. Thus, he was the first to break part of the law made for the preservation of knowledge, but he left it closed in such a way with his fabulous style that it was enough to preserve it. Aristotle—more bold and eager for expansion—with a new and personal manner and style of speaking, wished to further remove the lock of the fable and entirely break the law of preservation. He spoke of the matters of Philosophy in a scientific style and in prose. Yet it is very true that he used such wonderful artifice in his speech—so brief, so comprehensive, and of such profound significance—that it sufficed for the preservation of the sciences in place of verse and fables. 

So much was this the case that, in responding to Alexander of Macedon <note>Alexander the Great</note>, his disciple, who had written to him saying he was marvelling that Aristotle had published such secret books of sacred Philosophy, he replied that his books were "published and not published" <note>original: "editi, & nō editi"</note> except to those who have understood them from him. From these words you will note, O Sophia, the difficulty and artifice that exists in Aristotle's way of speaking.

SO. I note it, but it seems strange to me that he would say that no one would understand them except those who have understood them from him. Many philosophers have lived since then, and yet they have all understood them, or at least the greater part of them. For this reason, this speech of his seems to me not only false but even arrogant. If these books of his are clear, they ought to be understood by good intellects, even if the readers were not present with him. After all, writing is not meant for writing to those who are present, but to those who are distant in time and absent from the author. Why could Nature not ensure that such talents could understand Aristotle through his writings, without having heard them from him?

PH. This saying of Aristotle would indeed be strange if he had no other intention.

SO. What other intention?

PH. He calls "his listener" the intellect that understands and philosophizes in the same manner as Aristotle’s own intellect, in whatever time or land it may be found. He means that his written words do not make every man a philosopher, but only those whose minds are disposed toward philosophical knowledge, just as his was. Such people will understand him, and the others will not,

*...as happens in that philosophy whose meaning remains enclosed under poetic fiction.* **SO.** According to this, Aristotle did no wrong in removing the difficulties of verse and fable, since he left the doctrine with so many other locks that it was enough for the preservation of knowledge <term>science (scientia): here meaning structured philosophical knowledge</term> within clear minds. **PH.** He did no wrong, because he remedied the situation with the greatness of his own genius; but he certainly gave audacity to others, who lacked such genius, to write philosophy in loose prose. By passing from one public manifestation to another and entering into minds that were not fit for it, this prose has been the cause of philosophy being falsified, corrupted, and ruined. **SO.** You have told me enough of this; let us return to the poetic loves of the celestial Gods. The Romans named him "Best and Greatest" <note>original: "Ottimo grande," referring to the title Jupiter Optimus Maximus</note> because every being and every good proceeds from him. The Greeks call him *Zeusis* <note>a variation of Zeus</note>, which means life, because from him all things have life—indeed, he is the life of every thing. It is very true that this name "Jupiter" was shared by the omnipotent God with some of his most excellent creatures. In the celestial world, this name was given to the second of the seven planets, called Jupiter, for being the "Greater Fortune" <note>a technical term in Renaissance astrology for the most beneficial planet</note>, possessing the clearest splendor and the best effects on the lower world. It is the planet that produces the best, most excellent, and most fortunate men through its constellation and influence. In the lower world, the elemental Fire is also called Jupiter, for being the clearest and most active of all the elements, and acting as the life of all lower things, which (according to Aristotle) live by heat. This name was also shared with men, specifically some most excellent ones who were greatly helpful to the human race. One such was Lisma of Arcadia <note>likely a reference to a mythical civilizing figure or a variant of Lycaon</note>, who went to Athens and, finding those people crude and of bestial customs, not only gave them human law but also showed them divine worship. Because of this, they took him for their King and worshipped him as a God, calling him Jupiter because he shared in those divine virtues. Similarly, there was the Cretan Jupiter, son of Saturn, who served those people by forbidding them from eating human flesh and other bestial rites, and by showing them human customs, and—

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the names and nature of the divine. Philo has just explained that the Greeks call the supreme God "Zeusis" (Life). He now transitions into how the name "Jupiter" was applied to various beings and how the poets categorized different classes of gods.</meta>

...divine knowledge, he was called Jupiter and worshiped as God, for being (in their opinion) sent from God and formed by Him, whom they called Jupiter. **SO.** Did the poets perhaps call this supreme God by any other name? **PH.** Properly speaking, they called him Demogorgon <note>original: "Demogorgone," likely derived by the author from the Greek *daimon* (spirit/god) and *georgos* (earth-worker) or *gorgos* (terrible)</note>, which in Greek means God of the earth—that is, of the universe—or else "terrible God" because he is greater than all. They say he is the producer of all things. **SO.** After the supreme God, what other gods do the poets posit? **PH.** First they posit the celestial gods, such as the Pole, the Heavens, the Ether, and the seven Planets: namely Saturn, Jupiter, Mars, Apollo (that is, the Sun), Venus, Mercury, and Diana (that is, the Moon), all of whom they call Gods and Goddesses. **SO.** For what reason do they apply divinity to corporeal things, such as these celestial bodies? **PH.** Because of their immortality, brilliance, and greatness, and because of their great power in the universe; and most especially because of the divinity of their souls, which are intellects separated from matter and corporeality, <term>forever pure in act (purissempre in atto): a philosophical term meaning these beings are always fully realizing their potential without change or decay</term>. **SO.** Did the name of "God" extend further among the ancients? **PH.** It descends into the lower world, for the poets call the elements, the seas, the rivers, and the great mountains of the lower world "Gods." They call the element of Fire "Jupiter," and that of Air "Juno"; the Water and the Sea "Neptune," and the Earth "Ceres"; the depths of the earth "Pluto," and the mixed fire burning within the earth "Vulcan." And thus many other Gods for the parts of the earth and the water. 

**SO.** This is very strange, that they should call "Gods" bodies that are neither living nor sensitive, even more so than the soul. **PH.** They call them Gods for their greatness, renown, work, and the primacy they hold in this lower world. Also, because they believed each of these was governed by a spiritual power shared from the intellectual divinity; or else (as <term>Plato</term> feels) that each of the elements has an incorporeal formal principle, through participation in which they have their own natures. These they call <term>Ideas (Idee): the perfect, non-physical forms or templates of all things in Platonic philosophy</term>. He holds that the Idea of Fire is true fire by formal essence, and the elemental fire is fire only by participation in that Idea; and likewise for the others. It is not strange, then, to attribute divinity to the Ideas of things. Hence they also placed divinity in plants, especially those that are the most common and useful foods for men, such as Ceres for Grain and Bacchus for Wine, because of the universal utility and necessity men have for them; for plants also have their own Ideas just as elements do. And for this same reason they also call the human Virtues, Vices, and Passions "Gods" and "Goddesses," because these (by their force) participate somewhat in divinity, yet...

--- Page 55 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Sophia and Philo regarding the names of God and the nature of divinity. Philo has been explaining how the ancients assigned divine names to celestial bodies and elements; he now moves into the metaphysical realm of Platonic "Ideas" to explain how virtues, vices, and even heroic men are linked to the divine.</meta>

The principal cause is that every one of the **Virtues**, every one of the **Vices**, and every one of the human **Passions**, in a universal sense, has its own proper **Idea** <term>Platonic Ideas: the perfect, eternal, and non-physical blueprints of all things that exist in the physical world</term>, by participation in which they are found in men to a greater or lesser degree, either *intensely* or *mildly*. For this reason, among the Gods, they are named **Fama** <note>Fame</note>, **Amor** <note>Love</note>, **Gratia** <note>Grace</note>, **Cupidità** <note>Cupidity or Desire</note>, **Volontà** <note>Will</note>, **Litigio** <note>Strife</note>, **Fadiga** <note>Toil</note>, **Invidia** <note>Envy</note>, **Fraude** <note>Fraud</note>, **Pertinacia** <note>Obstinacy</note>, **Miseria** <note>Misery</note>, and many others of this sort; because every one has its own proper Idea and incorporeal principle, as I have told you, by which it is named God or Goddess. 

**SO.** Even if the Virtues (due to their excellence) possessed Ideas, in what way can the Vices and evil Passions have them? 

**PH.** Just as among the celestial Gods there are some good and excellent fortunes, such as **Jupiter** and **Venus**, from whom many blessings always depend, and there are also some bad ones that are misfortunes, such as **Saturn** and **Mars**, from whom every evil proceeds; so too, among the **Platonic Ideas**, there are some principles of good and virtue, and others that are principles of evil and vice. This is because the universe needs both the one and the other for its conservation; according to which, both evil and good are necessary. For everything that is necessary for the existence of the universe is certainly good, since the essence of the universe is good. Thus, evil and corruption are as necessary to the existence of the world as good and generation, for the one disposes the other and is the way toward it. Do not wonder, then, if both the one and the other have a divine principle of an immaterial Idea. 

**SO.** I had understood that vices and evils consisted in **privation** <note>the philosophical concept that evil is not a positive force, but merely the absence or "lack" of a corresponding good</note> and depended on the defect of **prime matter** <note>materia prima: the most basic, formless substance that makes up the physical world</note> and its imperfect potential essence. How then do they have divine principles? 

**PH.** Even if it were so (according to the way of the **Peripatetics** <note>the "Peripatetics" were followers of Aristotle's philosophy</note>), it cannot be denied that the same matter is produced and ordered by the divine mind, and that all its effects and defects are directed by supreme wisdom, since they are necessary to the total essence of the lower world and to human being. Therefore, proper Ideas are assigned to them by God as their non-material principles—not as materials, but as active and formal causes—which cause the existence of these imperfect things, founded in privation and given being for the necessary existence of the whole universe. 

**SO.** I am satisfied with this; let us return to the subject. Tell me if the name of **God** (among the poets) is more communicable. 

**PH.** Finally, they have wished to communicate it particularly to men, but only to those who have possessed some **heroic virtue** and have performed acts similar to the divine, and great things worthy of eternal memory, like divine things. 

**SO.** So, by this similarity alone, do they give the name of God to mortal men?

<summary>Philo explains that both virtues and vices possess divine "Ideas" because both are necessary for the balance and conservation of the universe. He further notes that the title of "God" was historically granted to heroic men whose deeds mirrored the excellence of the divine.</summary>
