--- Page 58 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Sophia and Philo. Philo has been explaining that the "Gods" of the ancient poets are often allegories for Platonic Ideas—immaterial archetypes of virtues, vices, and natural forces. He now moves to explain how human "heroes" were elevated to the status of gods and how the genealogies and "loves" of the gods should be understood philosophically rather than literally.</meta>

**PH.** Insofar as they are mortal, they are not called Gods; but they are so called because of that part of them which is immortal, which is the **intellective soul** <term>Intellective soul: In Renaissance philosophy, the highest part of the human soul capable of reason and understanding divine truths.</term>.

**SO.** This soul is in all men, yet not all men are Gods.

**PH.** It is not equally excellent and divine in all. Rather, we know the rank of a man's soul through his actions. The souls of those who resemble the divine in their virtues and deeds participate actually in divinity; they are like rays of that divinity, which is why they are called "Gods" with some justification. Some of these men, because of their excellence, were even titled with the names of the **celestial Gods**, such as **Jupiter**, **Saturn**, **Apollo**, **Mars**, **Venus**, **Mercury**, **Diana**, **Heaven**, **Pole**, and **Aether**, as well as other names of the fixed stars and the constellations of the **eighth sphere** <note>In the ancient Ptolemaic model of the universe, the eighth sphere was the outermost shell of the heavens containing the fixed stars, situated beyond the seven planetary spheres.</note>. Others were called the children of these, such as **Hercules**, son of **Jove** <note>Jupiter</note>, and **Neptune**, son of **Saturn**. Others who were not quite so excellent are named with the names of the **lower Gods**, such as **Ocean**, **Earth**, **Ceres**, **Bacchus**, and the like. Alternatively, they were named as children of these: for some, the father was a God and the mother a Goddess; for others, the mother was not a Goddess; for others still, the father was a celestial God and the mother a lower Goddess. In this way, the **poetic fictions** <note>original: "figmenti poetici"</note> regarding the heroic men called "Gods" have multiplied. By narrating their lives, deeds, and history, the poets signify matters of **Moral Philosophy**. When they name them after virtues, vices, and passions, they signify matters of **Natural Philosophy**. When they name them after the lower gods of the world, or of generation and corruption, they demonstrate **Astrology**—the science of the heavens. And by naming them after the celestial Gods, they signify the **Theology** of God and the Angels. Thus, these fictions were ingenious and filled with wisdom through this varied naming of the Gods.

**SO.** I have heard enough regarding the nature of the **Pagan Gods** <note>original: "Dei gentili"</note> and their many names. Tell me now of their **loves**, which is our primary interest. How can one conceive of their generative propagation and successive genealogy as the poets describe? This seems absurd—not only regarding those heroes who are called Gods by **participation** <term>Participation: The philosophical concept that physical things "participate" in or reflect the perfect qualities of divine archetypes or "Ideas."</term>, but even more so regarding the celestial and lower Gods, where the lasciviousness, marriages, and offspring narrated of them seem quite out of place.

**PH.** It is indeed time to explain to you something of their loves and their generation. Know, O Sophia, that not every **generation** <note>In this context, "generation" refers to the act of bringing something into being or "birthing" a new state of existence.</note> is a carnal propagation or a lascivious act. That mode of generating belongs only to men and animals. Yet, generation is common to all things in the world, from the **First God** <note>The ultimate source or Creator</note> down to the very last thing.

<summary>Philo explains how ancient poets used the names of gods and heroes as allegories for different branches of philosophy—moral, natural, astrological, and theological. He begins to transition into explaining that divine "generation" and "love" are metaphysical processes rather than literal carnal acts.</summary>
<keywords>intellective soul, eighth sphere, poetic fictions, moral philosophy, natural philosophy, theology, pagan gods, generation, participation, celestial gods</keywords>

--- Page 59 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Philo and Sophia. Philo is transitioning from explaining how human heroes were deified to explaining the allegorical nature of the "loves" and "genealogies" of the primary gods, framing them as philosophical interactions between creative principles.</meta>

...of the universe, and the fact that He is only a generator and not generated, while all other things are all generated, and most are also generative. Furthermore, most generated things have two principles for their generation: one **formal** and the other **material** <term>Formal and Material principles: In Aristotelian philosophy, the "form" provides the essence or design of a thing, while "matter" is the physical substance that receives that form.</term>, or rather, one that gives and the other that receives. Wherefore the Poets call the formal principle the **giving father**, and the material principle the **receiving mother**. Because these two principles must concur in the generation of every generated thing, it was necessary for them to love and unite with one another through **Love**, in order to produce the offspring, just as the fathers and mothers of men and animals do.

When this conjunction of the two parents of the offspring is ordinary in nature, it is called (among the poets) "matrimonial," and one is called the husband and the other the wife; but when it is an extraordinary conjunction, it is called "amorous" or "adulterous," and the parents—or rather the generators—are called "lovers." So it is that you may accept the loves, marriages, generations, kinship, and genealogies among the superior and inferior Gods without wonder.

**SO.** I have understood you, and I like this universal foundation regarding the loves of the Gods; but I would like you to declare more particularly the infatuations of some of them—at least the most famous ones—and their generations. I would be pleased if you would begin with the generation of **Demogorgon** <note>Demogorgon: In Renaissance mythology, often considered the primordial creator god or the "soul of the world."</note>, whom you say is understood to be the highest and first God; for I have heard that he produced children in a strange way. Tell me, I pray, what you feel about this.

**PH.** I will tell you what I have heard regarding the generation of Demogorgon. The poet **Pronapides** <note>Pronapides: A legendary Athenian poet, often cited in the Renaissance as the teacher of Homer, though his "Protocosmos" is likely a literary invention or lost work.</note> says in his *Protocosmos* <note>original: "Protocosmo"; literally "First World"</note> that while Demogorgon was accompanied only by **Eternity** and **Chaos**, as he was resting in that Eternity of his, he felt a tumult in the belly of Chaos. From this belly emerged **Strife** <note>original: "Litigio"</note>, making a great noise; and with a foul and dishonest face, he wished to fly on high, but Demogorgon threw him down to the depths. 

While Chaos remained burdened by sweats and fiery sighs, Demogorgon did not pull back his hand until he had also drawn from its belly **Pan**, along with three sisters called the **Fates** <note>original: "Parche"</note>. Since Pan appeared more beautiful to Demogorgon than any other generated thing, he made him his steward and gave him his three sisters as *pedissequie*—that is, handmaidens and companions. Seeing that Chaos was freed of its heaviness, by the command of Demogorgon, he placed Pan upon his throne. This is the fable of Demogorgon, although **Homer**, in his *Iliad*, attributes the generation of Strife—or rather **Discord**—to **Jupiter** <note>original: "Gione"; likely a corruption of "Giove" or Jove</note>, because...

<summary>Philo explains that divine "loves" represent the union of formal and material principles required for creation. He begins recounting the myth of Demogorgon, who birthed Strife, Pan, and the Fates from the belly of Chaos.</summary>

<keywords>Demogorgon, Chaos, Pan, The Fates, Strife, Discord, Pronapides, formal principle, material principle, divine genealogy, allegory, Protocosmos</keywords>

--- Page 60 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Philo and Sophia regarding the allegorical origins of the universe. Philo has just explained the concepts of formal and material principles as "father" and "mother" figures in ancient mythology.</meta>

...the daughter of whom it is said (because she displeased Juno at the birth of Eurystheus and Hercules) that she was cast down from heaven to earth <note>This refers to Ate, the personification of Delusion or Folly, who was cast out of Olympus by Zeus for her interference in the birth of Hercules</note>. They say further that Demogorgon <note>Demogorgon: A primordial deity frequently cited by Renaissance writers as the first of the gods and the spirit of the earth</note> fathered Polos <note>original: "Polo" - representing the celestial pole or the heavens</note>, Python <note>original: "Phitone" - the earth-dragon or spirit of chaotic matter</note>, Earth, and Erebus <note>original: "Herebo" - the personification of deep darkness</note>.

**SO.** Tell me the meaning behind this mythical genealogy of Demogorgon.

**PH.** It signifies the production, or rather the generation, of all things by the supreme Creator God. They say that Eternity was His companion because He alone is the truly eternal one, since He is, was, and always will be the principle and cause of all things, without there being in Him any temporal succession. They also give Him Chaos as an eternal companion, which is (as Ovid declares) the common matter, mixed and confused, of all things <note>A reference to the opening of Ovid's *Metamorphoses*, describing the state of the universe before the world was ordered</note>. The ancients held this matter to be co-eternal with God, and from it (when it pleased Him) He generated all created things as their true father; thus, this common matter is the mother of everything generated. In this way, they posit only these two parents of all things as eternal and ungenerated—one the father and the other the mother. However, they held the father to be the principal cause, and Chaos to be the accessory and formative cause. This seems to be the same way Plato felt in the **Timaeus** <term>Timaeus: Plato's dialogue concerning the nature of the physical world and the "Demiurge" or craftsman who shaped it</term>, regarding the new generation of things by the supreme God, produced from eternal and confused matter.

But in this they might be criticized, because if God is the producer of all things, it follows that He must have also produced the matter from which they are generated. Yet it should be understood that they mean—by Chaos having been in God’s company in eternity—that it was produced by Him **from eternity** <note>original: "ab eterno"</note>, and that God produced all other things anew out of this Chaos at the beginning of time, according to the Platonic opinion. Matter is called a "companion" not just because Chaos was produced from eternity and is always found in God’s company, but because it is the Creator's partner in the creation and production of all things—His consort in their generation. And since matter alone was produced immediately by God, and all other things were produced by God and that Chaos (or matter), it can rightly be called God’s companion; yet this does not mean that it was not produced by God from eternity. Just as Eve, having been produced from Adam, was his companion and consort, and all other humans were afterwards born from both of them.

**SO.** It seems clear that in this fable they mean to signify the generation of the universe by almighty God as Father, and by His Chaos or matter as Mother. But tell me something of the meaning of the specific details...

<summary>Philo explains the myth of Demogorgon as an allegory for creation, identifying God as the eternal Father and Chaos as the material Mother of the universe. He reconciles the idea of "co-eternal" matter by suggesting it was produced by God from eternity, using the biblical analogy of Adam and Eve.</summary>

<keywords>Demogorgon, creation, Chaos, Plato's Timaeus, Ovid, primary matter, eternal generation, Adam and Eve allegory, cosmogony, divine principles</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Philo and Sophia. Philo is interpreting the mythological origins of the universe, specifically the role of Demogorgon and Chaos as the primordial "parents" of all things. In the previous section, he identified God as the father and primal matter (Chaos) as the mother.</meta>

...of the fable, that is, of the tumult in the belly of Chaos, of the hand of Demogorgon, of the birth of Strife, and of the others. 

**PH. IL.** The tumult that Demogorgon felt in the belly of Chaos is the power of confused matter toward the germination of divided things, which division causes, and is accustomed to cause, tumult. The stretching out of the hand of Demogorgon to open the belly of Chaos is the divine power that wished to bring the universal potential of Chaos into a state of divided act. For this is what it means to open the belly of a pregnant woman: to draw out what is hidden within. And they have imagined this extraordinary way of generating—with the hand and not with the ordinary generative organ—to demonstrate that the first production or creation of things was not ordinary, like the natural generation that is usual and successive after the creation, but was strange and miraculous, by the hand of all-power. 

He says that the first thing to emerge from Chaos was Strife <note>original: "il Litigio" — here personified as the first-born of Chaos, representing the initial separation of elements</note>, because the first thing to emerge from primary matter was the division of things, which had been undivided within it; and in its birth, by the hand or power of the father Demogorgon, they were divided. He calls this division "Strife" because it consists of contrariety—that is, among the four <term>Elements</term> <note>The four classical elements: Earth, Water, Air, and Fire</note>, where one is the contrary of the other—and he gives it an ugly face because, in effect, division is contrariety and defect, just as union is concord and perfection. 

He says that Strife wished to ascend to heaven and was cast down from heaven to earth by Demogorgon, because in heaven there is no discord nor any contrariety, according to the <term>Peripatetics</term> <note>Peripatetics: Followers of Aristotle's philosophy, who taught that the heavens were made of an incorruptible fifth element (the quintessence), unlike the changing earthly realm</note>. Therefore, celestial bodies are not subject to <term>corruption</term> <note>corruption: in Renaissance philosophy, this refers to the process of decay, change, or the breaking down of matter</note>, but only the lower bodies are, because discord and contrariety exist among them; for contrariety is the cause of corruption. And by being cast from heaven to earth, it is understood that heaven is the cause of all lower contrarieties, while it remains without any contrariety itself. 

**SOPHIA.** How then can it cause it? 

**PHILONE.** Through the contrariety of the effects of planets, stars, and celestial signs, and through the contrariety of celestial motions—one from East to West, the other from West to East; one toward the North <note>original: "Settentrione"</note>, the other toward the South <note>original: "Mezzogiorno"</note>—and also through the contrariety of the position of the lower bodies placed within the sphere of the Moon. Those closest to the circumference of the heavens are light, and those far away, approaching the center, are heavy. Upon this contrariety depends every other contrariety of the elements. It could also signify that ancient and <term>Platonic</term> opinion that the stars and planets are made of fire, because the...

<summary>Philo interprets the birth of "Strife" as the initial division of primordial matter into the four elements. He explains the Aristotelian view that while the heavens are stable and perfect, their conflicting motions and influences cause the constant change and "corruption" observed in the earthly realm.</summary>

<keywords>Demogorgon, Chaos, Strife, Elements, Aristotelianism, Peripatetics, Celestial Motion, Corruption, Primordial Matter, Platonism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Philo and Sophia. Philo is explaining the mythological and etymological origins of the universe. He has just finished describing how Strife (division) was the first thing born from Chaos, and is now explaining the elemental composition of the heavens and the subsequent birth of Pan and the Fates.</meta>

...its brightness, and the rest of the celestial body of water, through its translucency and transparency. Hence the Hebrew name for the heavens, which is *Shamayim* <note>original: "Samaini"</note>, is interpreted as *Esh-mayim* <note>original: "Esmaini"</note>, which means, in the Hebrew language, fire and water <note>A traditional Hebrew folk etymology: *Esh* (fire) and *Mayim* (water) combined to form *Shamayim* (heavens)</note>. According to this, Strife <note>original: "il Litigio"</note> and contrariety (in the first creation) ascended to heaven, because they are made of fire and water; but they did not remain there subsequently; rather, they were cast down from heaven to dwell continually on earth, where successive generation occurs through constant contrariety.

**SO.** It seems strange to me that in heaven there should be contrary elemental natures, such as Fire and Water.

**PH.** If the <term>prime matter</term> <note>The fundamental, formless "stuff" of the universe before it is given specific shape</note> is common to both the lower and the celestial realms—as these thinkers and Plato also believe—it is not strange that some elemental contrariety should still be found in heaven.

**SO.** How is it then that it does not decay, as lower bodies do?

**PH.** He says that the heavens in themselves are still corruptible, but divine power makes them indissoluble; by this, he means the intellectual forms in act that give them shape. Furthermore, because these celestial elements are purer—almost like the souls of the lower elements—they are not mixed in heaven as they are in lower mixtures; Fire exists only in the bright parts, and Water only in the transparent parts, so that although Strife indeed ascended to heaven at the beginning of the production from the belly of Chaos, it was afterwards cast into the lower world, where its dwelling is today. 

Hence the fable continues that, while Chaos was burdened during this birth of Strife with sweat and fiery sighs, the hand of Demogorgon persisted and drew from its belly Pan with the three sisters, the Fates <note>original: "Parche"</note>. By those labors in the birth of Strife, he means the substances of the four contrary Elements: by the burden, he means Earth, which is the heaviest; by the sweat, Water; and by the fiery sighs, he means Air and Fire. And as a cause and remedy for the exhaustion caused by these contraries, divine power produced from Chaos the second son, Pan, which in Greek means "all." By him, he means universal nature, the orderer of all things produced from Chaos, and she who pacifies the contraries and accords them together; thus Pan was born after Strife, for concord succeeds discord and follows after it. 

He also produced the three sisters, the Fates <note>original: "Parche"</note>, called Clotho, Lachesis, and Atropos, whom Seneca calls the Destinies <note>original: "Fate"</note>. By them, he means the orders of temporal things—the Present, the Future, and the Past—which he says God made followers and handmaidens <note>original: "pidisseque"</note> of universal nature. For Clotho is interpreted as "calling," and it is the calling...

<summary>Philo explains how the heavens are composed of "fire and water" (Shamayim) and describes the birth of Pan (Universal Nature) and the three Fates from Chaos to bring order and time to the initial elemental discord.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Philo and Sophia. of present things, and she is the Fate who twists the thread that is being spun in the present <note>Philo is describing Clotho, the first of the three Fates, though her name likely appeared on the previous line.</note>. Lachesis is interpreted as "lot" <note>original: "prattione," likely a reference to the Greek *lēxis* or the acting out of one's destiny</note>, and she is the Fate who extends that thread which remains to be spun from the distaff. Atropos is interpreted as "without return," representing the past which cannot be undone, and she is the Fate who has finished spinning the thread already gathered on the spindle. They are called the <term>Parcae</term> <note>The Roman name for the Fates</note> by way of contradiction, because they "spare" <note>original: "perdonano." Afterward, he continues with the lineage of Demogorgon, describing a sixth son named Pole, which is the final sphere that revolves upon the two poles, the Arctic and the Antarctic; and a seventh named Python, which is the Sun; and an eighth who was female, namely the Earth, who is the center of the world. This Earth gave birth to Night, because the shadow of the Earth causes the night; furthermore, by "Night," he understands the corruption and privation of luminous forms, which proceeds from dark matter. 

They say that Fame was the second daughter of the Earth, because the Earth preserves the fame of mortals after they are buried within her. Her third son, they say, was Tartarus—that is, Hell—because all generated bodies return to the lower belly of the earth. They say the Earth gave birth to these children and others without a father because these things are defects and privations of being, which depend upon "rude" <note>crude or unformed</note> matter rather than any specific form. 

The ninth and final son of Demogorgon was Erebus, which means "inherence"—that is, the natural power inherent in all lower things. In the lower world, this is the matter of things capable of being generated, and it is the cause of the generation, corruption, and every variation and change in lower bodies. In man (who is called the <term>microcosm</term> <note>original: "mondo piccolo" or "little world," a common Renaissance idea that the human being mirrors the structure of the entire universe</note>), it is the appetite and desire for the acquisition of all new things. Whence they say that Erebus fathered many children, namely: *Love, Grace, Toil, Envy, Fear, Deceit, Fraud, Stubbornness, Poverty, Misery, Hunger, Lamentation, Disease, Old Age, Paleness, Darkness, Sleep, Death, Charon, Day, and Ether*. 

**SO.** Who was the mother of so many children? 

**PH.** Night, the daughter of the Earth, with whom Erebus fathered all these children. 

**SO.** Why do they attribute them all to Erebus and Night? 

**PH.** Because all of these arise from inherent power and from nocturnal privations, as much in the great lower world <note>the Macrocosm</note> as in the small human world <note>the Microcosm</note>. 

**SO.** Tell me how? 

**PH. Love**, which is to say Desire, is generated by...

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Philo and Sophia regarding the allegorical genealogy of the gods. Having previously discussed the Earth and Night, Philo now defines the various emotional and physical states born from the lack of form in matter, transitioning into an interpretation of Day, Ether, and the physical appearance of the god Pan.</meta>

inherent potential, and of the lack or deficiency, because matter (as the <term>Philosopher</term><note>A standard reference to Aristotle, who discussed the "appetite" of matter for form in his *Physics*.</note> says) desires all those forms of which it is deprived. **Grace** is the quality of the desired or loved thing which persists in the desiring mind or in the appetitive power. **Fatigue** represents the anxieties and labors of the seeker to reach the thing they desire. **Envy** is the fear of losing what has been newly acquired—because every acquisition is subject to loss—or it is the fear of not being able to acquire what one desires. **Deceit** and **Fraud** are the means used to acquire desired things. **Persistence** is what is employed in pursuing them. **Poverty**, **Misery**, and **Hunger** are the deficiencies of those who desire. **Complaint** refers to their laments when they cannot have what they desire, or when they lose what they have acquired. **Illness**, **Old Age**, and **Pallor** are the conditions leading to the loss and corruption of acquired things, generated by the will and the generative power. And **Obscurity** and **Sleep** are the first stages of loss. And **Death** is the final corruption. <term>Charon</term><note>The ferryman of the underworld in Greek mythology.</note> is the oblivion that follows the corruption and loss of the acquired thing. **Day**<note>original: "Die," from the Latin *Dies*.</note> is the luminous form which the inherent material potential—that is, the human intellect—can reach. In man, it represents luminous virtue and wisdom, toward which the will and the desire of the perfected are directed. **Ether** is the intellectual celestial spirit, which is the highest degree that material potential and the human will can participate in. Furthermore, these two children of <term>Erebus</term><note>The personification of darkness.</note>, **Day** and **Ether**, could signify the two natures of heaven: the brightness of the stars and planets, which he calls Day, and the transparency of the celestial sphere, which he calls Ether.

**SO.** What do these celestial natures have to do with Erebus, who is the matter of things that are generated and corrupted, and how can they be his children?

**PH.** Because many of the ancients, and <term>Plato</term> with them, affirm that these celestial natures are made of the same matter as the lower bodies; thus, they come to be the most excellent children of Erebus.

**SO.** What you have said briefly regarding the generation of <term>Demogorgon</term><note>The primordial creator god in this allegorical system.</note> is sufficient for me; I only lack an understanding of matters pertaining to love, such as the infatuation of **Pan**—the second son of Demogorgon—with the nymph **Syrinx**.

**PH.** The poets depict the god Pan with two horns on his head reaching toward heaven, with a fiery face, and a long beard that hangs down over his chest. He holds in his hand a wand or a <term>pipe</term><note>original: "fistula," the pan-flute or syrinx.</note> with seven reeds; he wears a skin spotted with diverse marks, and his lower members are harsh and coarse,

<summary>Philo explains how human emotions and physical states like envy, fatigue, and death are allegorical offspring of matter's deficiency, while Day and Ether represent intellectual light. He then begins to describe the physical iconography of the god Pan as an allegory for nature.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Sophia and Philo. Philo is transitioning from a description of the physical traits of the god Pan into a deeper allegorical explanation of his mythology, specifically his pursuit of the nymph Syrinx.</meta>

...and the goat-like feet. They say that when Pan came into conflict with **Cupid** and was defeated by him, he was forced to love Syrinx, a virgin <term>nymph</term><note>A minor nature deity in Greek mythology, often associated with specific locations like forests or rivers.</note> of Arcadia. While Pan was pursuing her and she was fleeing, she was blocked by the river Ladon; she asked the other nymphs for help and was transformed into <term>reeds</term><note>original: "calami," referring to hollow marsh reeds.</note> or marsh canes. Hearing the sound the wind made as it struck those reeds, Pan felt such a sweetness of harmony that, for the delight of the sound and for the love of the Nymph, he took seven of those reeds and joined them together with wax, creating the <term>pan-flute</term><note>original: "fistula," the multi-piped musical instrument synonymous with the god Pan.</note>, a sweet instrument to play.

SOPHIA. I would like to know from you if the poets have signified any <term>allegory</term><note>A symbolic narrative where characters and events represent deeper moral or spiritual meanings.</note> in this.

PHILO. Beyond the historical sense of a <term>Silvanus</term><note>A Roman forest deity, here humanized as a historical inventor from Arcadia.</note> of Arcadia who, being in love, devoted himself to music and was the inventor of the pan-flute with seven reeds joined together with wax, there is no doubt that it has another high and allegorical sense. That is, Pan—which in Greek means "all"—is <term>universal nature</term>, the orderer of all worldly things. The two horns he has on his forehead, which extend up to the sky, are the two <term>Poles</term> of the heavens, the <term>Arctic</term> and the <term>Antarctic</term>. The spotted skin he wears is the eighth sphere<note>In the ancient Ptolemaic system, the eighth sphere contained the "fixed stars," beyond the spheres of the seven planets.</note> full of stars. The fiery face is the **Sun** with the other planets, which are seven in total, just as there are seven organs in the face: two eyes, two ears, two nostrils, and the mouth, which (as we said above) signify the seven planets. The hair and the long beard hanging over his chest are the rays of the **Sun** and the other planets and stars that hang into the inferior world to bring about every <term>generation</term><note>The process of coming into being or biological reproduction.</note> and <term>mixture</term><note>The blending of the four elements to create complex physical matter.</note>. The lower and rough limbs are the <term>elements</term> and the inferior bodies, full of density and roughness compared to the celestial ones. Among these limbs, the feet are goat-like because the feet of goats never walk in a straight path but go crossing and jumping in an irregular way. Such are the feet of the inferior world and its motions and transformations from one essence into another, transversally and without a certain order; the celestial bodies are free from these coarsenesses and irregularities. This is the meaning of the figure of Pan.

SOPHIA. I like this, but tell me further the meaning of his love for Syrinx, which is more to our purpose.

PHILO. They also say that this universal nature—so great, powerful, excellent, and admirable—cannot be devoid of love; and therefore, it loved the pure and incorrupt virgin—that is, the stable and incorruptible order of worldly things—because nature loves the best and the most perfect. While following it, it fled, because the inferior world is entirely unstable...

<summary>Philo explains the myth of Pan and Syrinx as an allegory for Universal Nature, where Pan's physical features represent the cosmos, the stars, and the elements.</summary>
<keywords>Pan, Syrinx, allegory, Universal Nature, celestial spheres, astrology, elements, mythology, Arcadia, Cupid</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Philo and Sophia, moving from the physical description of Pan to the cosmological interpretation of his pursuit of the nymph Syrinx and the origin of the celestial gods.</meta>

...and always inordinately mutable, with goat-like feet; the flight of this virgin made the river Ladon cease. This signifies the Heavens, which run continually like a river, and in which is held the incorruptible yet fugitive stability of the <term>generable bodies</term><note>Bodies capable of being born, changing, and decaying, as opposed to the eternal stars.</note> of the lower world. Even though the heavens are not without continuous instability due to their constant local motion, this instability is ordered and everlasting—a virgin without corruption. Its "deformities" exist with an ordered and harmonic correspondence, as we said above regarding celestial music and melody. These are the <term>reeds</term><note>original: "calami"</note> of the river into which Syrinx was converted, and in them the spirit generates a sweet sound and harmony, because the intellectual spirit that moves the heavens causes its consonant musical correspondence. From these reeds, Pan made the <term>pan-flute</term><note>original: "fistula"</note> with seven of them, which signifies the gathering of the spheres of the <term>seven planets</term><note>In traditional astronomy: the Moon, Mercury, Venus, Sun, Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn.</note> and their wonderful harmonic accords. For this reason, they say Pan carries the wand and the flute, which he plays constantly, since nature continuously makes use of the ordered mutation of the seven planets for the constant changes of the lower world. See, O Sophia, how briefly I have told you the substance of the love of Pan for Syrinx.

SOPHIA. I enjoy the story of Pan’s falling in love with Syrinx; I would now like to know the lineages, marriages, adulteries, and loves of the other celestial Gods, and what their allegories are.

PHILO. I will tell you some part of those briefly, for to tell the whole would be a long and tedious matter. The origin of the celestial Gods comes from <term>Demogorgon</term><note>A primordial creator deity in Renaissance mythology, often depicted as the grandfather of all gods.</note>, from his two grandsons, the sons of <term>Erebus</term><note>The personification of darkness.</note>—or, as others would have it, his own sons—namely from <term>Aether</term><note>original: "Eter," representing the upper atmosphere or pure light.</note> and from **Day**<note>original: "Die"</note> his sister and wife. They say that from these two was born **Caelus**<note>original: "Celio," the personification of the Sky.</note>, or the Heavens. By this name, the <term>Gentiles</term><note>The non-Christian Greeks and Romans.</note> named **Uranus**, the father of Saturn, because he was so excellent in virtue and of such profound genius that he seemed celestial and worthy of being the son of Aether and Day; for he participated in ethereal spirituality in his intellect and divine light in his virtue. The allegory of this is quite manifest: for the sky, which surrounds, conceals, and covers all things, is the son of Aether and Day because it is composed of an ethereal nature in its subtle, even spiritual, transparency, and of a lucid and divine nature because of the luminous stars it possesses. Aether is called the "father" because it is the principal part of the heavens and because of its greatness, which encompasses all the spheres and (according to the mind of **Plato**) penetrates the entire universe. Plato posits that the universe is full of the ethereal spirit, but that the lucid bodies are particular members of the heavens—just as the woman is a part of the man, who is the whole—and also because Aether is a body more subtle and spiritual than the lucid bodies...

<summary>Philo explains the allegory of Pan's flute as the harmony of the seven planetary spheres and begins a genealogy of the gods, identifying Caelus (Uranus) as the offspring of Aether and Day.</summary>
<keywords>Pan, Syrinx, seven planets, celestial harmony, Demogorgon, Aether, Day, Caelus, Uranus, Plato, cosmology, allegory</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Philo and Sophia, moving from the mythology of Pan to the physical and allegorical nature of the heavens and the planet Saturn.</meta>

D ij
<meta>catchword: Saturn</meta>

of, of stars and planets. Wherefore Aristotle says (because the stars are of a thicker and denser substance than the rest of the heaven) that they are capable of receiving and retaining light within themselves, which the <term>Orb</term><note>The celestial sphere or the body of the heaven itself.</note> cannot do, because of its transparent thinness; and Plotinus holds that the thinness of the <term>Ether</term><note>The "fifth element" that makes up the heavens, thought to be purer than the four earthly elements.</note> is so great that it penetrates all the bodies of the universe, both superior and inferior, and that it remains with them in their places without any increase of space, since it is the interior sustaining spirit of all bodies, without enlarging its own physical mass. Hence the Ether has the property of a spiritual husband, and the Earth that of a more material wife, from which two natures the heaven is produced and composed.

SOPHIA. And from Heaven, who was born?
PHILO. Saturn.
SOPHIA. And who was the mother?
PHILO. Saturn, King of Crete, was the son of <term>Uranus</term><note>The personification of the Sky or Heaven.</note> and Vesta; and since this Uranus, because of his excellence, was called Heaven, Vesta his wife was called Earth, for being so generative of so many children, and especially for Saturn, who was inclined toward earthly things and was the inventor of many useful things in agriculture. Furthermore, this Saturn was of a slow and melancholic nature in the manner of the earth; and allegorically the earth, as I told you, is the wife of Heaven, in which all things of the lower world are generated.

SOPHIA. Since Saturn is a planet, how can he be the son of the earth?
PHILO. In one sense he is the son of Heaven, because he is the first planet and the one closest to the <term>starry sky</term><note>The Firmament or the sphere of the fixed stars, which sits above the planetary spheres.</note>—which is called "Heaven" in an absolute sense—and like a father he surrounds all the planets; yet this Saturn has many similarities to the earth. First in his leaden color, which tends toward the earthy; then because among all the <term>erratic planets</term><note>The "wandering stars" (planets) as opposed to the "fixed stars."</note> he is the slowest in his motion, just as the earth among all the elements is the heaviest. Saturn takes thirty years to revolve through his heaven; and Jupiter, being the next slowest of the others, takes twelve years; and Mars takes two years; and the Sun, Venus, and Mercury take one year; and the Moon takes one month.

Beyond this, Saturn resembles the earth in the <term>complexion</term><note>The combination of qualities—in this case, cold and dry—that determines a person's temperament and physical nature.</note> that he influences, which is cold and dry like the earth; he makes the men over whom he rules melancholic, sorrowful, grave, and slow, and of an earthy color, inclined toward agriculture, buildings, and earthly duties; and this planet also rules all these earthly things. He is depicted as old, sorrowful, ugly in appearance, pensive, poorly dressed, with a scythe in his hand, because he makes such men as are ruled by him, and the scythe is the instrument of agriculture, toward which he makes them inclined. In addition to this, he gives great intellect, profound thought, true science, right counsel, and constancy of soul, through the mixing of the nature of the celestial father with the earthly mother; and finally, from the father's side comes the divinity of the soul, and from the mother the ugliness and ruin of the body; and for this reason he signifies poverty, deaths, burials, and things hidden under the earth: without appearance or bodily ornament; wherefore they feign that Saturn...

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Philo and Sophia regarding the allegorical and astrological nature of the planets. It follows the discussion of Saturn's parentage (Heaven and Earth) and his association with agriculture and melancholy.</meta>

...he consumed all his male children, but not the females, because he [Time] destroys every individual being but preserves the "mother roots" <note>The author is explaining that while individual things die over time, the "roots" or species of things are preserved by the generative power of nature.</note> of the species; thus, he was rightly called the son of Heaven and Earth.

SOPHIA. And who was the son of Saturn?

PHILO. The poets assign many sons and daughters to Saturn, such as <term>Cronos</term><note>Philo follows the common Renaissance practice of conflating the Greek Titan Cronus with the word "Chronos," meaning Time.</note>, which means "determined time" or a temporal circuit. Another is the Year, which is the time of the Sun’s circuit; they say the Year is the son of Saturn because the longest temporal circuit that a man can see in his life—and that lasts the greatest amount of time—is the circuit of Saturn, which (as I told you) is completed in thirty years, while those of the other planets are completed in a shorter time.

SOPHIA. Who was the wife of Saturn and mother of Cronos?

PHILO. His wife, mother of Cronos and the other children, was Ops, his own sister, daughter of Heaven her father and Earth her mother.

SOPHIA. Do they perhaps mean something else by Ops other than the actual wife of Saturn, King of Crete?

PHILO. The allegory is that <term>Ops</term><note>Derived from the Latin "ops" meaning wealth, resources, or "opus" meaning work.</note> means "work," and signifies the labor of the earth, both in agriculture and in the building of cities and dwellings. She is, with good reason, the sister and wife of Saturn. She is his sister because she is the daughter of Heaven, who is the primary cause of earthly agriculture and the habitation of the earth; thus, Ops’s parents are the same as Saturn’s. She is his wife because Saturn produces buildings and agriculture as the active agent, while Ops serves as the passive and material recipient.

SOPHIA. What other children did Saturn have by Ops?

PHILO. Pluto, who signifies the center of the earth, called Hell; and Neptune, who signifies the abyss of the Sea, for Saturn has dominion over both. The poets give him other children as well. But returning to celestial matters, which are our purpose: I tell you that <term>Jupiter</term><note>The original uses "Gione," an archaic Italian variant of "Giove" (Jupiter).</note> was the son of Saturn. This Jupiter is the lower planet that follows Saturn, and in the celestial order he succeeds him, just as Jupiter, King of Crete, succeeded his father Saturn. This Jupiter took the name of this excellent and benign planet because of his own benign and noble virtue, just as his father took the name of Saturn for the similarities I already mentioned. These two kings, participating in the nature of these first two planets, were called by their names as if those celestial beings had descended to earth and become men. Furthermore, they resembled these planets in the events that happened to each of them individually and to one another.

SOPHIA. I have understood about Saturn; tell me about Jupiter, and the allegory of the things that happened between him and his father Saturn, and his own deeds as well.

PHILO. Which of their deeds do you want me to tell you about?

SOPHIA. About what they say: that when Jupiter was born, they hid him from his father Saturn, who ate—

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Philo and Sophia regarding the allegorical meanings of the gods. They have just discussed how Saturn consumes his children, symbolizing Time’s destruction of individual things.</meta>

...slaughtered all his children. 

PHILO: The allegory is that Saturn is the destroyer of all the beauties and excellences that reach the lower world from the other planets, and especially those that come from Jupiter, which are the first and most illustrious. These include **justice**, **liberty**, **magnificence**, **religion**, **ornament**, **splendor**, **beauty**, **love**, **grace**, **kindness**, **liberty**, **prosperity**, **riches**, **delights**, and similar things—of all of which Saturn is the ruiner and destroyer. And those who have Saturn powerful over Jupiter in their <term>nativities</term><note>A "nativity" refers to a person's astrological birth chart, which Renaissance thinkers believed determined one's character and fate.</note> are deprived of all these noble qualities, or else they find them clouded. Thus, just as the Cretan Jupiter, while a child and weak in strength, was hidden from the malevolence of his father Saturn—who wished to kill him because he [Jupiter] was meant to be powerful over him.

SOPHIA: And what is the allegory of the story where they say that while Saturn was in the prison of the **Titans**, Jupiter, his son, liberated him with sufficient force?

PHILO: It signifies that when Jupiter is strong in a person’s birth, or at the start of building some house or residence, or even a great work, if he is found in a favorable <term>aspect</term><note>In astrology, an "aspect" is the angle the planets make with each other, which determines whether their influence is harmonious or difficult.</note> overcoming Saturn, he liberates that person from every calamity, misery, and imprisonment, and represses all their misfortunes.

SOPHIA: And what of the story where they say that Jupiter, after having liberated Saturn, deprived him of his kingdom and confined him to the **Underworld** <note>original: "Inferno"</note>? What does that mean?

PHILO: In one sense, the history is that Jupiter (after he had liberated his father from the prison of the Titans) took the kingdom from him and made him flee to **Italy**. There, he reigned in the company of **Janus** and founded a land where **Rome** now stands, and having been כך confined, he died. The poets call Italy the "Underworld," either because at that time it was considered inferior to **Crete**—where the King [Jupiter] regarded Italy as a hell in comparison to his own kingdom—or because Italy is physically lower than Crete by being further West, since the East is "superior" and the West "inferior." 

But the allegory is that since Jupiter is more powerful than Saturn, in any particular act, he removes the harm Saturn causes that person and makes Saturn inferior in influence. It also signifies, more generally, that while Saturn reigns first in the process of **generation**, preserving the seeds beneath the earth and congealing the <term>sperm</term><note>In the biological theories of the time, "sperm" or "seed" referred to the initial potential of life in both plants and animals.</note> at the beginning of the creation of living things; nonetheless, in the time of the growth and ornamentation of things born, Jupiter is the one who reigns and is the principal power. By removing his father Saturn from dominion, he confines him to the Underworld—which represents the dark places where seeds are hidden at the beginning of generation, over which seeds Saturn has rightful dominion.

<summary>Philo explains the astrological and allegorical conflict between Saturn and Jupiter, representing the tension between destruction/dormancy and growth/justice. He also provides a historical and geographical explanation for Saturn's "exile" to Italy.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Philo and Sophia. Having previously discussed the destructive nature of Saturn (Time), they now transition to the benevolent and ordering influence of Jupiter, exploring his mythological role as a lawgiver and his astrological identity as the planet of justice.</meta>

These allegories of the events occurring between **Jupiter** and **Saturn** resonate with me; and since these have such a subtle meaning, how much more meaning will those things have that are said of the virtue and victory of Jupiter, and of his justice, liberality, and religion?

PHILO: It is just as they say: that he showed the common people the way of living well, forbidding them from many vices they had; for they used to eat human flesh and sacrifice it, and he removed them from that inhuman custom. This signifies that the celestial Jupiter, through his benevolence, prohibits every cruelty to men, makes them merciful, and prolongs and preserves them in life, defending them from death. This is why Jupiter is called *Zeus* in Greek, which means virtue. <note>original: "Zefs." Renaissance etymology often linked the name Zeus to the Greek verb for "to live" (*zên*) or, as Philo suggests here, to "virtue" (*Zefs* / *Zên*).</note> They say further that he gave laws and religion, and established temples. For the planet Jupiter provides such things to men, making them regulated, moderate, and attentive to divine worship. They say that he acquired the greater part of the world, which he divided among his brothers, children, relatives, and friends; and for himself, he desired only Mount Olympus, in which he made his residence, and men went to him to ask for his upright judgments, and he rendered reason and justice to all. These things signify that the planet Jupiter gives men victories, riches, and possessions with liberal distribution, and that he has in himself a pure substance and limpid nature, far removed from all greed and foulness; he makes men just, lovers of virtue and of upright judgments, and for this reason, in the Hebrew tongue, he is called *Tzedek*, which means justice. <note>original: "Sedech." *Tzedek* is the Hebrew name for the planet Jupiter and also the word for "righteousness" or "justice."</note>

PHILO: The historical account is that Jupiter has for his wife his sister Juno, the daughter of Saturn and Ops, both born in the same birth, and that Juno was born first. In the allegorical sense, some take Juno for the Earth and for Water, and Jupiter for the Air and for Fire; others take Juno for the Air and Jupiter for the Fire—between which there seems to be a brotherhood and a conjunction; others take her to be the Moon, and everyone adapts the fables of Juno to their own opinion.

the ancients; rather, they held that the air was contiguous to the sphere of the Moon, even though that first part, through the refinement of the heavens and their continuous motion, is the hottest; hence (through the universality of the air throughout the globe, which is most appropriately assigned to **Juno**) she is the governing virtue of the whole world, of generation, and of the elements. Just as **Jupiter** is the governing virtue of the celestial bodies, but is specifically assigned to the planet <term>Jupiter|Iuppiter</term><note>The author distinguishes between the mythological deity/cosmic force and the physical planet.</note> because it is the most benign, the most excellent, and the highest after **Saturn**, who is his father—that is, the <term>Intellect|Intelletto</term> which is the producer of the celestial soul—and **Opis** his mother, who is the center of the earth and the <term>prime matter|materia prima</term><note>In Renaissance philosophy, "prime matter" refers to the fundamental, formless substance that underlies all physical things.</note>. **Jupiter** remains in the middle of the celestial realm, acting as the principle and father of the other planets and of heaven, and of his sister **Juno**, who contains everything that exists from the center of the earth up to the heavens; and being contiguous with one another, they are called siblings, and it is said they were born in the same birth. This denotes that the celestial world and the elemental world were produced together by the Father-Intellect and the Mother-Matter, according to what **Anaxagoras** used to say, in conformity with Holy Scripture regarding the production or creation of the world. Where it says that from one principle and seed of things, God created the heaven and the earth. 

They say further that **Juno** emerged first from her mother’s womb, because they understood that the formation of the entire universe began from the center, and that it proceeded successively, ascending to the ultimate circumference of heaven, like a tree that goes on growing until it reaches the top. This conforms to the saying of the Psalmist, who says: *In the day that God created earth and heaven*, <note>original: "Nel diche creo Dio terra / & cielo." Reference to Genesis 2:4 or Psalm 102:25, where the earthly is sometimes mentioned before the heavenly to indicate the order of generation from the center outward.</note> which placed the inferior before the superior corporeal thing in the order of creation. And they are called joined in marriage because (as I told you above) the celestial world is the true husband of the elemental world, which is his true wife; one is the agent and the other the recipient. She is called **Juno** because she "helps" <note>original: "giuoua." The author is using a Renaissance etymology linking Juno (*Iuno*) to the Latin *iuvare*, "to help."</note>, just as the derivation of **Jupiter** [is the same], because both help in the generation of things, one as father and the other as mother. Nevertheless, **Juno** is called the Goddess of marriages and **Lucina** <note>An epithet for the goddess presiding over childbirth, meaning "she who brings to light."</note> of those in labor, because she is the governing virtue of the world, of the conjunction of the elements, and of the generation of things.

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Philo and Sophia regarding the cosmological roles of the gods. Having established Jupiter as the celestial governor and Juno as the spirit of the elemental world, Philo now explains the allegorical significance of their children, Hebe and Mars, representing the cycles of life, growth, and decay.</meta>

...[gave birth] to a daughter, called **Hebe**, who, for her beauty, was called the **Goddess of Youth**, and she married **Hercules**. 

**SO.** What is the allegory? 

**PH.** The Sun (who is called **Apollo**) being in the house of **Jupiter** his father—that is, in **Sagittarius**, which is the first domain of **Jupiter**, and from there to **Pisces**, which is the second sign of **Jupiter** in the **Zodiac**—and this occurs from mid-November until mid-March, because of the great cold and high humidity of those months. **Juno**, who is the elemental world, became pregnant. And this is what is meant when it is said that **Apollo** gave her wild lettuce to eat, which is very cold and humid; these two qualities make the earth pregnant, she having previously been sterile from the passing Autumn. At that time, the roots of the seeds of all things begin to take on a <term>germinating virtue|virtù germinativa</term><note>In Renaissance natural philosophy, this refers to the latent power within seeds and soil that allows life to sprout.</note>, which is true conception. 

And thus she comes to give birth in the **Spring**. This happens when the Sun passes from **Pisces** into **Aries**; and because at that time every plant is in bloom and everything becomes young again, she is therefore called the **Goddess of Youth**. In effect, **Hebe** is the germinating virtue of Spring, which is born from the celestial **Jupiter** and the terrestrial and elemental **Juno**, through the intercession of the Sun. 

And they say she is married to **Hercules** because men who are excellent and famous in virtue are called **Hercules**, for the fame of such men is always young, and never dies nor grows old. 

**SO.** I have understood about **Hebe**; tell me of **Mars**, their son. 

**PH.** **Mars**, as you know, is a hot planet, and produces heat in the lower world; this heat, mixed with the humidity signified by **Hebe**, creates the generation of this lower world, which is signified by **Juno**. So **Juno** gave birth to this daughter and this son from the celestial **Jupiter**, and from them, all lower generations are then made. 

Furthermore, we say that just as **Hebe** signifies the universal generation of the world, so **Mars**—who is <term>combustive|comburente</term> and destructive—signifies <term>corruption|corruttione</term><note>A technical philosophical term for the natural process of decay or the breaking down of physical forms, the opposite of "generation."</note>, which is caused primarily by the great heat of Summer, which dries up all humidity. 

So these two children of **Jupiter** and **Juno** are the generation and corruption of things, by which the lower world continues; and because corruption does not proceed from the celestial principle except by accident—since its own work and intention is generation—they therefore say that **Juno** gave birth to **Mars** through the <term>striking of the womb|percussione della Vulva</term><note>This refers to the myth where Juno conceived Mars not through union with Jupiter, but by touching a magical flower. Philo interprets this "solo" birth as a sign that decay (Mars) comes from the "defects" of matter rather than the divine intention of the heavens.</note>, because corruption comes from the defect and striking of matter, but not from the intention of the agent. 

**SO.** I like the allegory of the marriage, and of the sa... <meta>Catchword: sa (likely "sapienza" or "salute")</meta>

<summary>Philo explains the mythological birth of Hebe and Mars as an allegory for the seasonal cycles of the earth. Hebe represents the moist, germinating power of Spring (generation), while Mars represents the drying heat of Summer that leads to decay (corruption).</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Philo and Sophia. On the previous page, Philo explained how Juno (the elemental world) gave birth to Hebe (Spring) through the influence of Jupiter and the Sun. Here, the conversation shifts to other famous offspring of Jupiter, specifically the birth of Diana and Apollo, interpreting the myth as a cosmological event following the Great Flood.</meta>

...most high generation of **Jupiter** and **Juno**. I would like to know something of their love affairs and extraordinary offspring, such as those of **Latona**, **Alcmena**, and others. 

**PH.** They say that **Jupiter** fell in love with the virgin **Latona** and impregnated her. **Juno**, suffering this bitterly, not only stirred up all parts of the earth against her so that none would receive her, but also had her pursued by **Python**, an enormous serpent, who drove her from every place. Wherefore she, fleeing, came to the island of **Delos**, which gave her refuge, and there she gave birth to **Diana** and **Apollo**. But **Diana** came out first, and performing the office of <term>Lucina|Goddess of Childbirth</term><note>Lucina was the Roman goddess of childbirth who protected the mother during labor; here, the myth suggests Diana acted as a midwife for her own twin brother.</note>, she assisted her mother in the birth of **Apollo**. And once he was born, with his bow and arrows, he killed the aforementioned serpent **Python**. 

**SO.** Give me the allegory. 

**PH.** It means that during the <term>Flood|Il Diluvio</term><note>The author refers to the Great Flood (often associated with Deucalion in Greek myth or Noah in the Biblical tradition), a common starting point for Renaissance cosmological histories.</note>, and also shortly thereafter, the **Air** was so thickened by water vapors covering the earth—due to the great and continuous rains of the Flood—that neither **Lunar** nor **Solar** light appeared in the world, because their rays could not penetrate the density of the air. 

Hence it is said that **Latona** (who is the circumference of the heavens where the **Milky Way** runs) was pregnant by **Jupiter** her lover, and wishing to bring forth the **Lunar** and **Solar** light into the universe after the Flood, **Juno** (who is the **Air**, the **Water**, and the **Earth**), indignant with jealousy over that pregnancy, hindered **Latona’s** delivery and the appearance of the **Sun** and **Moon** in the world with her thickness and her vapors. 

In such a way, she caused her to be neither received nor seen in any part of the earth; and furthermore, the serpent **Python** (which was the great humidity remaining from the Flood) pursued her with the continuous ascent of vapors which, by thickening the **Air**, did not allow her to give birth or for the **Lunar** or **Solar** rays to appear. And it calls that superfluous humidity a "serpent" because it was the cause of the decay of plants and of all terrestrial animals. 

Finally, on the island of **Delos** (where the **Air** first purified itself through the dryness of the sea's saltiness), **Latona** gave birth to **Diana** and **Apollo**. For the Greeks hold that first after the Flood, the **Moon** and the **Sun** appeared in **Delos**. And **Diana** is said to have been born first because the appearance of the **Moon** at night happened first, and then **Apollo** was born and appeared the following day; in this way, the appearance of the **Moon** prepared the way for that of the **Sun**, as if she had been the **Lucina** for her mother during her brother's birth. And once **Apollo** was born, they say he killed with his [bow...]

<summary>Philo explains the myth of Latona, Diana, and Apollo as an allegory for the atmospheric clearing that followed the Great Flood, where the Sun and Moon finally became visible through the dissipating vapors (the serpent Python).</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Philo and Sophia. On the previous page, Philo began explaining the mythological genealogy of the gods as cosmological allegories, specifically discussing the birth of Apollo and Diana. Here, he moves to the birth of Hebe and Mars, explaining how the interactions of the planets and the seasons govern the cycles of life and decay on Earth.</meta>

...gave birth to a daughter, called **Hebe**, who, for her beauty, was called the **Goddess of Youth**, and she married **Hercules**. 

**SO.** What is the allegory? 

**PH.** The **Sun** (who is called **Apollo**) being in the house of **Jupiter** his father—that is, in **Sagittarius**, which is the first domain of **Jupiter**—and from there, until **Pisces**, which is the second sign of **Jupiter** in the Zodiac; and this is from mid-November until mid-March, because of the great cold and high humidity of those months. **Juno**, who is the <term>elemental world|mondo elementale</term><note>In Renaissance philosophy, the "elemental world" refers to the Earth and its atmosphere, where the four elements (earth, air, fire, water) are constantly changing, as opposed to the unchanging celestial heavens.</note>, became pregnant. And this is understood when it is said that **Apollo** gave her wild lettuce to eat, which is very cold and humid; and these two qualities make the earth conceive, it having previously been sterile from the past Autumn, and the roots of the seeds of things then begin to take on germinative power, which is true conception. And thus she comes to give birth in the **Spring**. This occurs when the **Sun** passes from **Pisces** into **Aries**, and because then every plant is in bloom and everything is rejuvenated, for this reason she is called the **Goddess of Youth**; for in effect **Hebe** is the germinative power of **Spring**, who is born of celestial **Jupiter** and terrestrial and elemental **Juno**, through the intercession of the **Sun**. And they say she married **Hercules** because men who are excellent and famous in virtue are called **Hercules**, for the fame of such men is always young and never dies, nor grows old. 

**SO.** I have understood about **Hebe**; tell me of **Mars**, their son. 

**PH.** **Mars**, as you know, is a hot planet and produces heat in the lower world; this heat, mixed with humidity (represented by **Hebe**), causes the generation of this lower world (represented by **Juno**). So **Juno** gave birth to this daughter and this son by celestial **Jupiter**, by whom all lower generations are then made. They also say that just as **Hebe** signifies the universal generation of the world, so **Mars**, who is burning and destructive, signifies <term>corruption|corruttione</term><note>In this context, "generation and corruption" is an Aristotelian concept referring to the natural cycle of birth and decay.</note>, which is caused, principally, by the great heat of Summer, which dries up all humidity. So these two children of **Jupiter** and **Juno** are the generation and corruption of things, by which the lower world continues; and because corruption does not come from the celestial principle except by accident—since its own work and intention is generation—for this reason they say that **Juno** gave birth to **Mars** by the <term>striking of the flower|percussione della Vulua</term><note>original: "percussione della Vulua." This refers to the myth where Juno conceived Mars without a father by touching a magical flower. In Renaissance allegory, "percussione" (striking or contact) implies that destruction/Mars is a side effect of material contact rather than a primary divine plan.</note>, because corruption comes from the defect and striking of matter, but not from the intention of the agent. 

**SO.** I like the allegory of the marriage, and of the sa...

<summary>Philo explains the births of Hebe and Mars as allegories for the biological and seasonal cycles of the Earth, identifying Hebe with the rejuvenating power of Spring and Mars with the destructive heat of Summer.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Philo and Sophia regarding the mythological births of the gods and their physical interpretations. Philo transitions from discussing the seasonal cycle of growth (Hebe) to the story of Latona (Leto) and the birth of the twin luminaries, the Sun and the Moon, following a primordial catastrophe.</meta>

...most righteous offspring of **Jupiter** and **Juno**. I would like to know something of their love affairs and extraordinary births, such as those of **Latona**, **Alcmene**, and others. 

**PH.** They say that **Jupiter** fell in love with the virgin **Latona**, and that he impregnated her. **Juno**, suffering this thing bitterly, not only stirred up all parts of the earth against her so that none would receive her, but also had her persecuted by **Python**, a massive serpent who drove her from every place. Whereupon she, fleeing, came to the island of **Delos**, which received her; and there she gave birth to **Diana** and **Apollo**. But **Diana** was born first, and performing the office of <term>Lucina|the Roman goddess of childbirth</term><note>In mythology, Diana (Artemis) was said to have been born first and immediately assisted her mother in the delivery of her twin brother, Apollo.</note>, she helped her mother in the birth of **Apollo**. And as soon as he was born, with his bow and arrows, he killed the said serpent **Python**. 

**SO.** Tell me the allegory. 

**PH.** It signifies that during the **Deluge** <note>The Great Flood, often associated in this period with the story of Noah or Deucalion.</note>, and also shortly thereafter, the **Air** was so thickened by the vapors of the water covering the earth—due to the great and continuous rains that occurred during the flood—that no **Moonlight** or **Sunlight** appeared in the world, because their rays could not penetrate the density of the air. Wherefore it is said that **Latona** (who represents the circumference of the heaven where the **Milky Way** is) was pregnant by **Jupiter**, her lover. Wishing to give birth to the light of the **Moon** and the **Sun** into the universe after the flood, **Juno** (who represents the **Air**, the **Water**, and the **Earth**) being incensed by jealousy over that pregnancy, hindered the birth of **Latona** and the appearance of the **Sun** and the **Moon** in the world with her own thickness and vapors. 

In this way, she ensured that in no place on earth could **Latona** be received or seen. Furthermore, the serpent **Python** (representing the great humidity that remained from the flood) persecuted her with the continuous rising of vapors, which, by thickening the air, would not allow the **Lunar** or **Solar** rays to be born or appear. And he calls that superfluous humidity a "serpent" because it was the cause of the corruption <note>In the scientific language of the time, "corruption" refers to the rotting or breaking down of organic matter.</note> of plants and of all terrestrial animals. 

Finally, on the island of **Delos** (where the air was first purified by the dryness of the saltiness of the **Sea**), **Latona** gave birth to **Diana** and **Apollo**. This is because the Greeks hold that the **Moon** and the **Sun** first appeared at **Delos** after the flood. It is said that **Diana** was born first because the appearance of the **Moon** occurred first at night; afterwards **Apollo** was born, appearing the following day. Thus, the appearance of the **Moon** prepared the way for that of the **Sun**, as if she had been the midwife to her mother in the birth of her brother. And once **Apollo** was born, they say that he killed with his...

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Sophia and Philo concerning the philosophical and astrological interpretations of Greek and Roman myths. Having just discussed Latona and the birth of the luminaries (the Sun and Moon), Philo now turns to the children of Jupiter and Juno—Hebe and Mars—to explain the cycles of life and decay on Earth.</meta>

...gave birth to a daughter, named **Hebe**, who, because of her beauty, was called the **Goddess of Youth**, and she married **Hercules**. 

**SO.** What is the **allegory**? 

**PH.** The **Sun** (who is called **Apollo**) being in the house of **Jupiter** his father—that is, in **Sagittarius**, which is the first domain of **Jupiter**, and from there until **Pisces**, which is the second sign of **Jupiter** in the **Zodiac**—and this takes place from mid-November until mid-March, because of the great cold and much humidity of those months. **Juno**, who represents the elemental world, became pregnant. And this is understood when it is said that **Apollo** had given her wild lettuce to eat <note>This refers to a specific myth where Juno conceived Hebe without a father after eating wild lettuce at a banquet given by Apollo. In this philosophical context, it represents the sun's influence on the earth's moisture.</note>, which are very cold and humid; and these two qualities cause the earth to become pregnant, having previously been sterile from the past **Autumn**. Then the roots of the seeds of all things begin to take on a germinative power, which is true conception. And thus she comes to give birth in the **Spring**. This occurs when the **Sun** passes from **Pisces** into **Aries**; and because every plant is then in bloom and everything is rejuvenated, she is therefore called the **Goddess of Youth**. In effect, **Hebe** is the germinative power of **Spring**, which is born of the celestial **Jupiter** and the terrestrial and elemental **Juno**, through the intercession of the **Sun**. And they say she is married to **Hercules**, because men who are excellent and famous in virtue are called **Hercules**, for the fame of such men is always young and never dies nor grows old. 

**SO.** I have understood **Hebe**; tell me of **Mars**, their son. 

**PH.** **Mars**, as you know, is a hot planet and produces dryness in the lower world; this heat, mixed with the humidity signified by **Hebe**, causes the **generation** <term>generation|the natural process of birth, growth, and coming into being</term> of this lower world, which is signified by **Juno**. Thus **Juno** gave birth to this daughter and this son by the celestial **Jupiter**, through whom all lower generations are then made. Furthermore, we say that just as **Hebe** signifies the universal generation of the world, so **Mars**, who is burning and destroying, signifies **corruption** <term>corruption|the natural process of decay, death, or the breaking down of matter</term>, which is caused principally by the great heat of **Summer**, which dries up all humidity. So these two children of **Jupiter** and **Juno** are the **generation** and **corruption** of things, by which the lower world continues. And because corruption does not come from the celestial principle except by accident—since the proper work and intention of the heavens is generation—they therefore say that **Juno** gave birth to **Mars** through the striking of the womb <note>original: "percussione della Vulua." Some versions of the myth claim Juno conceived Mars by touching a specific flower or by being struck; the author uses this to explain that "decay" is a side effect of physical matter rather than a divine plan.</note>, because corruption comes from the defect and striking of matter, but not from the intention of the agent.

<meta>This page continues the dialogue where Philo is explaining the mythological birth of Apollo and Diana as an allegory. Having just mentioned how Apollo killed the serpent Python, Philo now connects this Greek myth to the biblical account of Creation found in Genesis.</meta>

...bow and arrows [he killed the] serpent **Python**. That is, the **Sun**, as it appeared, dried up with its rays the humidity that prohibited the generation of animals and plants.

**SO.** What is the bow of **Apollo**?

**PH.** I could tell you that it is the circumference of the solar body, from which the rays emerge like arrows—since arrows presuppose a bow—but in effect, the bow of **Apollo** is another more specific one, which I will explain to you when we speak of his loves <note>This likely refers to the myth of Apollo and Daphne, where Cupid's bow plays a central role.</note>. I could also tell you another allegory, more ancient and wise, regarding the birth of **Diana** and **Apollo**.

**SO.** Tell it to me, I pray you.

**PH.** It denotes their production—the creation of the world—in forms mostly consistent with the **Sacred Mosaic Scripture** <note>Referring to the Book of Genesis, traditionally attributed to Moses.</note>.

**SO.** In what way?

**PH.** **Moses** writes that when God created the superior celestial world and the inferior terrestrial world, the terrestrial one, with all its elements, was confused, and made into a dark and obscure abyss. He writes that with the divine spirit breathing upon the waters of the abyss, He produced light; and there was first night and then day—the first day. This is what the fable of the birth of **Latona** signifies. She is the celestial substance, with whom **Jupiter**—who is the High God, creator of all things—was in love, and he impregnated her with luminous bodies in potential, primarily the **Sun** and the **Moon**. However, **Juno** (who is the sphere of the elements while in a state of confusion) would not allow the appearance of the luminous bodies; their rays could not penetrate her, but instead were rebuffed from every part of the globe. Furthermore, the abyss of water—which is the serpent **Python**—prevented the heavens from "giving birth" to its light (meaning the **Sun** and the **Moon**) upon the earth.

Finally, on the island of **Delos**—which represents the uncovered parts of the earth (which in the beginning were not large, but like a small island placed within the waters)—they appeared for the first time; because, through the recession of the water, the air was no longer so thick. Thus, in the **Sacred Scripture**, it is told that after the first day (night and day) and the second day (the spreading of the ethereal firmament, which was the division of air, water, and earth), the earth was then revealed. On the third day, He gave beginning to the production of plants. On the fourth day, there was the appearance of the **Sun** and the **Moon** over the already revealed earth. This is the figure <note>original: "figura," meaning a symbolic representation or "type."</note> of the birth of **Latona** on the island of **Delos**. In this birth, it is denoted that their greatness existed on the first day, but their "birth" and appearance occurred on the fourth of the six days of creation. And they say that **Diana** emerged first, and that she was the helper in the birth of **Apollo**—because in the creation, night precedes the day...

<summary>Philo provides a detailed allegorical interpretation of the birth of Apollo and Diana, synchronizing Greek mythology with the Hexameron (the six days of creation) from the Book of Genesis.</summary>

He—who by his divine virtues shared by **Jupiter** was the true son of **Jupiter** himself, and figuratively and instrumentally the son of **Amphitryon**—is the model for all excellent men, who may also be called **Hercules**, like that most illustrious son of **Alcmene**.
