<meta>This page continues the philosophical dialogue between Philo and Sophia. While the previous page concluded technical astrological details regarding planetary "terms" and "faces," the conversation has now transitioned into a metaphysical inquiry into the nature of love. Philo is explaining how love operates among spiritual beings and between the spiritual and material realms, defining love not just as a desire for what one lacks, but as a generous impulse from the perfect toward the imperfect.</meta>

...to unite themselves <note>The word begins mid-sentence, likely "unirsi" (to unite).</note> with the spiritual ones, who are perfect; but how can the perfect ones desire to unite themselves with the imperfect?

**PHILO.** Spiritual beings possess love not only for one another, but they also love bodily and material things. What you say—that love is desire, and that desire is a lack <note>original: "mancamento"; Philo is referencing the Platonic idea that we only desire what we do not already possess.</note>—is the truth. However, it is not inconsistent (given that there are orders of perfection among the spiritual beings, where one is more perfect than another and of a clearer and more sublime essence) that the inferior, who possesses less, should love the superior and desire to be united with him. Hence, all beings love principally and supremely the highest and perfect God, who is the Fountain from which all their being and goodness flows; they all most passionately desire union with Him and seek it always through their intellectual acts.

**SOPHIA.** I grant you that spiritual beings love one another because the inferior loves the superior. But the superior does not love the inferior, nor do spiritual beings love bodily or material things, because they are more perfect and have no lack of the imperfect. Therefore, they cannot desire or love them as you have said.

**PHILO.** I was just about to answer this second argument of yours, if you had been patient. Know that just as the inferiors love the superiors, desiring to unite with them because of what they lack of the superiors' greater perfection, so too the superiors love the inferiors and desire to unite them with themselves so that they may become more perfect. This desire indeed presupposes a lack—not in the superior who desires, but in the inferior who is in need. For the desiring superior, loving the inferior, desires to use his superiority to supply what is lacking in the inferior’s perfection. In this way, spiritual beings love material bodies: to supply their lack with their own perfection, to unite them with themselves, and to make them excellent.

**SOPHIA.** And which do you hold to be the truer or more complete love: that of the superior for the inferior, or that of the inferior for the superior?

**PHILO.** That of the superior for the inferior, and of the spiritual for the material.

**SOPHIA.** Tell me the reason.

**PHILO.** Because one is for the purpose of receiving, and the other for the purpose of giving. The superior spiritual being loves the inferior as a father loves his son, and the inferior loves as a son loves his father. You well know how much more perfect a father's love is than a son's. Furthermore, the love of the spiritual world for the material world is similar to the love a male has for a female, and that of the material for the spiritual is similar to the love a female has for a male, as I have already explained to you above. Have patience...

<summary>In this passage from Leone Ebreo's "Dialogues of Love," Philo explains that love exists in the spiritual realm as both a desire for higher perfection (receiving) and a desire to perfect lower beings (giving). He argues that the love of a superior for an inferior is "more perfect" because it mirrors the selfless giving of a father or the generative relationship between the masculine and feminine principles.</summary>
<keywords>Leone Ebreo, Dialoghi d'Amore, Neoplatonism, metaphysics of love, perfection, hierarchy of being, divine fountain, fatherly love, spiritual vs material</keywords>

--- Page 111 ---
<meta>This page continues Philo’s explanation of why superior, spiritual beings feel love for inferior, material ones. On the previous page, Philo began to counter Sophia’s argument that love is only a "lack" felt by the inferior toward the superior. He is now using the analogy of the benefactor and the "giving" nature of the masculine to illustrate a more perfect form of love.</meta>

<sig>F iij</sig>

...Sophia, that the male who gives loves more perfectly than the female who receives; just as among men, benefactors love those who receive their benefits more than the beneficiaries love their benefactors. This is because the latter love for the sake of gain, while the former love for the sake of virtue. The first kind of love pertains to utility, while the other is entirely honorable <note>original: "honesto"; in this philosophical context, it refers to moral integrity and noble purpose rather than just honesty</note>; and you well know how much more excellent the honorable is than the useful. Thus, it is not without reason that I told you that love in spiritual beings is much more excellent and perfect toward corporeal ones than it is in corporeal beings toward spiritual ones.

**SOPHIA.** What you have told me certainly satisfies me, but two other doubts still occur to me. One is that if desire presupposes a lack <note>original: "manchamento"; Philo is referencing the Platonic idea that we only desire what we do not already possess</note>, then it must be a lack of the desired thing within the desiring lover, and not a lack of perfection in the beloved thing caused by the lover, as you seem to say—namely, that the lack exists in the inferior being which is desired and loved by the superior. The other doubt is that I have heard that beloved persons, insofar as they are loved, are more perfect than the lovers; this is because love is directed toward good things, and thus the beloved thing is the end and intent of the lover, and the end is always the more noble. How then can the imperfect be loved by the perfect as you say?

**PHILO.** Your doubts are of some importance. The solution to the first is that in the order of the universe, the inferior depends upon the superior, just as the corporeal world depends on the spiritual. Thus, a lack in the inferior would bring about a lack in the superior upon which it depends; for the perfection <note>In this context, Philo implies that the state of the effect reflects back upon the cause</note> of the effect denotes the perfection of the cause. Therefore, when the cause loves its effect, and the superior loves the inferior, it desires the perfection of the inferior and desires to unite the inferior with itself to free it from defect. By freeing the inferior, the superior saves itself from lack or imperfection. If the inferior does not come to be united with the superior, not only does it remain defective and unhappy, but the superior also remains stained <note>original: "maculato"</note> by a lack of its own sublime perfection. A father cannot be a happy father if his son is imperfect; therefore the ancients say that the sinner places a stain upon divinity and offends it, just as the righteous man exalts it.

Hence, with good reason, not only does the inferior love and desire to be united with the superior, but the superior also loves and desires to unite the inferior with itself. This is so that each of them may be perfect in its own degree, without lack, and so that the universe may be united and bound together successively with the bond of love, which joins the world...

<summary>Philo explains that superior beings love inferior ones to ensure the perfection of the entire universe, arguing that a "cause" (like a father or a creator) is only truly perfect when its "effect" is also perfected. He addresses Sophia's confusion about how the perfect can desire the imperfect by framing love as a cosmic bond of unity.</summary>

<keywords>Platonic love, hierarchy of being, cause and effect, virtue vs utility, cosmic unity, divine perfection, benefactor, Sophia, Philo</keywords>

--- Page 112 ---
<meta>This page continues Philo’s explanation of the cosmic hierarchy of love. He is addressing Sophia’s second doubt regarding why a superior being would love an inferior one if the "beloved" is supposed to be more perfect than the "lover."</meta>

...the corporeal with the spiritual, and the inferior with the superior; this union is the primary goal of the **Supreme Architect** <note>original: "sommo Opifice"; a common Renaissance term for God as the craftsman of the universe</note> and omnipotent God in the production of the world, through ordered diversity and unified plurality.

**SOPHIA.** I see the solution to the first doubt; now solve the second for me.

**PHILO.** Aristotle solves it. He proved that those who eternally move the celestial bodies are **intellectual souls** <term>intellectual souls: in medieval and Renaissance cosmology, these were the "Intelligences" or angelic beings believed to guide the planets in their orbits</term> and are immaterial. They move these bodies for some end and purpose of their own souls, and Aristotle says that such an end is more noble and more excellent than the **mover** <term>mover: the "Prime Mover" or the intelligence responsible for a sphere's motion</term> itself, since the end of a thing is always more noble than the thing itself.

Of the four causes of natural things—which are the **Material**, the **Formal**, the **Agent cause** (which makes or moves the thing), and the **Final cause** (which is the end or purpose that moves the agent to act)—the Material is the lowest of all. The Formal is better than the Material, and the Agent is better and more noble than both because it is their cause. However, the **Final cause** <note>original: "causa Finita" or "Finale"</note> is the most noble and excellent of all four, even more than the Agent cause, because the agent only moves for the sake of the end. Thus, the End is called the "cause of all causes."

From this, we conclude that the "end" (for which the intellectual soul of each of the Heavens moves its orbit) is of greater excellence not only than the body of the Heaven, but even than that soul itself. Aristotle says that this end, being loved and desired by the soul of the Heaven, causes that intellectual soul—through firm desire and insatiable appetite—to move the celestial body assigned to it eternally. The soul loves that body and gives it life, even though the body is less noble and inferior to the soul (since the body is matter and the soul is intellect). It does this primarily out of the love it has for its **superior beloved** <note>This refers to God or the higher cosmic Intelligences</note>, which is more excellent than itself, desiring to be united with it eternally and to find happiness in that union, just like a true lover with their beloved.

Therefore, Sophia, you can understand that superiors love inferiors, and spiritual beings love corporeal ones, because of the love they have for their own superiors. To enjoy that higher union, they love the inferiors, and by loving them, they improve and "perfect" those who are below them.

**SOPHIA.** Tell me, I pray, what beings are greater than the intellectual souls that move the heavens? Who could be their lovers and the object of their desire for union, making them so happy and so diligent in eternally moving their heavens? Also, you must tell me how superiors, by loving inferiors, enjoy union with their own superiors, for the reason for this is not manifest to me.

<summary>Philo explains that celestial souls move the planets out of love for a "Final Cause" (God or a higher intelligence) that is more perfect than themselves. By loving and animating the inferior physical heavens, these spiritual souls achieve a state of union with the divine.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, four causes, Final Cause, intellectual souls, celestial spheres, hierarchy of love, divine union, Philo, Sophia</keywords>

--- Page 113 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Philo and Sophia regarding the celestial hierarchy. Philo is explaining why the "movers" of the heavens (the Intelligences) are motivated by a desire for something even more perfect than themselves, addressing Sophia's question about the nature of these superior beings.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<sig>F iiij</sig>

**PHILO.** Regarding your first question, the philosophers who commented on **Aristotle** <note>The Greek philosopher whose works on physics and metaphysics formed the basis of medieval science.</note> sought to understand who these excellent beings were—those who serve as the "ends" or goals, and who are even more sublime than the **intellectual souls** that move the heavens. The first "Academy" of the Arabs—**Al-Farabi**, **Avicenna**, **Al-Ghazali**, and our own **Rabbi Moses of Egypt** <note>Referring to Maimonides (1135–1204), the preeminent Jewish philosopher</note> in his *Guide* <note>original: "More"; referring to the *Moreh Nevukhim* or *Guide for the Perplexed*</note>—say that to every **orb** <term>orb: the transparent, rotating spheres that were believed to carry the planets and stars around the Earth</term> two **Intelligences** are assigned. One of these moves the orb effectively; it is the intellectual, motivating soul of that sphere. The other moves it as a "final cause," because it is the "end" or goal for which the mover (the Intelligence that animates the heaven) performs its motion. This second Intelligence is loved by the first as a more excellent being; desiring to unite with what it loves, the mover eternally rotates its heaven.

**SOPHIA.** How then would that common philosophical teaching hold up—the one stating that the number of **Angels** (or separate Intelligences) that move the heavens is equal to the number of the orbs they move? According to these Arabs, there would be double the number of Intelligences as there are orbs.

**PHILO.** They say that this saying and this number still hold true for each of these two species of Intelligences—the **movers** and the **final ends**. This is because there must be as many moving Intelligences as there are orbs, and likewise, as many "final" Intelligences as there are movers.

**SOPHIA.** They truly alter that ancient saying by doubling the number. But what will they say of the **First Mover** of the highest heaven, whom we hold to be God? It is surely impossible for Him to have any "end" or goal better than Himself.

**PHILO.** These Arab philosophers hold that the **First Mover** is not the Supreme God, because if He were, God would be a soul assigned to a specific orb, just like the other moving Intelligences. Such an assignment and such an equality with others would be a significant impropriety for God. Rather, they say that the "end" for which the First Mover moves is the Supreme God.

**SOPHIA.** And is this opinion accepted by all the other philosophers?

**PHILO.** Certainly not. **Averroes** <note>The 12th-century Andalusian philosopher known as "The Commentator" for his influential work on Aristotle.</note> and others who have since commented on Aristotle hold that there are only as many Intelligences as there are orbs, and no more. They believe the First Mover *is* the Supreme God. Averroes says it is not an impropriety for God to be assigned to the orb as its soul or as the form that gives being to the **Highest Heaven**. Since such souls are separate from matter, and since His orb is the one that contains and embraces the entire universe, and which moves with its motion...

<summary>Philo explains the "Arab" philosophical view that each celestial sphere has two Intelligences—a mover and a beloved goal—while noting Averroes' counter-argument that God is the First Mover of the outermost sphere.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, Maimonides, Al-Farabi, Avicenna, Al-Ghazali, Averroes, Intelligences, Celestial Orbs, First Mover, Final Cause, cosmology, medieval philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 114 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Philo and Sophia. Philo is detailing the perspective of Averroes regarding the "Intelligences" that move the celestial spheres, contrasting it with the earlier views of Arab and Jewish philosophers like Maimonides. The discussion centers on whether the "end" or goal of a celestial mover is a separate, higher entity or a state of perfection within itself.</meta>

[mo]vement of all the other heavens, that **Intelligence** <term>Intelligence: in medieval cosmology, these were non-physical, angelic beings responsible for the movement of the celestial spheres.</term> which informs and moves it, and gives it the status of a mover, does not make itself equal to the others; instead, it remains much higher and more sublime, just as its **Orb** is more sublime than those of the other Intelligences, and just as its heaven encompasses and contains all the others, so too its power contains the power of all the other movers. And if, by being called a "mover" like the others, it were equal to them, then according to the first group <note>Referring to the philosophers mentioned on the previous page, such as Al-Farabi and Maimonides.</note> it would be equal to the other final Intelligences, by being, like them, the goal of the first mover. And in conclusion, **Averroes** <note>Ibn Rushd (1126–1198), the Andalusian philosopher known for his influential commentaries on Aristotle.</note> says that to posit more Intelligences than what the force of philosophical reason necessitates is not the work of a philosopher, since one cannot perceive anything otherwise except what reason demonstrates.

**SOPHIA.** This opinion seems more limited to me than that of the first group; but what will this man say regarding what **Aristotle** affirms—and reason with him—that the goal of the mover of the **Orb** is more noble than the mover itself?

**PHILO.** **Averroes** says that **Aristotle** means that the same **Intelligence** that moves the orb is the goal of itself in its continuous movement. This is because it moves the **Orb** to fulfill its own perfection; in this regard, it is more noble as the *end* of the motion than as the *beginning* of it. Thus, this saying of **Aristotle** is a comparison between two types of causality found within the same **Intelligence**—that is, the **Effective** and the **Final** <term>Effective and Final: 'Effective' causality refers to the agent that brings about a change (the mover), while 'Final' causality refers to the purpose or goal for which the change occurs.</term>—rather than a comparison of one **Intelligence** to another, as the first group says.

**SOPHIA.** It seems strange to me that for these reasons **Aristotle** would say that one and the same **Intelligence** is more perfect than itself.

**PHILO.** It also seems unreasonable to me that a statement intended as an absolute comparison, such as this one by **Aristotle**, should be understood relatively within the same **Intelligence**. Even though this opinion of **Averroes** is true—especially regarding the First Mover, who, being God, must be the "end" of His own motion and action—and even if it is true that the final cause is more excellent than the effective cause, it does not seem that this was **Aristotle's** intention in that saying, nor that he wished to imply such a meaning.

**SOPHIA.** What, then, do you think his meaning was?

**PHILO.** To demonstrate that the goal of all the movers of the heavens is one **Intelligence**, more sublime and superior to all others, loved by all with a desire to unite with it, in which their supreme happiness consists; and this is the Supreme God.

**SOPHIA.** And do you, O **Philo**, hold that He is the first mover?

<summary>Philo explains Averroes' view that celestial movers find their purpose within their own perfection rather than in a separate higher Intelligence, though Philo ultimately argues that all movers are motivated by a singular love for the Supreme God.</summary>
<keywords>Averroes, Aristotle, celestial movers, Intelligences, effective cause, final cause, divine love, cosmology, scholasticism</keywords>

--- Page 115 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue where Philo explains the Aristotelian and Averroistic concepts of the "mover" and the "end" (goal) of celestial and human souls. He clarifies that the ultimate goal of any intellectual being is union with God, who acts as the final cause for all existence.</meta>

It would be long to tell you all that could be said on this matter, and perhaps it would be bold to favor one opinion over the other. But even if I were to grant you that **Aristotle’s** <note>Aristotle (384–322 BCE), the Greek philosopher whose works formed the basis for medieval science and logic.</note> intent was that the **first mover** is God, I tell you that he holds God to be the end of all movers, and more excellent than all others—over whom He is superior—but he does not say that He is more excellent than Himself, even though in Him the most fundamental aspect is being the **final cause** <term>final cause: the purpose or "end goal" for which a thing exists or is done.</term> of every thing, because the one is the goal toward which the other is directed.

**SOPHIA.** Tell me, do you deny that the other movers move the heavens to fulfill their own perfection, which they desire to enjoy, as **Averroes** says?

**PHILO.** I do not deny it; rather, I tell you that they desire their union with God to fulfill their perfection, so that their ultimate end and intent is indeed their own perfection. However, since that perfection consists in their union with the divinity, it follows that their ultimate end is in the divinity and not in themselves. Wherefore **Aristotle** says that this divinity is a higher end than their own, and it is not a part of their own proper perfection remaining within them, as **Averroes** believes.

**SOPHIA.** And would the **beatitude** <term>beatitude: supreme blessedness or spiritual happiness.</term> of human intellectual souls and their ultimate end also consist in this same reason of **divine union**?

**PHILO.** Certainly—because their ultimate perfection, their end, and their true beatitude do not consist in the souls themselves, but in their elevation and union with the divinity. Since the supreme God is the end of all things and the beatitude of all intellectual beings, this does not exclude their own perfection from being their ultimate end; for in the act of happiness, the intellectual soul is no longer in itself but in God, who is happiness itself through that union. It is there that its ultimate end and happiness exist, and not within itself, insofar as it might lack this blessed union.

**SOPHIA.** This subtlety pleases me, and I am satisfied regarding my first question; let us come to the second.

**PHILO.** You wish for me to explain to you in what way—by loving and moving the corporeal celestial **Orb**—that **Intelligence** <term>Intelligence: in this context, a non-material, angelic being that governs the movement of a planet or sphere.</term> can be magnified and elevated in the love of the supreme God and arrive at its happy union.

**SOPHIA.** That is exactly what I wish to know from you.

**PHILO.** The doubt becomes even greater because, for an **Intelligence** separated from matter, its own essential act is to understand itself; and as everything reflects together within it, the divine essence is within it in clear vision—like the Sun in a mirror—which contains the essence...

<summary>Philo argues that while celestial and human spirits seek their own perfection, that goal is only achieved through union with God, who is the ultimate final cause. He begins to explain how an immaterial Intelligence achieves this union through the act of moving a physical celestial body.</summary>

<keywords>Aristotle, Averroes, final cause, divine union, beatitude, Intelligence, celestial spheres, intellectual soul, perfection, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 116 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue where Philo explains the Aristotelian and Averroistic concepts of the "mover" and the "end" (goal) of celestial and human souls. He clarifies that the ultimate goal of any intellectual being is union with God, who acts as the final cause for all existence.</meta>

of all things, and is the cause of all, in this act must consist its happiness and its ultimate end, and not in the moving of a celestial body, which is a material thing and an act extrinsic to its true essence.

**SOPHIA.** I like to see you bloodying the wound <note>original: "infanguinarmi la piagha" — a metaphor for probing a difficult problem or "opening" a wound in order to properly treat it.</note>, so as to heal it better later; let us have, then, the remedy.

**PHILO.** You have heard from me at other times, O Sophia, that the whole universe is a single individual—that is, like one person—and every one of these spiritual and corporal, eternal and corruptible things is a member and a part of this great individual. And since the whole and each of its parts are produced by God for a common end in the whole, along with a proper end in each of the parts, it follows that both the whole and the parts are perfect and happy insofar as they rightly and entirely achieve the duties to which they are directed by the supreme Craftsman <term>Craftsman: 'Opifice' in Italian, referring to God as the creator who 'builds' or fashions the world.</term>. The end of the whole is the unified perfection of the entire universe, designed by the divine Architect; and the end of each of the parts is not only the perfection of that part in itself, but that through it, it may rightly serve the perfection of the whole. This is the universal end, the primary intent of Divinity, and it is for this common end more than for its own that every part was made, ordered, and dedicated. Thus, if a part were to fail in such service within the acts pertaining to the perfection of the universe, it would suffer a greater defect and become more unhappy than if it lacked its own proper act; and so it finds greater happiness in the common good than in its own. This is in the manner of a human individual, where the perfection of one of its parts—such as the eye and the hand—consists not only, nor principally, in being a beautiful eye or a beautiful hand, nor even in the eye seeing much, nor in the hand performing many arts; but first and principally it consists in the eye seeing and the hand doing what is fitting for the well-being of the whole person. It becomes more noble and more excellent through the right service it performs for the whole person than through its own beauty and proper act. Hence, many times, to save the whole person, the part naturally offers and exposes itself to its own danger, as the arm is accustomed to do, which presents itself to the sword for the salvation of the head. This law being always observed in the universe, the Intelligence <term>Intelligence: a spiritual being or 'angel' that Renaissance philosophers believed was responsible for moving the celestial spheres.</term> finds more happiness in moving the celestial Orb (which is an act necessary for the existence of the whole, even if it is an extrinsic and bodily act) than in its intrinsic essential understanding, which is its

<summary>Philo argues that the universe functions as a single organism where the happiness of each part, including celestial Intelligences, comes primarily from serving the perfection of the whole rather than their own individual essence.</summary>
<keywords>organicism, universal perfection, common good, Intelligence, celestial spheres, macrocosm, divine architect</keywords>

--- Page 117 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue where Philo explains that the ultimate purpose of an intellectual being is not merely its own function (like moving a celestial sphere) but the service of the whole universe and union with God. He moves from discussing celestial intelligences to the specific purpose of the human soul within the material body.</meta>

proper act. And this is what Aristotle means when he says that the Intelligence moves for a higher and more excellent end, which is God, achieving its own order in the universe; so that by loving and moving its Sphere <note>original: "Orbe" — referring to the celestial shells that carry the planets and stars in the Ptolemaic system.</note> it links the union of the universe, through which it properly attains the love, the union, and the divine grace that unifies the world—which is its ultimate end and desired happiness. 

**SOPHIA.** I am pleased, and I believe that for this same reason the spiritual, intellectual souls of men link themselves to a body as fragile as the human one: to achieve the divine order in the linking and union of the whole universe. 

**PHILO.** You have said well, and such is the truth: for our souls, being spiritual and intellectual, could encounter no good in corporeal, fragile, and corruptible society that would keep them from being much better off within their own intrinsic and pure intellectual act; yet they apply themselves to our body solely out of love and service to the supreme Creator of the universe, drawing life, intellectual knowledge, and divine light from the eternal superior world to the corruptible inferior one, so that this lowest part of the world should not be deprived of divine grace and eternal life. And therefore this great animal <note>A reference to the 'Anima Mundi' or World Soul; the Neoplatonic idea that the universe is a single, living, breathing organism.</note> has no part that is not alive and intelligent, just as he is in his entirety. And our soul, by practicing the union of the entire world-universe in this way, according to the divine order—which is the common and principal end in the production of things—rightly enjoys divine love and arrives at union with the supreme God after separation from the body; and this is its ultimate happiness. But by erring in such administration <note>referring to the soul's governance of the body and its actions on earth.</note>, it lacks this love and this divine union, and this is for it the highest and eternal punishment; because, while it could rise to the highest Paradise through the rectitude of its governance in the body, through its iniquity it remains in the lowest Hell, banished forever from divine union and from its own beatitude, unless divine piety were so great as to give it time and means to find a remedy. 

**SOPHIA.** May God keep us from such error and make us upright administrators of His holy will and His divine order. 

**PHILO.** May God do so; but yet you already know, O Sophia, that this cannot be done without love. 

**SOPHIA.** Truly, love in the world is not only common to everything, but also supremely necessary, since no one can be blessed without love. 

**PHILO.** Not only would beatitude be lacking

<summary>Philo explains that human souls inhabit fragile bodies to bring divine light to the material world, maintaining the unity of the universe as a "great animal." He concludes that the soul's ultimate happiness or eternal punishment depends on how well it governs the body through love and divine order.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, God, human soul, macrocosm, great animal, divine grace, Paradise, Hell, beatitude, Love, celestial spheres</keywords>

--- Page 118 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Philo and Sophia regarding the metaphysical necessity of love. The previous page established that human and celestial souls unite with material bodies not for their own benefit, but to serve the divine order of the universe, ensuring that even the lowest material realms participate in the divine life.</meta>

...if love were lacking, the world would have no being, nor would anything be found within it.

**SOPHIA.** Why such a great claim?

**PHILO.** Because the world and its components possess being only insofar as the whole is unified and linked with all its parts, in the manner of the limbs of an individual <note>A reference to the 'Microcosm' theory, where the universe is viewed as a giant human body and the human body as a small universe.</note>; otherwise, division would cause its total destruction. And since nothing else unites the universe with all its diverse parts except love, it follows that this love is the cause of the being of the world and of all its things.

**SOPHIA.** Tell me how love unifies the world and makes one single thing out of so many diverse things.

**PHILO.** From what has already been said, you can easily understand that the supreme God produces and governs the world with love, and binds it together in a single union. Because God is one in the simplest unity, it is necessary that what proceeds from Him also be one in entire unity; for from one, one proceeds, and from pure unity comes perfect union. Furthermore, the spiritual world is unified with the corporeal world through love. Nor would the separate Intelligences <note>original: "Intelligentie separate" — In Renaissance philosophy, these are non-material beings, often identified with Angels, who move the celestial spheres.</note> or divine Angels ever unite with the celestial bodies, nor would they give them form, nor would they act as souls granting them life, if they did not love them. Nor would intellectual souls unite with human bodies to make them rational if love did not compel them. Nor would the soul unite with this World Soul <note>original: "anima del mondo" — the Neoplatonic concept of a spiritual force that animates the entire physical universe.</note> and with this globe of generation and corruption <note>A traditional term for the Earthly realm below the moon, where things are born (generation) and die (corruption), unlike the "eternal" heavens.</note> if it were not for love. Furthermore, the lower things are united with the higher, the corporeal world with the spiritual, the corruptible with the eternal, and the entire universe with its Creator, through the love that it has and the desire it feels to unite with Him and find bliss in His divinity. In short, love is a unifying spirit that penetrates the entire world and is a bond that unites the universe.

**SOPHIA.** Since you feel this way about love, there is no need to speak any further of its commonality, which we have discussed all day. Now you have yet to tell me of the birth of love <note>original: "nascimento del' amore"</note>, as you promised me; for regarding its common presence throughout the universe and in each of its things, you have told me enough, and I clearly see that in the world, nothing can exist that does not have love. I only lack knowledge of its origin, and also something regarding its good and bad effects.

**PHILO.** I am in your debt regarding the birth of love, but its effects would be a new request; for neither the one nor the other is there sufficient time, as it is now too late to begin new subject matter. Ask me another day when it seems best to you. But tell me...

<summary>Philo explains that love is the essential "glue" of the universe, binding the spiritual and material realms together and maintaining the world's existence. Sophia accepts this argument and asks to move on to the origins (the "birth") of love.</summary>

<keywords>unity of the world, divine love, separate intelligences, world soul, generation and corruption, macrocosm, Neoplatonism, Leone Ebreo</keywords>

--- Page 119 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Philo and Sophia. In the preceding section, Philo argued that love is the binding force of the universe, connecting the spiritual and material realms. Now, the conversation shifts from cosmic metaphysics to the personal dynamics between the two speakers, as Philo laments Sophia's lack of reciprocal affection despite his philosophical arguments.</meta>

**PHILO.** O Sophia, how is it that love, being so universal, is not found in you?

**SOPHIA.** And you, Philo, do you love me very much?

**PHILO.** You see it, or rather you know it, since love is usually reciprocal between two people.

**SOPHIA.** According to what I have heard from you so many times, it must be either that you are feigning love for me, or that I am feigning it for you.

**PHILO.** I would be content if your words possessed as much falsehood as mine have truth; but I fear that you, like me, are not speaking the truth—that is, that love cannot for long be fled or denied.

**SOPHIA.** If you have true love, I cannot be without it.

**PHILO.** What you do not wish to say, so as not to speak a lie, you want me to believe through the power of arguments. I tell you that my love is true, but it is sterile, since in you it cannot produce its like; it is enough to bind me, but not to bind you.

**SOPHIA.** How so? Does love not have the nature of a Magnet <note>original: "Calamita" — A lodestone. In the Renaissance, the magnet was a favorite metaphor for the invisible, "sympathetic" forces that draw objects together.</note>, which unites diverse things, brings the distant close, and attracts the heavy?

**PHILO.** Yes, love is even more attractive than the Magnet; however, for those who do not wish to love, it is much heavier and more resistant than iron.

**SOPHIA.** You cannot deny that love unites lovers.

**PHILO.** Yes, when both are lovers; but if I am only a lover and not loved, and you are only loved and not a lover, how do you expect love to unite us?

**SOPHIA.** Who ever saw a true lover not be loved?

**PHILO.** I believe I am with you another Apollo with Daphne <note>A reference to the Greek myth where the god Apollo pursued the nymph Daphne, who fled from him.</note>.

**SOPHIA.** So you mean that Cupid <note>original: "Cupidine"</note> has wounded you with the golden arrow, and me with the leaden one? <note>In mythology, Cupid's gold-tipped arrows sparked love, while his lead-tipped arrows caused the heart to repel love.</note>

**PHILO.** I would not wish it, but I see it, because your love is more desired by me than gold, and mine is heavier to you than lead.

**SOPHIA.** If I were Daphne to you, out of fear of your words I would have been turned into a Laurel <note>original: "Lauro" — To escape Apollo, Daphne was transformed into a laurel tree.</note> sooner than she was for fear of Apollo's arrows.

**PHILO.** Words have little power to do what the rays of the eyes usually do with a single glance—that is, create mutual love and reciprocal affection. Yet, in resisting me, I see you transformed into a Laurel: so immobile in place and unchangeable in purpose, and so difficult to draw toward my desire, even though I draw ever closer to yours. And like the Laurel, you are always green and fragrant, in whose fruit no other flavor is found but bitterness and harshness, mixed with a biting coldness for whoever tastes it. Thus, to me, you have entirely become a Laurel; and if you wish to see the laureled sign of your conversion, look at my deaf...

<summary>Philo and Sophia discuss why their love is not reciprocal, using the metaphors of the magnet and the myth of Apollo and Daphne to describe Philo's pursuit and Sophia's resistance.</summary>
<keywords>reciprocity, Magnet, Apollo and Daphne, Cupid's arrows, Laurel, unrequited love, Renaissance Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 120 ---
<meta>This page concludes the final dialogue between Philo and Sophia. It follows a lengthy philosophical discussion where Philo has used the metaphor of the magnet and the harmony of the spheres to argue for the necessity of mutual love. Sophia, representing wisdom, remains elusive but hints at a deeper connection through reason before the two part ways for the night.</meta>

Lyre <note>original: "Cetara" — Philo likely refers to the lyre of Apollo, associated with harmony and the laurel tree.</note>, which would not sound if it were not adorned with your most beautiful leaves.

**SOPHIA.** Whether I love you, O Philo, it would not be virtuous to confess, nor any more so to deny; believe that which reason makes most fitting, even though you fear the contrary. And since the time now invites us to rest, it will be well for each of us to go and take it; we will return soon; meanwhile, attend to recreation, and remember your promise.

->TO GOD<-
